Automated Reasoning with Nested Datatypes
Tomer Hakak, Yoni Zohar, Andrew Reynolds, Clark Barrett, Cesare Tinelli

Abstract
We introduce a theory of nested datatypes. The theory is obtained by restricting the naive combination of datatypes and arrays, so as to prevent non-standard models from emerging. A decision procedure for the theory is given and proven correct. Finally, we describe an implementation of the procedure, as well as an evaluation over both real-world and crafted benchmarks.

Introduction

The SMT-LIB theory of datatypes allows for a direct and convenient encoding
of common data structures, such as lists, trees, and more,
which makes it useful for software verification.
And indeed, the 2025 release of the SMT-LIB benchmark library includes
over 50K benchmarks that involve datatypes
16
.

The expressivity of datatypes is, however, limited. For example,
there are no operators to update an existing datatype value,
and there are no random-access operators.
Such limitations call for the combination of datatypes with theories for other
data structures, like arrays.

A naive combination of datatypes and arrays introduces a problem:
arrays bypass the principle of acyclicity, according to which a datatype value cannot occur in itself.
Indeed,
a datatype value could now occur as an element
of an array, where the array is a field of the same value.
Such behavior is undesirable, as it does not match the intuitive combination of arrays and datatypes,
which only intends to increase expressivity, not break acyclicity.
We refer to such models that admit cycles as
non-standard models
.
A more detailed description of this issue, both intuitively and formally, is given in

Example

15
and
Figure

2
below.

In this paper we define a theory of
nested datatypes
, obtained
by restricting the aforementioned naive combination to
exclude non-standard models.
We introduce a decision procedure for the new theory, based on
a reduction to the (naive) combination of datatypes, arrays and uninterpreted functions.
We have implemented a prototype of our reduction-based algorithm in the state-of-the-art
SMT-solver
\mathbf{cvc5}

1
,
and evaluated it on benchmarks arising from the Move prover verification
tool
21
, and on synthetic benchmarks that
control the “hidden cycles” in datatypes and arrays.
Our experimental evaluation shows promise.
On the organic benchmarks from the Move Prover, it is comparable to and sometimes outperforms an existing solver for nested datatypes implemented in
\mathbf{Z3}

8
.
On the synthetic benchmarks, it outperforms
\mathbf{Z3}
.

In summary, our contributions are:

(i)
The introduction of a theory of nested datatypes;

(ii)
A reduction-based decision procedure for the new theory;

(iii)
An implementation in
\mathbf{cvc5}
and an evaluation using both real-world and crafted benchmarks.

Informal overview:

We intuitively explain the problem using
the first row of

Figure

2
.
This motivating example will be formally introduced and developed as a running
example throughout the paper.
Here
x
is a datatype value, while
a
is an array whose elements are datatype
values.
The first row visually describes two simple constraints:
x
is read
from array
a
at index
i
,
a
is a field of
x
.
Each constraint is harmless by itself.
Together, however, they describe a cycle: following the array field of
x

leads to
a
, and reading
a
at index
i
leads back to
x
.
The naive combination of the theories of datatypes and arrays
misses this because the cycle is split between the
theories.
The theory of Nested datatypes that we propose here reject the combined constraint.

Related work:

The introduced theory of nested datatypes generalizes the SMT-LIB theory of
algebraic datatypes
3
(see
4
;
15
for prominent decision procedures and
17
for a more recent one).
The SMT solver
\mathbf{Z3}

8
supports nested datatypes.
However, to the best of our knowledge, the literature lacks a precise definition of the supported theory and the implemented algorithm.

Outline:

Section

2
goes over the necessary notions from first-order logic and Satisfiability Modulo Theories.
In
Section

3
we introduce the theory of nested datatypes.
In
Section

4
we present a decision procedure for this theory, and then in
Section

5

we describe an implementation and evaluation of it.
The correctness proofs for the decision procedure are quite involved and long.
They are included in the appendix.

2
Preliminaries

For natural numbers
0\leq k\leq n
, we write
[k,n]
to denote
\{k,k+1,\ldots,n\}
.
For sets
X
and
Y
,

\text{AC}({X},{Y})

is the set of
almost constant functions
from
X
to
Y
, i.e.,
\{f:X\to Y\mid\exists c\in Y.\;\exists S\subseteq X.\;|S|<\infty\wedge\forall x\notin S,\;f(x)=c\}
.
Given set
X
and element
e
,

\textit{const}_{{X}}({e})
is the constant function whose domain is
X
and
always returns
e
. When
X
is clear from the context, we omit it, writing

\textit{const}({{e}})
.

Definition 1
(Well-founded relation)

A binary relation
R
on a set
X
is
well-founded

if there is no infinite
sequence
x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},\ldots
of elements of
X
such that
x_{n+1}\,R\,x_{n}
for every
n
.

2.1
Signatures and Structures

We briefly review the usual definitions of
many-sorted first-order logic with equality (see
9
;
19
).
For any set
S
, an
S
-sorted set

A
is a function from
S
to

{{\cal P}({X})}\setminus\left\{\emptyset\right\}
for some set
X

such that
A(s)\cap A(s^{\prime})=\emptyset

whenever
s\neq s^{\prime}
. We use
A_{s}
to denote
A(s)
.

A
many-sorted signature

\Sigma
consists of
a set
{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
of
sorts
,
a set
{\cal F}_{\Sigma}
of
function symbols
,
and a set
{\cal P}_{\Sigma}
of
predicate symbols
.
Function symbols have arities
\sigma_{1}\times\ldots\times\sigma_{n}\rightarrow\sigma
,
and predicate symbols have arities
\sigma_{1}\times\ldots\times\sigma_{n}
,
with
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n},\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
.
For each sort
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
,

{\cal P}_{\Sigma}
includes an
equality symbol

=_{\sigma}
of arity

\sigma\times\sigma
, denoted

=
when
\sigma
is clear.

\Sigma
is called
finite
if
{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
,
{\cal F}_{\Sigma}
,
and
{\cal P}_{\Sigma}
are finite.
We assume a
{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
-sorted set
of
variables
.
Well-sorted terms, formulas, and literals are defined as usual.
For a
\Sigma
-formula
{{\phi}}
and a sort
\sigma
, we denote the set of
free variables in
{{\phi}}
of sort
\sigma
by
{\it vars}_{\sigma}({{{\phi}}})
.
This notation naturally extends to
{\it vars}_{S}({{{\phi}}})
when
S
is a set of
sorts. When
S={\cal S}_{\Sigma}
we may omit it.
Let
B\subseteq{\cal F}_{\Sigma}
be a set of function symbols.
We denote by
\Sigma_{\mid B}
the signature with the same sorts as
\Sigma
, no predicate symbols (except
=_{\sigma}
for
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
), and whose set of function symbols is
B
.

A
\Sigma
-literal is called
flat
if it has one of the following forms:

x=f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
,

P(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
, or
\neg P(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

for some variables
x,y,x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
and function and predicate symbols

f
and
P
from
\Sigma
.
A (flat)
cube
is a conjunction of (flat) literals.

A
\Sigma
-structure
consists of a
{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
-sorted set
A
, and interpretations
to the function and predicate symbols of
\Sigma
.
We further require that
=_{\sigma}
is interpreted
as the identity relation over
A_{\sigma}
for every
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
.
A
\Sigma
-interpretation

{\cal A}
is an extension of
a
\Sigma
-structure with interpretations to some set of variables.
For any
\Sigma
-term
\alpha
,
\alpha^{{\cal A}}

denotes the interpretation of
\alpha
in
{\cal A}
.
When
X
is a set of
\Sigma
-terms,
X^{{\cal A}}=\left\{x^{{\cal A}}\mid x\in X\right\}
.
Similarly,
f^{{\cal A}}
and
P^{{\cal A}}
denote the interpretation
of
f
and
P
in
{\cal A}
, and we denote
A_{\sigma}
by
\sigma^{{\cal A}}
for every

\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
.
Given a signature
\Sigma^{\prime}
which is contained in
\Sigma
, we denote by
{\cal A}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}
the restriction of
{\cal A}
to
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Satisfaction is denoted by
\models
.
A
\Sigma
-theory

T
is a class of
\Sigma
-structures.
A
\Sigma
-interpretation
whose variable-free part is in
T
is
a
T
-interpretation
.

{{\phi}}
is
T
-satisfiable
(denoted
\models_{T}{{\phi}}
)
if
{\cal A}\models{{\phi}}
for some
T
-interpretation
{\cal A}
.

{{\phi}}
and
{{\varphi}}
are
T
-equivalent
if they are satisfied by the same class of
T
-interpretations.

The
\Sigma
-theory of equality and uninterpreted functions (EUF) is
the class of all
\Sigma
-structures.
A signature
\Sigma
is called an
arrays signature

if

{\cal S}_{\Sigma}=\{A,I,E\}
for distinct sorts
A
,
I
, and
E
,

{\cal F}_{\Sigma}=\{\textbf{select}_{A},\textbf{store}_{A}\}
,
and
{\cal P}_{\Sigma}
is empty.
The arity of
\textbf{select}_{A}
is

A\rightarrow E
, and the arity of

\textbf{store}_{A}
is
(A\times I\times E)\rightarrow A
.
When
A
is clear from context, we may omit it, writing
select
and
store
.
For an array signature
\Sigma
, we say that a
\Sigma
-structure
{\cal A}
is an
arrays
\Sigma
-structure
if it satisfies
the axioms for arrays as specified in SMT-LIB.
The
\Sigma
-theory of arrays is the class of all
arrays
\Sigma
-structures (see, e.g.
7
).

2.2
The SMT-LIB 2 Theory of Datatypes

We adapt the definition
from
18

of
the SMT-LIB theory of datatypes
3
.

2.2.1
Syntax

Definition 2
(Well-founded sort (DT))

Let
\Sigma
be a signature and

S\subseteq{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
.
Define a sequence of functions
G_{0},G_{1},\ldots
as follows:

G_{0}(\Sigma,S)=S
;

G_{i+1}(\Sigma,S)=G_{i}(\Sigma,S)\cup\{\sigma\mid\exists c\in{\cal F}_{\Sigma}.c:\sigma_{1}\times\ldots\times\sigma_{n}\rightarrow\sigma,\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n}\in G_{i}(\Sigma,S)\}
.
Then, a sort
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
is well-founded w.r.t.
S

in
\Sigma

if
\sigma\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}G_{i}(\Sigma,S)
.
We sometimes drop
S
, and just say “well-founded” when it is clear.

Definition 3
(Datatypes signature)

A finite signature
\Sigma
is called a datatypes signature
if

{\cal S}_{\Sigma}={\bf Elem}_{\Sigma}\uplus{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma}
and

{\cal F}_{\Sigma}={{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma}\uplus{{\cal S}}{{\cal E}}_{\Sigma}
,
for some
{\bf Elem}_{\Sigma},{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma},{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma},{{\cal S}}{{\cal E}}_{\Sigma}
,
such that

{{\cal S}}{{\cal E}}_{\Sigma}=\{s_{(c,i)}:\sigma\rightarrow\sigma_{i}\mid c\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma},c:\sigma_{1}\times\ldots\times\sigma_{n}\rightarrow\sigma,1\leq i\leq n\}
and

{{\cal P}}_{\Sigma}=\{\text{is}_{c}:\sigma\mid c\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma},c:\sigma_{1}\times\ldots\times\sigma_{n}\rightarrow\sigma\}
.
The elements of
{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma}
are called constructors,
those of
{{\cal S}}{{\cal E}}_{\Sigma}
selectors,
and of
{{\cal P}}_{\Sigma}
testers.
We further require that for every constructor
c
with arity
\sigma_{1}\times\ldots\times\sigma_{n}\rightarrow\sigma
,
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma}
and all sorts in
{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma}
are well-founded w.r.t.
{\bf Elem}_{\Sigma}
in
{\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma}}
.

Selectors are often renamed, e.g.,
linked lists are often modeled using
constructors
{\mathbf{nil}}
and
{\mathbf{cons}}
, where the two selectors for
{\mathbf{cons}}

are named
{\mathbf{hd}}
and
{\mathbf{tl}}
, rather than

s_{({\mathbf{cons}},0)}
and

s_{({\mathbf{cons}},1)}
.

We will use the signatures in
Table

1
as running examples.
The signature
\Sigma_{0}
is a pure datatypes signature with element sort
I

and two struct sorts,
A
and
E
.
In
\Sigma_{0}
, the sort
A
is a list sort for
E
-elements, built using

{\mathbf{nil}}
and
{\mathbf{cons}}
.
The sort
E
is built using the constructor
{\mathbf{c}}
, whose selectors are
{\mathbf{id}}
and
{\mathbf{chld}}
; intuitively,
{\mathbf{id}}
stores an index of
sort
I
, and
{\mathbf{chld}}
stores the list of children of sort
A
.
The signature
\Sigma_{1}
uses the same sort names for arrays from
I
to
E
,
with array sort
A
.
Thus,
A
is not meant to be both a datatype list sort and an array sort
inside one component theory: it is a list sort in
\Sigma_{0}
and an array sort in

\Sigma_{1}
.
The signature
\Sigma_{2}^{-}
keeps only the datatype constructor
{\mathbf{c}}
,
and treats sort
A
as an element sort for datatypes.

\Sigma_{2}
extends it with the constant constructor
{\mathbf{emp}}:E
, used as an empty

E
-value.
Finally,
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}^{-}
and
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}
are obtained by combining the array
signature
\Sigma_{1}
with
\Sigma_{2}^{-}
and
\Sigma_{2}
, respectively.
The formulas in the last column will be used to illustrate how cycles arise
across the datatype and array components.

Example 4

Consider the datatypes signature
\Sigma_{0}
from
Table

1
.
Sorts
A
and
E
are well-founded w.r.t.
{\bf Elem}_{\Sigma_{0}}
in
{\Sigma_{0}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{0}}}
:

G_{0}({\Sigma_{0}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{0}}},{\bf Elem}_{\Sigma_{0}})=\{I\}
,

G_{1}({\Sigma_{0}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{0}}},{\bf Elem}_{\Sigma_{0}})=\{I,A\}
, and

G_{2}({\Sigma_{0}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{0}}},{\bf Elem}_{\Sigma_{0}})=\{I,A,E\}={\cal S}_{\Sigma_{0}}
.
But, if we remove constructor
{\mathbf{nil}}
,
then both sorts are no longer well-founded.

2.2.2
Semantics

Definition 5
(trees)

Given a signature
\Sigma
,
a set

S\subseteq{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
and
an
S
-sorted set
A
,
the set of
\Sigma
-trees over
A
of sort
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
is denoted by

T_{\sigma}(\Sigma,A)
and is inductively defined as follows:

T_{\sigma,0}(\Sigma,A)=A_{\sigma}
if
\sigma\in S
and

\emptyset
otherwise; and

T_{\sigma,i+1}(\Sigma,A)=T_{\sigma,i}(\Sigma,A)\cup\{c(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\mid c\in{\cal F}_{\Sigma},c:\sigma_{1}\times\ldots\times\sigma_{n}\rightarrow\sigma\in{\cal F}_{\Sigma},t_{j}\in T_{\sigma_{j},i}(\Sigma,A),1\leq j\leq n\}
for each

i\geq 0
.
Finally,
T_{\sigma}(\Sigma,A)=\bigcup_{i\geq 0}T_{\sigma,i}(\Sigma,A)
.

T_{\sigma}(\Sigma,A)
contains the ground
\sigma
-sorted terms over
\Sigma
with
A
as constants.

Definition 6
(Datatypes structure)

Let
\Sigma
be a datatypes signature and
D
an
{\bf Elem}
-sorted set.
A
\Sigma
-structure
{\cal A}
is a datatypes
\Sigma
-structure generated by
D
if,
for every
c\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma}
of arity
(\sigma_{1}\times\ldots\times\sigma_{n})\rightarrow\sigma

and every
t_{j}\in\sigma_{j}^{{\cal A}}
:

(i)

\sigma^{{\cal A}}=T_{\sigma}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma}},D)
for every
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
;

(ii)

c^{{\cal A}}(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})=c(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
;

(iii)

s_{(c,i)}^{{\cal A}}(c(t_{1},\dots,t_{n}))=t_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
; and

(iv)

\text{is}_{c}^{{\cal A}}=\left\{c(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\mid t_{1}\in\sigma_{1}^{{\cal A}},\dots,t_{n}\in\sigma_{n}^{{\cal A}}\right\}
.
The
\Sigma
-theory of datatypes
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma}}^{{\mathit{D}T}}
is the class of datatypes
\Sigma
-structures.

Selectors
s_{(c,i)}
on terms with top constructor different than
c
are interpreted arbitrarily, as in SMT-LIB 2.

) of axioms

(see, e.g.,

); axiom

Example 8

Consider again
\Sigma_{0}
from
Table

1
,
and let
{\cal A}
be a
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma_{0}}}^{{\mathit{D}T}}
-interpretation
with
I
interpreted as the integers.
Then, e.g.,
{\mathbf{c}}(0,{\mathbf{nil}})
and

{\mathbf{c}}(0,{\mathbf{cons}}({\mathbf{c}}(0,{\mathbf{nil}}),{\mathbf{nil}}))
are elements of
E^{{\cal A}}
.
The
\Sigma_{0}
-formula
{\varphi_{0}}
from
Table

1
is
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma_{0}}}^{{\mathit{D}T}}
-unsatisfiable,
as it violates
{{\mathit{(}{cyc})}}
:
Figure

2
’s first row shows why –
x
cannot contain itself.

2.3
Decidability and Combination

A
\Sigma
-theory is
decidable
if the set of
{\cal T}
-satisfiable flat
\Sigma
-cubes is decidable;
arrays, EUF, and datatypes are decidable (see, e.g.,
7
).
Given signatures
\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}
and theories
T_{1},T_{2}
over them,

\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2}
is the union of their sort, function, and predicate symbols,
and the
combination

T_{1}\oplus T_{2}
is the class of
\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2}
-structures
{\cal A}

with
{\cal A}^{\Sigma_{1}}\in T_{1}
and
{\cal A}^{\Sigma_{2}}\in T_{2}
.

T
is
stably infinite w.r.t.
S\subseteq{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
if every
T
-satisfiable
quantifier-free
\Sigma
-formula
{{\phi}}
has a
T
-interpretation
{\cal A}\models{{\phi}}

with
\lvert\sigma^{{\cal A}}\rvert
infinite for all
\sigma\in S
.
EUF and arrays are stably infinite w.r.t. all their sorts (see, e.g.,
7
);
for a datatypes signature
\Sigma
,
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma}}^{{\mathit{D}T}}
is stably infinite w.r.t.
{\bf Elem}_{\Sigma}
(see, e.g.,
4
).
The Nelson-Oppen (NO) method
14
;
19
decides satisfiability
in the combination of stably infinite theories, by reasoning about
arrangements
(conjunctions of (dis)equalities) between shared variables.

3
The Theory of Nested datatypes

In this section we define the theory of nested
datatypes.
There are two challenges in doing so, one syntactic, and the other semantic.
The first invites a new definition for a well-foundedness of a sort, that must take into account the existence of arrays.
The second requires to exclude unwanted structures from the defined theory.

3.1
First challenge: well-foundedness

Going back to
Table

1
, notice that

{\varphi_{0}}
uses
the theory
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma_{0}}}^{{\mathit{D}T}}
of datatypes to represent a nested structure.
This is
natural, but sometimes not expressive enough.
For example,
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma_{0}}}^{{\mathit{D}T}}
does not have an operator
to update such lists, or a random-access reading operator.
Thus, it might be desirable to have the second field of datatype
E

be an
array
of datatypes, rather than a list,
as the next example does.

Example 9

Consider signature
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}^{-}
, obtained as the union of

\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}^{-}
,
from
Table

1
.

\Sigma_{1}
is an arrays signature, while

\Sigma_{2}^{-}
is a datatypes signature.

Noticeably,
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}^{-}
no longer has the
constructor
{\mathbf{nil}}
, that was available in
\Sigma_{0}
.
There, it was used as an empty list constructor.
However, we have now replaced lists with arrays, and there are no
empty arrays.
But now we have a problem.
The well-foundedness condition from
Definition

2
that was ensured
by
{\mathbf{nil}}
in
Example

4
is now lacking:
in terms of
Definition

2
,
G_{1}
will no longer include the sort

A
, and so
G_{2}
will not include the sort
E
.
Thus, replacing lists by arrays requires an appropriate
definition of well-foundedness,
that explicitly incorporates arrays sorts.

Definition 10
(Well-founded sort (NDT))

Let

\Sigma_{1},\dots,\Sigma_{n}
be arrays signatures, each with
{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{k}}=\{A_{k},I_{k},E_{k}\}

for each
1\leq k\leq n
, and let
\Sigma_{n+1}

be a datatypes signature.
Let

\Sigma\;=\;\bigcup_{k=1}^{n+1}\Sigma_{k}
.
For a set
S\subseteq{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
, set:

F_{0}(\Sigma,S)=S
;

F_{i+1}(\Sigma,S)=F_{i}(\Sigma,S)\cup\{\sigma\mid\exists c\in{\cal F}_{\Sigma}.c:\sigma_{1}\times\ldots\times\sigma_{n}\rightarrow\sigma,\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n}\in F_{i}(\Sigma,S)\}\cup\{A_{k}\mid E_{k},I_{k}\in F_{i}(\Sigma,S)\}
.
A sort
\sigma

is
well-founded in
\Sigma

w.r.t.
S

if
\sigma\;\in\;\bigcup_{i\geq 0}\!F_{i}\Bigl(\Sigma,\;S\Bigr)
.

Notice that while in
Definition

2
well-foundedness is defined for datatypes signatures,
in
Definition

10
it is defined for signatures obtained by a union
of a datatypes signature with a number of array signatures.

Example 11

Using
Definition

10
, we see that sorts
A
and
E
are not well-founded in signature
{\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}^{-}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{2}^{-}}}
w.r.t.
\{I\}
:
indeed,

F_{0}({\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}^{-}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{2}^{-}}},\{I\})=\{I\}
for every
i
.
However, denote by
\Sigma_{2}
the signature obtained
from
\Sigma_{2}^{-}
by adding a nullary constructor
{\mathbf{emp}}
of sort
E
, and let
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}=\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2}
, as described in
Table

1
.
We now get that both
E
and
A
are well-founded
in
{\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{2}}}
w.r.t.
\{I\}
.

We shall also require all array sorts to be distinct from one another
and from the datatype sorts.
We further assume that the nesting of arrays and datatypes only
involves the element sort of arrays, not the index sort;
and that there is no nesting between arrays.
This is formalized as follows:

Notation 1

Let
\Sigma_{1},\dots,\Sigma_{n}
be array signatures,
s.t.
{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{j}}=\{A_{j},I_{j},E_{j}\}
for all
1\leq j\leq n
.
Let

\Sigma_{n+1}

be a signature s.t.

\Sigma_{1},\ldots,\Sigma_{n+1}
are pairwise disjoint.
For
\Sigma:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n+1}\Sigma_{i}
,

{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}:=\{A_{j}\mid 1\leq j\leq n\}
.

Definition 12
(NDT signature)

Let
\Sigma_{1},\ldots,\Sigma_{n}
be arrays signatures,
each with

{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{i}}=\{\,E_{i},\,I_{i},\,A_{i}\}
,
and
let
\Sigma_{n+1}
be a datatypes signature
and denote

{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}={\bf Elem}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\uplus{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
.
Let
\Sigma=\bigcup_{k=1}^{n+1}\Sigma_{k}
.
Suppose the sorts in
{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}\,\cup\,{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
are well-founded w.r.t.

{\cal S}_{\Sigma}\,\setminus\,({\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}\,\cup\,{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}})
,

|{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}|=n
,

\{I_{1},\ldots,I_{n}\}\;\cap\;({\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma})\;=\;\emptyset
,
and

{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\,\cap\,{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}\;=\;\emptyset
.
Then
\Sigma
is called the
NDT signature
of

\Sigma_{1},\ldots,\Sigma_{n+1}
.

Example 13

Both
A
and
E
in
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}
from
Table

1
are well founded w.r.t.
\{I\}
,

|{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}}|=1
,
\{I\}\cap({\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{2}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}})=\emptyset
, and

{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{2}}\,\cap\,{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}}=\emptyset
.
Therefore,
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}
can be called the NDT signature of

\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}
.

3.2
Second challenge: non-standard interpretations

Let us start with the most natural candidate as semantics for nested datatypes,
obtained by simply taking the theory combination of datatypes and arrays.

Definition 14
(Pre-NDT theory)

Let
T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}
be array theories with signatures

\Sigma_{1},\ldots,\Sigma_{n}
.
Let
T_{n+1}
be a datatypes theory with signature
\Sigma_{n+1}
.
Assume that
\Sigma
is the NDT signature of
\Sigma_{1},\ldots,\Sigma_{n+1}
.
Then
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma}}^{{\mathit{P}NDT}}:=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n+1}T_{k}

is the
\Sigma
-theory of pre-NDTs
.

As the next example shows, this theory exhibits unexpected behavior.

Example 15

Let

T_{1}
be the array theory over
\Sigma_{1}
and
T_{2}
the
\Sigma_{2}
-theory of datatypes,
where
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
are as in
Table

1
.
Then
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}}}^{{\mathit{P}NDT}}=T_{1}\oplus T_{2}
is the
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}
-theory of pre-NDTs.
Consider the formula

{{\varphi}}

from
Table

1
,
obtained from
{\varphi_{0}}
by replacing the datatype selector
with an array select.

{{\varphi}}

is

{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}}}^{{\mathit{P}NDT}}
-satisfiable, even though it can be viewed as a variant
of the
\Sigma_{0}
-formula
{\varphi_{0}}
,
which is unsatisfiable in the theory of
\Sigma_{0}
-datatypes.
This is unsatisfactory.
The intended meaning of
{{\varphi}}
is depicted in the last column
of
Figure

2
– the same meaning as
{\varphi_{0}}
, just
using arrays.
Its
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}}}^{{\mathit{P}NDT}}
-satisfiability can be shown using the
Nelson-Oppen method:
it suffices to show that

\psi_{1}^{\prime}
is
T_{1}
-satisfiable
and
\psi_{2}
is
T_{2}
-satisfiable,
as for each sort they share at most one variable,
and so only trivial arrangements exist.

We have seen that
{{\varphi}}
from
Table

1

is satisfiable in the theory
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma}}^{{\mathit{P}NDT}}
, even though
intuitively, it should not be.
The next definition identifies the problem with that
interpretation, and allows us to restrict our attention
to standard interpretations.
This is done by defining a relation in
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma}}^{{\mathit{P}NDT}}
-structures,
and require its well-foundedness.
The relation connects input and output sorts of both arrays and datatypes.
Its formal definition is quite involved, but intuitively it simply
relates terms to their sub-terms and arrays to their elements.
With this relation, we can finally define the theory of nested datatypes that we will adopt.

Definition 16
(NDT relation and theory)

Let
T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}
be the array theories of signatures

\Sigma_{1},\ldots,\Sigma_{n}
and

T_{n+1}
the theory of
\Sigma_{n+1}
-datatypes.
Let
\Sigma
be the NDT signature of
\Sigma_{1},\ldots,\Sigma_{n+1}
.
Further assume that
{\cal F}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}={{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\uplus{{\cal S}}{{\cal E}}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
,
and
{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{i}}=\{A_{i},I_{i},E_{i}\}

for each
1\leq i\leq n
.
Let
{\cal A}
be a
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma}}^{{\mathit{P}NDT}}
-structure.
The NDT-relation of
{\cal A}
(denoted
{\cal R}_{{\cal A}}
) is
defined as follows:

(\alpha,\beta)\in{\cal R}_{{\cal A}}
if and only if one of the following
conditions holds:

(i)

\alpha=t_{j}
and
\beta=c(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
for some

c\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
with arity

(\sigma_{1}\times\ldots\times\sigma_{m})\rightarrow\sigma

such that

t_{1}\in\sigma_{1}^{\cal A},\ldots,t_{m}\in\sigma_{m}^{{\cal A}}

and
1\leq j\leq m
;

(ii)

\alpha=\textbf{select}_{A_{i}}^{{\cal A}}(a,j)
and
\beta=a

for some

1\leq i\leq n
,
a\in{A_{i}}^{{\cal A}}

and
j\in I_{i}^{{\cal A}}
.
We say
that
{\cal A}
is an
NDT
\Sigma
-structure
if

{\cal R}_{{\cal A}}
is well-founded.
1
1

1

The term
well-founded
has been overloaded.

Table

2
summarizes its various usages.

The theory of
\Sigma
-nested datatypes, denoted
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}

is the class of all NDT
\Sigma
-structures.

fixes the problem raised in

Example 17

The formula
{{\varphi}}
from

is
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-unsatisfiable, as mentioned in

Figure

2
.

4
Decision Procedure for Nested Datatypes

In this section we introduce a decision procedure for the theory
of nested datatypes.
In what follows,
let
\Sigma
be the NDT signature of
\Sigma_{1},\ldots,\Sigma_{n+1}
,
where
\Sigma_{1},\dots,\Sigma_{n}
are arrays signatures and
\Sigma_{n+1}
is a datatypes signature.
Also, let
{{\phi}}
be a
\Sigma
-formula.
Our procedure will determine the

{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-satisfiability of
{{\phi}}
.

The procedure works as follows:
we define a new signature
\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}
and a

\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}
-formula
\bf{trFormula}({{\phi}})
such that

{{\phi}}
is
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-satisfiable
if and only if

\bf{trFormula}({{\phi}})
is satisfiable in the

\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}
-theory obtained by combining
the theories of datatypes, arrays, and uninterpreted functions
(but without the restriction to NDT structures).
The latter can be decided using existing SMT-solvers
for the resulting combination, in a Nelson-Oppen fashion.

4.1
Translation of Signatures and Theories

We begin by defining new sorts.
The idea is to have the theories of arrays and datatypes separated,
so that datatypes will not have array sorts
as element sorts.
The connection between the datatypes and the arrays from the original signature will be maintained by uninterpreted functions.

For every sort
\sigma
in
{\cal S}_{\Sigma}

we define a new sort
{\sigma^{*}}
.
For each array sort
A
in
{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}
,
we introduce two additional sorts:

A^{\prime}
and
{{A}^{\circ}}
.
In total, each sort
A
in
{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}

gives rise to three new sorts:

{A^{*}}
,
A^{\prime}
and
{{A}^{\circ}}
;
and each other sort
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}\setminus{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}

gives rise to a single new sort
{\sigma^{*}}
.
The new sorts are summarized in
Table

4
.
Intuitively, for each non-array sort
\sigma
,
we create a copy of it named
{\sigma^{*}}
.
But if
\sigma
is an array sort,

{\sigma^{*}}
is no longer a copy of it.
Instead, it is a datatypes sort that is meant to represent
the original array sort.
Additionally,
\sigma^{\prime}
is an array sort, and its
element sort may represent a datatype, but not a nested one.
Thus, every array sort
\sigma
is represented by two sorts:

{\sigma^{*}}
and
\sigma^{\prime}
.
The sort
{\sigma^{*}}
will be used only for checking cycles,
whereas
\sigma^{\prime}
is used for the regular array reasoning.
Finally,
{{\sigma}^{\circ}}
is an additional sort that serves
as a technical separator
between
datatypes with sort
{\sigma^{*}}
.
Then, the new signature
\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}
is defined as follows.

Sort

Set

Sorts

\sigma

{\cal S}_{\Sigma}\setminus{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}

{\sigma^{*}}

A_{i}

{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}

{A_{i}^{*}},A_{i}^{\prime},{{A_{i}}^{\circ}}

Table 3:
New sorts.

{\cal S}_{{\Sigma_{i}^{*}}}

\{{I_{i}^{*}},{E_{i}^{*}},A_{i}^{\prime}\}

{\cal F}_{{\Sigma_{i}^{*}}}

\{\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}:(A^{\prime}_{i}\times{I_{i}^{*}})\rightarrow{E_{i}^{*}},\textbf{store}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}:(A^{\prime}_{i}\times{I_{i}^{*}}\times{E_{i}^{*}})\rightarrow A^{\prime}_{i}\}

{\cal P}_{{\Sigma_{i}^{*}}}

\emptyset

Signatures
{\Sigma_{i}^{*}}
.
Arities are inline.

Definition 18

\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}=\Bigl(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}{\Sigma_{i}^{*}}\Bigr)\cup{\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}}\cup\Sigma_{n+2}
,
where for each

1\leq i\leq n
,

{\Sigma_{i}^{*}}
is given in
Table

4
,

\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}_{n+1}
is given in
Table

6
,
and
\Sigma_{n+2}
is given in
Table

6
.

Notice that the
n+2
signatures

{\Sigma_{1}^{*}},\ldots,{\Sigma_{n}^{*}},\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}_{n+1},\Sigma_{n+2}

may share sorts, but are otherwise disjoint.
Let us elaborate on
\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}
by focusing on each
of the constructed signatures separately.
We start with signatures

{\Sigma_{1}^{*}},\ldots,{\Sigma_{n}^{*}}
,
given in
Table

4
.
Let
1\leq i\leq n
.

{\Sigma_{i}^{*}}
is an arrays signature,
whose index sort is

{I_{i}^{*}}
,
its element sort is

{E_{i}^{*}}
, and its array sort is

A_{i}^{\prime}
.
As an arrays signature, it has two function symbols

\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}},\textbf{store}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}
,
with the appropriate arities.

Next, we describe
{\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}}
.
It relies on the number of terms used
under selects and stores in
{{\phi}}
.

Definition 19

For a sort
A_{i}\in{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}
,
and a flat
\Sigma
-cube
{{\phi}}
,
let
{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}

be the set of variables
x

in
{\it vars}_{I_{i}}({{{\phi}}})
such that

\textbf{select}_{A_{i}}(a,x)
or

\textbf{store}_{A_{i}}(a,x,v)
occur in
{{\phi}}

for some
a
,
x
and
v
.

n_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}
is its size.

Example 20

Referring to
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}
-formula
{{\varphi}}
from
Table

1
,
we have
{\mathbb{I}}_{(A,{{\varphi}})}=\{i\}
and
n_{(A,{{\varphi}})}=1
.

\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}_{n+1}
is defined in
Table

6
.
The idea is to mimic

\Sigma_{n+1}
, while avoiding arrays completely.
Let us start with its sorts.
For every
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
,
we have
{\sigma^{*}}\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}_{n+1}}
, and for every sort
\sigma\in{\bf Elem}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cup\{E_{i}\mid i\in[1,n]\}
that is not in

{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}
or
{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
,
we have
{\sigma^{*}}\in{\bf Elem}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}_{n+1}}
.
For every
A_{i}
, where
i\in[1,n]
,
we have
{A_{i}^{*}}\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}_{n+1}}
.

For each constructor
c:(\rho_{1}\times\ldots\times\rho_{m})\rightarrow\sigma
in
{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
,
we create a corresponding constructor

{c^{*}}:({\rho_{1}^{*}}\times\ldots\times{\rho_{m}^{*}})\rightarrow{\sigma^{*}}

in
{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}_{n+1}}
.
Intuitively,
{c^{*}}
is a copy of
c
that operates on the transformed sorts.
For every
1\leq i\leq n
, we create a single constructor
c_{i}
of arity

({{A_{i}}^{\circ}}\times{{E_{i}^{*}}\times\ldots\times{E_{i}^{*}}})\rightarrow{A_{i}^{*}}
.
Intuitively, this constructor represents an array using a finite tuple:
the first argument (of sort
{{A_{i}}^{\circ}}
) is a unique identifier for the array,
and the remaining
n_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}
arguments
store the elements that can be accessed using the indices from
{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}
.
The reason for the first argument is to ensure that if two arrays
are different, due to indices not in
{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}
,
their translations will remain different.
We can always assume w.l.o.g that
n_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}\geq 1
;
if it is zero, we add a literal
\textbf{select}_{A_{i}}(a,j)=v
to
{{\phi}}
with fresh variables
a
,
j
, and
v
.
Once the sorts and constructors of
\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}_{n+1}
are defined,
the selectors and testers are induced as usual for datatypes.
Notice,

\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}_{n+1}
itself is a datatypes signature:

Lemma 21

{\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}}
is a datatypes signature.

The last signature that is left to discuss is
\Sigma_{n+2}
,
presented in
Table

6
.
Its purpose is to relate
the new sorts
A^{\prime}
and
{A^{*}}
to one another.
For each
A\in{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}
,
we have both
A^{\prime}
and
{A^{*}}

as sorts in
\Sigma_{n+2}
,
as well as two function symbols,

f_{A}
and
g_{A}
,
with arities

{A^{*}}\rightarrow A^{\prime}

and

A^{\prime}\rightarrow{A^{*}}

\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}

We now define our target theory,
as a combination of arrays, datatypes and EUF.

Definition 23

For
1\leq i\leq n
, let

be the
{\Sigma_{i}^{*}}
-theory
of arrays,

Remark 25

It might be more natural to represent each array variable
a

with its own constructor
c_{a}
, thus omitting the first
argument (and the sort
{{A}^{\circ}}
altogether).
However, we want to make sure that if our input formula entails
an equality
a=b
between two array variables
a
and
b
,
then the datatype terms that represent them will also be equal.
But if these datatypes are constructed using distinct constructors,
they will surely be distinct.
In contrast, having an identifier as an argument to the constructor,
allows us to keep the resulting datatypes equal.

4.3
Lemmas

To fix the issue raised by
Example

27
, we augment
\bf{tr}
with lemmas, that are
given in
Table

10
.

Recall that each constructor
c_{j}
has an arity based on the set

{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{j},{{\phi}})}
.
In order to properly apply the constructors
c_{j}
s, we fix
an ordering for the set
{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{j},{{\phi}})}
by
fixing a bijection:

Definition 28

Given
1\leq j\leq n
and
{{\phi}}
,

o_{(A_{j},{{\phi}})}

denotes a fixed bijection
from

{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{j},{{\phi}})}
to
\{1,\ldots,n_{(A_{j},{{\phi}})}\}
.
When
A_{j}
and
{{\phi}}
are clear from the context,
we omit them and simply write
o
.

Notation

Definition

\mathbf{L_{0}^{{{\phi}}}}({a,i})

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{l}\Bigl\{u_{(a,o_{(A_{j},{{\phi}})}(i))}=\textbf{select}_{A_{j}^{\prime}}(a^{\prime},{i^{*}})~~~,~~~u_{(a,o_{(A_{j},{{\phi}})}(i))}=s_{(c_{j},o_{(A_{j},{{\phi}})}(i)+1)}({a^{*}})\Bigr\}\end{array}

\mathbf{L_{1}}({{{\phi}}})

\displaystyle\bigcup_{x=\textbf{select}_{A}(a,i)\in{{\phi}}}\mathbf{L_{0}^{{{\phi}}}}({a,i})

\mathbf{L_{2}}({{{\phi}}})

\displaystyle\bigcup_{b=\textbf{store}_{A}(a,j,v)\in{{\phi}}}~~\bigcup_{i\in{\mathbb{I}}_{(A,{{\phi}})}}\Bigl(\mathbf{L_{0}^{{{\phi}}}}({a,i})\cup\mathbf{L_{0}^{{{\phi}}}}({b,i})\Bigr)

\mathbf{L_{3}}({{{\phi}}})

\displaystyle\bigcup_{A\in{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}}~~\bigcup_{a\in{\it vars}_{A}({{{\phi}}})}\Bigl\{a^{\prime}=f_{A}({a^{*}}),\quad{a^{*}}=g_{A}(a^{\prime})\Bigr\}

Table 10:
Lemmas
{\bf L^{{{\phi}}}_{0}}({a})
,
\mathbf{L_{1}}({{{\phi}}})
,
\mathbf{L_{2}}({{{\phi}}})
, and
\mathbf{L_{3}}({{{\phi}}})
.
The sort of
a
is
A_{j}
.

\mathbf{L_{0}^{{{\phi}}}}({a,i})
ensures that the value
obtained by a select on the array variable
a^{\prime}
of sort

A_{j}^{\prime}
at index
{i^{*}}
agrees with the corresponding field of
{a^{*}}
.
It utilizes variables

u_{(a,1)},\ldots,u_{(a,n_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})})}

to keep the translation flat.

\mathbf{L_{1}}({{{\phi}}})
applies
{\mathbf{L_{0}}}
to every select literal in

{{\phi}}
, while
\mathbf{L_{2}}({{{\phi}}})
applies it for all indices
occurring in the formula and every array variable that appears under a store.

The motivation for
\mathbf{L_{3}}({{{\phi}}})
is to ensure that
for every array sort
A
,
there is a one to one mapping between the interpretations of free variables of sort
A^{\prime}
and those of sort
{A^{*}}
.

f_{A}
and
g_{A}
are asserted to be inverses of one another
when restricted to the relevant domain, thus ensuring the 1-1 correspondence.

We now combine these sets of literals to obtain the full set of lemmas
associated with the formula
{{\phi}}
.
This allows us to define the final translation function
\bf{trFormula}
.

Definition 29

Let
{{\phi}}
be a flat cube in an NDT signature
\Sigma
.
The set of lemmas associated with
{{\phi}}
is

{\bf L}({{{\phi}}})=\mathbf{L_{1}}({{{\phi}}})\cup\mathbf{L_{2}}({{{\phi}}})\cup\mathbf{L_{3}}({{{\phi}}}).

The translation
\bf{trFormula}
is defined by:

\bf{trFormula}({{\phi}})=\bf{tr}({{\phi}})\land\bigwedge{\bf L}({{{\phi}}}).

Example 30

Consider
{{\varphi}}
from
Table

1
.

\mathbf{L_{0}^{{{\varphi}}}}({a,i})=\{u_{(a,1)}=\textbf{select}(a^{\prime},{i^{*}}),u_{(a,1)}=s_{(c_{1},2)}({a^{*}})\}
.
Further,

\mathbf{L_{1}}({{{\varphi}}})=\mathbf{L_{0}^{{{\varphi}}}}({a,i})
.
Since there are no stores,
\mathbf{L_{2}}({{{\varphi}}})=\emptyset.

Thanks to
\mathbf{L_{1}}({{{\varphi}}})
, we obtain the desired

T_{\text{new}}^{{{\varphi}}}
-unsatisfiability,
as illustrated in
Figure

3
. Notice that unsatisfiability
follows directly from the theory of datatypes, as there is a cycle that does not go through
any array:
indeed,
{x^{*}}
and
{a^{*}}
are of sorts

{E^{*}}
and
{A^{*}}
, respectively, and both are datatype sorts that belong to

{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{2}^{{\varphi}}}
.
This is in contrast to the last two rows of
Figure

2
, where
x
is a datatype variable
and
a
is an array variable.

4.4
Correctness

The next theorem shows that our translation is correct, in the sense that it preserves
(un)satisfiability, assuming a stable infiniteness condition.

Theorem 31

Suppose
T_{n+1}
is
stably infinite w.r.t.
\big(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{i}}\big)\cap{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
.
Then

{{\phi}}
is
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-satisfiable
iff

\bf{trFormula}({{\phi}})
is
T_{\text{new}}^{{{\phi}}}
-satisfiable.
The left-to-right direction holds without
assuming stable infiniteness.

Given that the translation is correct and is easily computable, it is only left to show that the target theory
is decidable, which holds under the same stable infiniteness assumptions.

Theorem 32

Suppose
T_{n+1}
is stably infinite w.r.t.

\big(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{i}}\big)\cap{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}

Then
T_{\text{new}}^{{{\phi}}}
is decidable.

Example 33

Consider
\Sigma_{1}
,
\Sigma_{2}
,
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}
from
Table

1
;
by
Example

13
,
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}
is the NDT signature of
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
.
Let
T_{1},T_{2}
be the array theory over
\Sigma_{1}
and the
\Sigma_{2}
-theory of datatypes;

T_{2}
is stably infinite w.r.t.
\{I,A\}
(see
Section

2.3
).
By
Theorems

31
and
32
,
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
is decidable.

5
Implementation and Evaluation

A prototype implementation of the translation is
implemented as a preprocessing pass in the
\mathbf{cvc5}
SMT
solver
1
. Although the reduction is defined over flat cubes, the
implementation handles arbitrary input formulas by applying the translation
function
\bf{trFormula}
recursively to all subterms.

5.1
Setup

All experiments were conducted on a machine running Ubuntu 22.04 via WSL2 (Windows Subsystem for Linux 2, kernel
6.6.87.2-microsoft-standard-WSL2
), equipped with a 13th Gen Intel Core i7-1355U processor (4 cores, 8 logical CPUs) and 11 GiB of RAM.
A time limit of 10 seconds was imposed per benchmark.
We used
\mathbf{Z3}
version 4.15.6 with default options, and our fork of
\mathbf{cvc5}
, invoked with the options to support our implementation.
2
2

2

An artifact that includes the source code, binaries, benchmarks, scripts, and results is available in
https://zenodo.org/records/20055878
.

5.2
Benchmarks

{\psi}({k}):=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}\Bigl(a_{i}={\mathbf{chld}}(x_{i})\land x_{i\bmod k+1}=\textbf{select}(a_{i},j)\land\text{is}_{{\mathbf{c}}}(x_{i})\Bigr).

{\psi}^{\prime}({k}):=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}\Bigl(a_{i}={\mathbf{chld}}(x_{i})\land\text{is}_{{\mathbf{c}}}(x_{i})\Bigr)\land\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k-1}x_{i\bmod k+1}=\textbf{select}(a_{i},j).

Figure 4:
Hand-crafted formulas.

We evaluated our reduction on two independent benchmark suites:

Synthetic cycle benchmarks:

formulas that systematically generalize
{{\varphi}}
from
Table

1
.
For each
k\geq 1
, the formula
{\psi}({k})
is given in

Figure

4
.
It encodes the existence of a cycle of size
2k
, where half of the cycle goes through arrays and half through datatypes.
Notice that

{\psi}({1})
is the same as
{{\varphi}}
from
Table

1
.

For every
k
,
{\psi}({k})
is not satisfiable in the theory of nested datatypes.
In order to test our implementation on satisfiable formulas, we generate
satisfiable variants
by removing a single constraint from
{\psi}({k})
,
which corresponds to breaking the cycle. These
are formulas
{\psi}^{\prime}({k})
from
Figure

4
.

We have generated
{\psi}({k})
for
1\leq k\leq 500
.
The satisfiable instances were much more difficult for the solvers,
and hence
we generated
{\psi}^{\prime}({k})
for
1\leq k\leq 50
.
This smaller bound is already beyond
the largest
k
either solver can solve.

Move Prover benchmarks:

verification benchmarks that originate from the Move Prover
21
.
The Move Prover
21
is a formal verification tool for smart contracts written in the Move language
5
.
It works by encoding smart contracts, as well as verification conditions for them, in the Boogie intermediate verification language
2
, which then generates SMT-LIB queries that are sent to SMT-solvers.
3
3

3

We used commit

ac0b70a656bfb63eb7e569444b7a3a5ed318d6de
of
12
,
and commit

044e533713346840b55e53c33082911fd077d299
of

6
.

In order to correctly model persistent data structures, the encoding
employs the combination of datatypes and arrays, where the intended meaning is that of nested datatypes.
4
4

4

In fact, the work presented in this paper was motivated and initiated by the needs of the Move Prover.

We considered 15 tests
from the Move Library.
These tests use features that our translation does not support, like quantifier reasoning, incremental solving and constant arrays.
Therefore, we performed the following steps using a script (available in the artifact):

1.

Deleted top-level commands that use unsupported features, namely
quantifiers and constant arrays.

2.

Inlined all incremental
check-sat
calls by tracking the
push
and

pop
commands and generating SMT-LIB files that contain all relevant
definitions and assertions in each context.

3.

Removed options specific to
\mathbf{Z3}
and Boogie labels, which are
automatically produced by Boogie.

Concretely, across the 15 original benchmarks, the preprocessing considered
13,313 top-level SMT-LIB commands and removed 4029 of them:
2832 containing quantifiers, 236 containing constant arrays, 944
\mathbf{Z3}

options, and 17 Boogie label commands.
The remaining 9284 commands were then split according to the incremental
contexts.
This way, we generated 76 non-incremental quantifier-free SMT-LIB files (51 satisfiable and 25
unsatisfiable), with an average of about 5 non-incremental benchmarks for each
original incremental benchmark.

In the Move prover, some nested datatypes include arrays whose
index sort is a (nested) datatype, violating
the assumption

{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\,\cap\,{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}\;=\;\emptyset

of
Definition

12
.
This assumption was introduced to simplify the presentation,
and
our implementation supports such nested datatypes.
The reason is that nested datatypes appearing in array indices are handled as
a separate NDT signature from the one used for array elements.
Thus, cycles through array elements are still detected by the reduction
described in this paper, while the nested structure of the index sort is
solved by an independent NDT component.
Sorts that cannot participate in such cycles, such as integers, may still be
shared between these components; the remaining interaction is then handled by
standard theory-combination techniques.

While the Move prover benchmarks originate from a real-world application,
they represent only a part of that application, as unsupported features are removed.
Still, including them is relevant because the resulting benchmarks represent
the basic quantifier-free non-incremental reasoning necessary (though not sufficient)
for the original benchmarks.

Table 11:
Results. MPS and MPU denote satisfiable and unsatisfiable Move Prover benchmarks.

Table

11
summarizes the results, in terms of number of benchmarks that were solved within the time limit, and the total runtimes (in seconds) over the benchmarks that
were commonly solved
by both
\mathbf{cvc5}
and
\mathbf{Z3}
.

\mathbf{cvc5}
is able to solve all benchmarks from the unsatisfiable synthetic set, and
\mathbf{Z3}
can solve most of them. On the commonly solved benchmarks,
\mathbf{cvc5}
is faster, and for most instances is able to solve the formula in under 2 seconds.
The satisfiable synthetic benchmarks are harder for both solvers.

\mathbf{cvc5}
solves half of them, while
\mathbf{Z3}
only solves one.
Both solvers are able to solve all Move Prover benchmarks quickly, though
\mathbf{cvc5}
is slightly faster on the satisfiable ones.

6
Conclusion

We have introduced and studied a theory of nested datatypes, that avoids
non-standard models.
A decision procedure was presented, which is based
on a translation to the standard combination between arrays and datatypes,
augmented with uninterpreted functions.
Our empirical evaluation supports the decision procedure and shows its effectiveness
on both real-world and synthetic benchmarks.

Next immediate steps is to expand the theoretical and practical support of our framework
to incremental solving and quantifiers.
More generally,
he desired combination of arrays and datatypes is just a particular instance of
a wider problem with naive theory combination. Indeed, similar undetected cycles
can appear when replacing arrays with sequences, sets, and other data structures.
A more general solution is therefore needed.
We are hopeful that the particular case of arrays that we have solved here
can serve as a basis for a more general solution.

Appendix

In this appendix we prove the main theoretical results
of the paper, namely

Theorem

31
(correctness) and
Theorem

32
(decidability).
For
Theorem

31
, we split
the proof for soundness and completeness.
Soundness is proven in
Appendix

A
, and individually stated as
Theorem

35
.
Completeness is proven in
Appendix

B
, and individually stated as
Theorem

B.60
.
The proof of
Theorem

32
is presented in
Appendix

C
.

We shall make use of the following classical result:

Theorem 34
(Nelson–Oppen
14
;
19
)

Let
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
be decidable
\Sigma_{1}
- and
\Sigma_{2}
-theories, respectively,
such that:

(i)

{\cal F}_{\Sigma_{1}}\cap{\cal F}_{\Sigma_{2}}=\emptyset
and

{\cal P}_{\Sigma_{1}}\cap{\cal P}_{\Sigma_{2}}=\emptyset
; and

(ii)

T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are both stably infinite w.r.t.
S_{0}={\cal S}_{\Sigma_{1}}\cap{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{2}}
.

Then
T_{1}\oplus T_{2}
is decidable.

Appendix A
Soundness

In what follows,
let
\Sigma
be the NDT signature of
\Sigma_{1},\ldots,\Sigma_{n+1}
,
where
\Sigma_{1},\dots,\Sigma_{n}
are arrays signatures and
\Sigma_{n+1}
is a datatypes signature.
We prove the following soundness theorem:

Theorem 35

For every flat
\Sigma
-cube
{{\phi}}
,
if
{{\phi}}
is
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-satisfiable,
then
\bf{trFormula}({{\phi}})
is
T_{\text{new}}^{{{\phi}}}
-satisfiable.

As a motivating example, the formula
{{\varphi}}
from
Table

1
is
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-unsatisfiable,
and thus cannot be used for the current section.
We therefore use the following
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-satisfiable example instead.

Example 36

Table

12
includes a
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-satisfiable formula

{{\psi}}
.
The translated formula
\bf{trFormula}({{\psi}})
, according to
Definition

29
, is
also presented in
Table

12
.
Here,
{x^{*}},{y^{*}}
and
u_{(a,1)}
are variables of sort
{E^{*}}
,

{a^{*}}
is a variable of sort
{A^{*}}
,

{j^{*}},{k^{*}}
are of sort
{I^{*}}
,
and
a^{\prime}
is an array variable of sort
A^{\prime}
.
We shall later see that indeed this translated formula is

T_{\text{new}}^{{{\phi}}}
-satisfiable.

Let us now consider an example of an NDT
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}
-interpretation.

Example 37

An example for a
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}
-interpretation

is given in

.
Notice that
{\cal A}_{0}\models{{\psi}}
.
For illustration,

also includes

that do not occur in

.
It can be shown that

E^{{\cal A}_{0}}=\{{\mathbf{emp}}\}\cup\{{\mathbf{c}}(n,y)\mid n\in\mathbb{N},\,y\in A^{{\cal A}_{0}}\}
,
and
A^{{\cal A}_{0}}=\text{AC}({I^{{\cal A}_{0}}},{E^{{\cal A}_{0}}})
.

y^{{\cal A}_{0}}
can be thought of as a container with id 2 and infinitely
many copies of
{\mathbf{emp}}
in an integer indexed array.
It can be shown that
{\cal A}_{0}
is an NDT-
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}
-structure.

{{\psi}}

x=\textbf{select}(a,j)\;\land\;y={\mathbf{c}}(k,a)

\bf{trFormula}({{\psi}})

{x^{*}}=\textbf{select}(a^{\prime},{j^{*}})\land{y^{*}}={{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}({k^{*}},{a^{*}})\land

u_{(a,1)}=\textbf{select}(a^{\prime},{j^{*}})\land u_{(a,1)}=s_{(c_{1},2)}({a^{*}})\land

a^{\prime}=f_{A}({a^{*}})\land{a^{*}}=g_{A}(a^{\prime})

Table 12:
A
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-formula and its translation.
Note that
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}^{{{\psi}}}
is the same as
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}^{{{\varphi}}}
,which is

Table

.

Domains

I^{{\cal A}_{0}}=\mathbb{N}

E^{{\cal A}_{0}}=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}E^{i}

A^{{\cal A}_{0}}=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}A^{i}

where:

A^{0}=E^{0}=\emptyset

E^{i+1}=E^{i}\cup\{{\mathbf{emp}}\}\cup\{{\mathbf{c}}(n,y)\mid n\in\mathbb{N},\,y\in A^{i}\}
,

A^{i+1}=\text{AC}({\mathbb{N}},{E^{i}})

Functions

select
and
store
are defined as

read and update
operations on functions.

{\mathbf{c}}^{{\cal A}_{0}}(n,y)={\mathbf{c}}(n,y)

{\mathbf{id}}^{{\cal A}_{0}}({\mathbf{c}}(n,y))=n

A.2
Interpretation of the Variables

In order to define the interpretation of the variables, we define two mappings between the domains of the sorts in
\Sigma
and the domains of the sorts in
\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}
.

The mapping
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}
will be used to define the interpretation of all variables in
\bf{trFormula}({{\phi}})
whose sorts are not in
\{A_{i}^{\prime}\mid i\in[1,n]\}
, and the mapping
\Gamma_{\text{Arr}}
will be used to define the interpretation of the variables of sorts
A_{i}^{\prime}
for
i\in[1,n]
.

A.2.1
Defining
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}

In order to define the first mapping,
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}
, we first need to define the following partial function:

Definition 39
(Height)

We define the partial function
0pt:\bigcup_{\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}}\sigma^{\cal A}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
in the following way:
For each
t\in\bigcup_{\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}}\sigma^{\cal A}
,

(a)

If
t=c(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})
for some
c\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}
and
0pt
is defined over
t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}
, we thus define:

0pt(t)\;=\;1+\max\{0pt(t_{1}),\ldots,0pt(t_{m})\}.

(b)

If
t\in A_{j}^{\cal A}
for some
j\in[1,n]
.
Then, let

\iota_{1},\dots,\iota_{n_{(\sigma,{{\phi}})}}

be the elements of
{\mathbb{I}}_{(\sigma,{{\phi}})}
,
listed in the order determined by
o_{(\sigma,{{\phi}})}
.
Then, we define:

0pt(t)\;=\;1+\max\{0pt(\textbf{select}_{\sigma}^{\cal A}\!(t,\iota_{1}^{\cal A})),\ldots,0pt(\textbf{select}_{\sigma}^{\cal A}\!(t,\iota_{n_{(\sigma,{{\phi}})}}^{\cal A}))\}.

If
0pt
is defined over all the elements of the set above.

(c)

If
\sigma\notin({\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma})
, then
define:

0pt(t)=0

Example 40

Consider
{\cal A}_{0}
from
Table

13

Then, we have:

\displaystyle 0pt(x^{{\cal A}_{0}})

\displaystyle=0pt({\mathbf{emp}})=1+\max\emptyset=1,

\displaystyle 0pt(a^{{\cal A}_{0}})

\displaystyle=0pt(\textit{const}({{\mathbf{emp}}}))=1+\max\bigl\{0pt(x^{{\cal A}_{0}})\bigr\}=1+\max\{1\}=2,

\displaystyle 0pt(y^{{\cal A}_{0}})

\displaystyle=0pt({\mathbf{cons}}(2,\textit{const}({{\mathbf{emp}}})))=1+\max\bigl\{0pt(2),0pt(a^{{\cal A}_{0}})\bigr\}=1+\max\{0,2\}=3,

\displaystyle 0pt(i^{{\cal A}_{0}})

\displaystyle=0pt(j^{{\cal A}_{0}})=0.

Lemma 41

The function
0pt
is a total function over
\bigcup_{\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}}\sigma^{\cal A}
.

Proof A.42
.

Suppose, towards a contradiction, that there exists some
t\in\bigcup_{\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}}\sigma^{\cal A}
such that
0pt(t)
is not defined.
We know that
t\notin{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}
, since otherwise
0pt(t)
would be defined by the third case of
Definition

39
.
Thus, there are 2 possibilities:

1.

t
is of sort
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
.
Then, since
{\cal A}^{\Sigma_{n+1}}
is a datatypes
\Sigma_{n+1}
-interpretation,
t
must be of the form
c(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})
for some constructor
c\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}\in\sigma^{\cal A}
.
So there must be some
t_{j}
such that
0pt(t_{j})
is not defined.
Note that
(t_{j},t)\in{\cal R}_{{\cal A}}
.

2.

t
is of sort
A_{j}
for some
j\in[1,n]
.
Then, there must be some
i\in{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{j},{{\phi}})}
such that
0pt(\textbf{select}_{A_{j}}^{\cal A}(t,i^{\cal A}))
is not defined.
Note that
(\textbf{select}_{A_{j}}^{\cal A}(t,i^{\cal A}),t)\in{\cal R}_{{\cal A}}
.

In either case, there exists some
s\in\bigcup_{\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}}\sigma^{\cal A}
such that
(s,t)\in{\cal R}_{{\cal A}}
and
0pt(s)
is not defined.
We can apply this argument again and get an infinitely descending sequence of elements in
{\cal R}_{{\cal A}}
, which contradicts the well-foundedness of
{\cal R}_{{\cal A}}
.

Now, we can use the function
0pt
to define the mapping
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}
.

Definition A.43
.

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the partial function

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}\;\colon\;\bigcup_{\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}}\{x\in\sigma^{\cal A}\mid 0pt(x)\leq k\}\;\longrightarrow\;\bigcup_{\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}}{\sigma^{*}}^{{\cal B}}

In the following way:
For
k=0
, we define
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{0}(t)=t
for every
t\in\sigma^{\cal A}
such that
0pt(t)=0
.
For
k>0
, we define
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}(t)
as follows:

(a)

If
0pt(t)<k
, then we define
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}(t)=\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k-1}(t)
.

(b)

If
0pt(t)=k
, then we define
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}(t)
as follows:

(i)

If
t=c(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})
for some
c:\sigma_{1}\times\ldots\times\sigma_{m}\rightarrow\sigma\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
and
\forall i\in[1,m]
,
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}(t_{i})
is defined and in
{\sigma_{i}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}
, then we define:

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}(t)\;=\;{c^{*}}\bigl(\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}(t_{1}),\,\dots,\,\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}(t_{m})\bigr).

(ii)

If
t\in A_{j}^{\cal A}
for some
j\in[1,n]
, and for every
i\in{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{j},{{\phi}})}
,
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}(\textbf{select}_{A_{j}}^{\cal A}(t,i^{\cal A}))
is defined and in
{E_{j}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}
, then we define:

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}(t)\;=\;c_{j}\bigl(\,t,\,\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}(\textbf{select}_{A_{j}}^{\cal A}(t,\iota_{1}^{\cal A})),\dots,\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}(\textbf{select}_{A_{j}}^{\cal A}(t,\iota_{n_{(A_{j},{{\phi}})}}^{\cal A}))\bigr).

We then define:

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}=\bigcup_{k=0}^{\infty}\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}.

Lemma A.44
.

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}:\bigcup_{\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}}\sigma^{\cal A}\rightarrow\bigcup_{\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}}{\sigma^{*}}^{\cal B}
is a total function.
In addition,

\forall x\in\sigma^{\cal A}.\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(x)\in{\sigma^{*}}^{\cal B}
for every
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
.

Proof A.45
.

First note that for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}
is a partial function over
\bigcup_{\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}}\{x\in\sigma^{\cal A}\mid 0pt(x)\leq k\}
, and that for every
j,k\in\mathbb{N}
, if
j<k
, then
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{j}\subseteq\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}
.
Thus,
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}
is a partial function over
\bigcup_{\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}}\sigma^{\cal A}
.

In order to show it is a total function, we first use induction on
k
to show that
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}
is a total function over
\bigcup_{\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}}\{x\in\sigma^{\cal A}\mid 0pt(x)\leq k\}
and that
\forall\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}.\forall x\in\sigma^{\cal A}.
if
0pt(x)\leq k
, then
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}(x)\in{\sigma^{*}}^{\cal B}
.

\forall x\in\sigma^{\cal A}.\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}(x)\in{\sigma^{*}}^{\cal B}

Base case:
For
k=0
, let
t\in\sigma^{\cal A}
such that
0pt(t)=0
.
Then, by
Definition

39
,
\sigma\notin({\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma})
. Hence, we have
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{0}(t)=t
and since
\sigma^{\cal A}={\sigma^{*}}^{\cal B}
, we have that
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{0}(t)\in{\sigma^{*}}^{\cal B}
.

Inductive step:
Assume that
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}
is a total function over
\bigcup_{\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}}\{x\in\sigma^{\cal A}\mid 0pt(x)\leq k\}
and that
\forall\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}.\forall x\in\sigma^{\cal A}.
if
0pt(x)\leq k
, then
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}(x)\in{\sigma^{*}}^{\cal B}
.
Let there be
t\in\sigma^{\cal A}
such that
0pt(t)\leq k+1
.
If
0pt(t)<k+1
, then by the inductive hypothesis, we know that
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}(t)
is defined and in
{\sigma^{*}}^{\cal B}
.
Otherwise, by
Definition

39
, we have two cases:

(a)

If
t\in\sigma^{\cal A}
where
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
, then since
{\cal A}^{\Sigma_{n+1}}
is a datatypes
\Sigma_{n+1}
-interpretation, we must have

t=c(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})

for some
c
and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}
. By the definition of the
0pt
function, we know that
0pt(t_{i})\leq k
for every
i\in[1,m]
.
By the inductive hypothesis, we have that
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k+1}(t_{i})=\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}(t_{i})
and in
{\sigma_{i}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}
for every
i\in[1,m]
.
Thus,

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k+1}(t)\;=\;{c^{*}}\bigl(\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k+1}(t_{1}),\,\dots,\,\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k+1}(t_{m})\bigr).

Since
{c^{*}}\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}}
, we have that

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k+1}(t)\in{\sigma^{*}}^{\cal B}
.

(b)

If
t\in A_{j}^{\cal A}
for some
j\in[1,n]
, then by the definition of the
0pt
function, we know that
0pt(\textbf{select}_{A_{j}}^{\cal A}(t,i^{\cal A}))\leq k
for every
i\in{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{j},{{\phi}})}
.
By the inductive hypothesis, we have that
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k+1}(\textbf{select}_{A_{j}}^{\cal A}(t,i^{\cal A}))=\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}(\textbf{select}_{A_{j}}^{\cal A}(t,i^{\cal A}))
and in
{E_{j}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}
for every
i\in{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{j},{{\phi}})}
.
Thus,

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k+1}(t)\;=\;c_{j}\bigl(\,t,\,\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k+1}(\textbf{select}_{A_{j}}^{\cal A}(t,\iota_{1}^{\cal A})),\dots,\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k+1}(\textbf{select}_{A_{j}}^{\cal A}(t,\iota_{n_{(A_{j},{{\phi}})}}^{\cal A}))\bigr).

Since
c_{j}\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}}
, we have that

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k+1}(t)\in{\sigma^{*}}^{\cal B}
.

Let there be
t\in\sigma^{\cal A}
we now show that
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(t)
is defined and in
{\sigma^{*}}^{\cal B}
.
Since
Lemma

41
shows that
0pt
is a total function over
\bigcup_{\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}}\sigma^{\cal A}
, we have that
\forall t\in\bigcup_{\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}}\sigma^{\cal A}.\;\exists k\in\mathbb{N}.\;0pt(t)=k
. Thus,
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(t)=\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}(t)
and
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}^{k}(t)\in{\sigma^{*}}^{\cal B}
.

Remark A.46
.

Note that the function
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}
satisfies the following properties:
For each
t\in\sigma^{\cal A}
and every
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
, we have:

(a)

If
t=c(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})
for some
c\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
,

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(t)\;=\;{c^{*}}\bigl(\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(t_{1}),\,\dots,\,\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(t_{m})\bigr).

(b)

If
\sigma=A_{j}
for some
j\in[1,n]
.
Then, let

\iota_{1},\dots,\iota_{n_{(\sigma,{{\phi}})}}

be the elements of
{\mathbb{I}}_{(\sigma,{{\phi}})}
,
listed in the order determined by
o_{(\sigma,{{\phi}})}
.
Then,

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(t)\;=\;c_{j}\bigl(\,t,\,\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(\,\textbf{select}_{\sigma}^{\cal A}\!(t,\iota_{1}^{\cal A})),\dots,\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(\,\textbf{select}_{\sigma}^{\cal A}\!(t,\iota_{n_{(\sigma,{{\phi}})}}^{\cal A}))\bigr).

(c)

If
\sigma\notin({\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma})
, then

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(t)=t
.

Example A.47
.

Consider the following elements in the interpretations of the sorts in
Table

13
:

1.

f_{1}:=\textit{const}({{\mathbf{emp}}})\in A^{{\cal A}_{0}}
.

2.

x_{1}:={\mathbf{c}}(1,f_{1})\in E^{{\cal A}_{0}}
.

3.

f_{2}:=\textit{const}({x_{1}})\in A^{{\cal A}_{0}}
.

4.

x_{2}={\mathbf{c}}(2,f_{2})\in E^{{\cal A}_{0}}
.

Then,

1.

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(f_{1})=c_{1}\bigl(f_{1},\Gamma_{\text{DT}}({\mathbf{emp}})\bigr)=c_{1}\bigl(f_{1},{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}}\bigr)
.

2.

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(x_{1})={{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}\bigl(\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(1),\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(f_{1})\bigr)
=

{{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}\bigl(1,c_{1}\bigl(f_{1},{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}}\bigr)\bigr)
.

3.

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(f_{2})=c_{1}\bigl(f_{2},\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(x_{1})\bigr)=c_{1}\bigl(f_{2},{{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}\bigl(1,c_{1}\bigl(f_{1},{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr)
.

4.

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(x_{2})={{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}\bigl(\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(2),\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(f_{1})\bigr)
=

{{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}\bigl(2,c_{1}\bigl(f_{2},{{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}\bigl(1,c_{1}\bigl(f_{1},{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr))
.

Lemma A.48
.

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}
is injective.

Proof A.49
.

We show first that if
t_{1}\in\sigma_{1}^{\cal A}
and
t_{2}\in\sigma_{2}^{\cal A}
with
\sigma_{1}\neq\sigma_{2}
, then
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(t_{1})\neq\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(t_{2})
.
Indeed, by
Lemma

A.44

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(t_{1})\in{\sigma_{1}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}
and
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(t_{2})\in{\sigma_{2}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}
,
and the domains for different new sorts are disjoint (see
Table

14
).

It remains to prove that for each fixed sort
\sigma
, whenever
t_{1},t_{2}\in\sigma^{\cal A}
and
t_{1}\neq t_{2}
, then

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(t_{1})\neq\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(t_{2})
. We proceed by induction on

N\;:=\;\max\bigl\{h(t_{1}),\,h(t_{2})\bigr\},

Base case (
N=0
).

According to the definition of
0pt
, if
N=0
, then
\sigma\notin({\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma})
, and thus
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(t_{1})=t_{1}\neq t_{2}=\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(t_{2})
.

Inductive step.

Assume the claim holds for all pairs whose maximum depth is
<N
. Let
t_{1},t_{2}\in\sigma^{\cal A}
with
\max\{h(t_{1}),h(t_{2})\}=N>0
. There are two cases to consider:

1.

If
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
, then there are two options:

(a)

If
t_{1}=c(s_{1},\dots,s_{m})
and
t_{2}=\hat{c}(r_{1},\dots,r_{k})
with
c\neq\hat{c}
, then according to
Remark

A.46
, we have

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(t_{1})={c^{*}}(\dots)\;\neq\;{\hat{c}^{*}}(\dots)=\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(t_{2}),

since distinct constructors yield disjoint images.

(b)

Otherwise both use the same constructor
c
, say

t_{1}=c(s_{1},\dots,s_{m})
and
t_{2}=c(r_{1},\dots,r_{m})
, but
t_{1}\neq t_{2}
. Then for some
j
,
s_{j}\neq r_{j}
and

h(s_{j}),h(r_{j})<N
. By the inductive hypothesis

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(s_{j})\neq\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(r_{j})
. Thus, due to
Remark

A.46
,

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(t_{1})={c^{*}}(\ldots,\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(s_{j}),\ldots)\neq{c^{*}}(\ldots,\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(r_{j}),\ldots)=\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(t_{2})
.

2.

If
\sigma=A_{j}\in{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}
, then

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(t_{1})=c_{j}\bigl(t_{1},\ldots\bigr)\neq c_{j}\bigl(t_{2},\ldots\bigr)=\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(t_{2})

We can now define the function
\Gamma_{\text{Arr}}
that will be used to define the interpretation of some of the variables in
\bf{trFormula}({{\phi}})
.

A.2.2
Defining
\Gamma_{\text{Arr}}

We define a function

\Gamma_{\text{Arr}}:\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}^{\cal A}\to\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}^{\prime{\cal B}}

as follows:
for each
t\in A_{j}^{\cal A}
(with
j\in[1,n]
),

\Gamma_{\text{Arr}}(t)\in A_{j}^{\prime{\cal B}}={I_{j}^{*}}^{\cal B}\rightarrow{E_{j}^{*}}^{\cal B}
is a function
defined as follows:

\forall i\in{I_{j}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}=I_{j}^{\cal A}
:

\Gamma_{\text{Arr}}(t)(i)=\Gamma_{\text{DT}}\Bigl(\textbf{select}_{A_{j}}^{\cal A}(t,i)\Bigr).

Lemma A.50
.

For every
t\in A_{j}^{\cal A}
(with
j\in[1,n]
),

\Gamma_{\text{Arr}}(t)\in A_{j}^{\prime{\cal B}}

Proof A.51
.

Let
t\in A_{j}^{\cal A}
(with
j\in[1,n]
).
Then note that every element in
{I_{j}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}
is a function from
{I_{j}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}
to
{E_{j}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}
.
Let there be
i\in{I_{j}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}
.
Since
{I_{j}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}=I_{j}^{\cal A}
, we have that
i\in I_{j}^{\cal A}
. We know that
\textbf{select}_{A_{j}}^{\cal A}(t,i)
is defined and in
E_{j}^{\cal A}
since
t\in A_{j}^{\cal A}
and
i\in I_{j}^{\cal A}
.
Lemma

A.44
shows that
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(\textbf{select}_{A_{j}}^{\cal A}(t,i))\in{E_{j}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}
.
Thus,
\Gamma_{\text{Arr}}(t)(i)=\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(\textbf{select}_{A_{j}}^{\cal A}(t,i))
is defined and in
{E_{j}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}
.

Example A.52
.

Consider
a^{{\cal A}_{0}}=\textit{const}({{\mathbf{emp}}})
from
Table

13
.
Then
\Gamma_{\text{Arr}}(a^{{\cal A}_{0}})
is a function from
{I_{1}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}=\mathbb{N}
to
{E_{1}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}
, defined as follows:

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.\Gamma_{\text{Arr}}(a^{{\cal A}_{0}})(n)=\Gamma_{\text{DT}}(\textbf{select}_{A_{1}}^{{\cal A}_{0}}(a^{{\cal A}_{0}},n))=\Gamma_{\text{DT}}({\mathbf{emp}})={{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}}
.
Thus,
\Gamma_{\text{Arr}}(a^{{\cal A}_{0}})=\textit{const}({{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}}})

Lemma A.53
.

\Gamma_{\text{Arr}}
is injective.

Proof A.54
.

Let
t_{1}\in A_{j}^{{\cal A}}
and
t_{2}\in A_{k}^{{\cal A}}
with
j,k\in[1,n]
, such that
t_{1}\neq t_{2}
.

Case 1:

j\neq k
.
Then
t_{1}\in A_{j}^{\cal A}
and
t_{2}\in A_{k}^{\cal A}
, so

\Gamma_{\text{Arr}}(t_{1})\in A_{j}^{\prime{\cal B}}\land\Gamma_{\text{Arr}}(t_{2})\in A_{k}^{\prime{\cal B}}.

These are disjoint domains by construction, so
\Gamma_{\text{Arr}}(t_{1})\neq\Gamma_{\text{Arr}}(t_{2})
.

Case 2:

j=k
, but
t_{1}\neq t_{2}
.
Then
t_{1},t_{2}\in A_{j}^{{\cal A}}
. Since
{\cal A}
satisfies extensionality for arrays, there exists some index
i\in I_{j}^{{\cal A}}={I_{j}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}
such that

\textbf{select}_{A_{j}}^{{\cal A}}(t_{1},i)\neq\textbf{select}_{A_{j}}^{{\cal A}}(t_{2},i).

By the definition of
\Gamma_{\text{Arr}}
and the injectivity of
\Gamma_{\text{DT}}
(Lemma
A.48
),

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\text{Arr}}(t_{1})(i)=\Gamma_{\text{DT}}\bigl(\textbf{select}_{A_{j}}^{\cal A}(t_{1},i)\bigr)\neq\Gamma_{\text{DT}}\bigl(\textbf{select}_{A_{j}}^{\cal A}(t_{2},i)\bigr)=\Gamma_{\text{Arr}}(t_{2})(i)

Hence
\Gamma_{\text{Arr}}(t_{1})\neq\Gamma_{\text{Arr}}(t_{2})
in both cases. This proves that

\Gamma_{\text{Arr}}
is injective.

\Gamma_{\text{DT}}
and

.

x
for

,

in

of

in

;

a
in
{\cal A}
; finally,
if

for some

A_{i}

\Sigma

,
then
x

using the following considerations.
First, let
j
such that
k=o_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}(j)

(or, equivalently,
j=o_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}^{-1}(k)
).
The reason that every
u_{(a,k)}
is interpreted this way is that we want to ensure that every possible application of
\mathbf{L_{0}^{{{\phi}}}}({a,i})
is satisfied, as seen in
Section

A.6
.

Example A.55
.

Consider the formula
\bf{trFormula}({{\psi}})
from

.
Its variable interpretations in

,
