.

B.2
Defining the Domains of the
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-interpretation

Recall that starting from a
T_{\text{new}}^{{{\phi}}}
interpretation

{\cal B}
, we are constructing a
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-interpretation
{\cal A}
.
Due to
Lemma

B.64
we can assume that
the conditions it specifies hold.

We define the domains of
{\cal A}
as follows.
For each sort
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
, we inductively define a sequence

\{\sigma^{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

in
Table

22
,
and then set

\sigma^{\cal A}=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\sigma^{i}
.

Example B.67
.

Consider again the formula
{{\psi}}
in
Table

12
.
We have seen in
Example

B.66
that

\bf{trFormula}({{\psi}})
is
satisfied by the interpretation

{\cal B}_{1}

from
Table

21
.
The domains of the sorts in the above construction
are the same as those in
Table

13

(The interpretations of the variables may differ).

Our next goal is to show that the defined domains are indeed non-empty. In order to prove it we first show the following lemma:

Lemma B.68
.

For every sort
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
and every
i\in\mathbb{N}
,

\sigma^{i}\;\subseteq\;\sigma^{i+1}
.

Proof B.69
.

We consider three cases depending on the nature of
\sigma
.

1.

If
\sigma\notin{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}
, then by definition

\sigma^{i}={\sigma^{*}}^{{\cal B}}=\sigma^{i+1}
,
so the inclusion is immediate.

2.

If
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
, then

\sigma^{i+1}=\sigma^{i}\;\cup\;T_{\sigma}\bigl({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B^{i}\bigr),

and hence
\sigma^{i}\subseteq\sigma^{i+1}
.

3.

For all
k\in[1,n]
, we use induction over
i\in\mathbb{N}
to show that
A_{k}^{i}\subseteq A_{k}^{i+1}
.

base:
if
i=0
, then

A_{k}^{0}=\emptyset,

inductive step:

If
i\geq 1
, then since

E_{k}^{i-1}\subseteq E_{k}^{i}

and

I_{k}^{i-1}=I_{k}^{i}
,
we have

A_{k}^{i}=\text{AC}({I_{k}^{i-1}},{E_{k}^{i-1}})=\text{AC}({I_{k}^{i}},{E_{k}^{i-1}})\;\subseteq\;\text{AC}({I_{k}^{i}},{E_{k}^{i}})=A_{k}^{i+1}.

The inclusion holds since enlarging the codomain
Y
in
\text{AC}({X},{Y})
preserves all almost constant functions.

Now we can prove that the domains of the sorts in
\Sigma
are non-empty.

Lemma B.70
.

For every sort
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
, the domain
\sigma^{{\cal A}}
is nonempty.

Proof B.71
.

Let

S\;=\;{\cal S}_{\Sigma}\,\setminus\,\bigl({\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}\cup{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\bigr).

Since all the sorts in
{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
are well-founded with respect to
S
, for
each
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
there is some
k\in\mathbb{N}

with

\sigma\in F_{k}(\Sigma,S)

(see
Definition

10
).
We prove by induction on
k
that

\forall\sigma\in F_{k}(\Sigma,S).\quad\sigma^{k}\neq\emptyset.

Base case (
k=0
).

Then
\sigma\in F_{0}(\Sigma,S)=S
, and by definition
\sigma^{0}={\sigma^{*}}^{\cal B}
, which is nonempty, as
{\cal B}
is an interpretation.

Inductive step.

Assume the claim holds for
k
. Take any
\sigma\in F_{k+1}(\Sigma,S)
. By definition

F_{k+1}=F_{k}\;\cup\;\bigl\{\sigma\mid\exists\,c:\sigma_{1}\times\cdots\times\sigma_{n}\to\sigma,\;\sigma_{i}\in F_{k}\bigr\}\;\cup\;\{A_{i}\mid I_{i},E_{i}\in F_{k}\}.

There are three cases:

1.

If
\sigma\in F_{k}
, then
\sigma^{k}\neq\emptyset
by IH, so according to
Lemma

B.68
,
\sigma^{k+1}\supseteq\sigma^{k}\neq\emptyset
.

2.

If there exists a constructor
c:\sigma_{1}\times\cdots\times\sigma_{n}\to\sigma
with each
\sigma_{i}\in F_{k}
, then IH gives

\sigma_{i}^{k}\neq\emptyset
. Hence

\bigl\{c(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\mid t_{i}\in\sigma_{i}^{k}\bigr\}\;\subseteq\;T_{\sigma}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B^{k})\;\subseteq\;\sigma^{k+1},

so
\sigma^{k+1}\neq\emptyset
.

3.

If
\sigma=A_{i}
is an array sort where
E_{i},I_{i}\in F_{k}
, then IH gives
I_{i}^{k}\neq\emptyset
and
E_{i}^{k}\neq\emptyset
. Therefore:

A_{i}^{k+1}=\text{AC}({I_{i}^{k}},{E_{i}^{k}})\neq\emptyset
.

Hence for each
\sigma
there is some
k
with
\sigma^{k}\neq\emptyset
, and so
\sigma^{{\cal A}}=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\sigma^{k}
is nonempty.

B.3
Properties of the Domains of the
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-interpretation

In order to show that
{\cal A}
is a
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-interpretation, we need to show that the relation
{\cal R}_{{\cal A}}
is well-founded (see
Definition

16
).
5
5

5

Technically,
{\cal A}
has not been fully defined yet, however, for proving well-foundedness, the interpretations of the domains, the constructors and array operators suffice.

We first prove the following lemmas, which provide a more intuitive understanding of the domains of the sorts in
{\cal A}
.

Lemma B.72
.

For every
i\in[1,n]
:

A_{i}^{\cal A}\;=\;\text{AC}({I_{i}^{\cal A}},{E_{i}^{\cal A}}).

Proof B.73
.

(
\supseteq
)
Let there be
t\in\text{AC}({I_{i}^{\cal A}},{E_{i}^{\cal A}})
, then its image is finite and lies in

\bigcup_{j}E_{i}^{j}=E_{i}^{\cal A}
.
Lemma

B.68
suggests that

\forall j\in\mathbb{N}.\;E_{i}^{j}\subseteq E_{i}^{j+1}
,
so there exists a
k
such that the image of
t
lies in

E_{i}^{k}
.
Since
I_{i}^{\cal A}=I_{i}^{k}
,
we get
t\in\text{AC}({I_{i}^{k}},{E_{i}^{k}})\;\subseteq\;A_{i}^{\cal A}.

(
\subseteq
)
By definition

A_{i}^{\cal A}\;=\;\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\text{AC}({I_{i}^{j}},{E_{i}^{j}}),

and each

E_{i}^{j}\subseteq E_{i}^{\cal A}
,

I_{i}^{j}=I_{i}^{\cal A}
,
so
\text{AC}({I_{i}^{j}},{E_{i}^{j}})\subseteq\text{AC}({I_{i}^{\cal A}},{E_{i}^{\cal A}})
.
Hence
A_{i}^{\cal A}\subseteq\text{AC}({I_{i}^{\cal A}},{E_{i}^{\cal A}})
.

Lemma B.74
.

Let
B
be the
{\bf Elem}
-sorted set with
B_{\tau}=\tau^{\cal A}
for every
\tau\in{\bf Elem}
.
Then for every
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}
.

\sigma^{\cal A}\;=\;T_{\sigma}\bigl({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B\bigr).

Proof B.75
.

(
\subseteq
).

Recall
\sigma^{\cal A}=\bigcup_{i\geq 0}\sigma^{i}
with

\sigma^{i+1}=\sigma^{i}\cup T_{\sigma}\bigl({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B^{i}\bigr),\qquad\forall\tau\in{\bf Elem}.B^{i}_{\tau}:=\tau^{i}.

For every
\tau\in{\bf Elem}
we have
B^{i}_{\tau}=\tau^{i}=\subseteq\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\tau^{j}=B_{\tau}

so according to
Lemma

B.62

T_{\sigma}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B^{i})\subseteq T_{\sigma}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)
for all
k
.
Taking the union over
i
yields

\sigma^{\cal A}\subseteq T_{\sigma}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)
.

(
\supseteq
).

For
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}
we proceed by induction on
k
that
T_{\sigma,k}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)\subseteq\sigma^{\cal A}
.

Base
k=0
.

T_{\sigma,0}=\emptyset\subseteq\sigma^{\cal A}
.

Step.
Assume

T_{\tau,k}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)\subseteq\tau^{\cal A}
for all
\tau
.
Let

c(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})\in T_{\sigma,k+1}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)
;
each
t_{j}
lies in
T_{\tau_{j},k}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)

and hence in
\tau_{j}^{\cal A}
by the IH.
Hence there exists
l_{1},\ldots l_{m}
such that

\forall j\in\{1,\ldots,m\}.\ t_{j}\in T_{\tau_{j}}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B^{l_{j}})

Denoting
l=\max\{l_{1},\ldots,l_{m}\}
, and since
B^{l_{j}}_{\tau}\subseteq B^{l}_{\tau}
for every
\tau\in{\bf Elem}
, we have, according to
Lemma

B.62

\forall j\in\{1,\ldots,m\}.\ t_{j}\in T_{\tau_{j}}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B^{l})

Therefore
c(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\in T_{\tau}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B^{l})\subseteq\sigma^{\cal A}
.

B.4
Infiniteness of Each
E_{i}^{\cal A}

In order to prove that each
E_{i}^{\cal A}
is infinite, we first
define the directed graph
G_{\Sigma}=(V,E)
over the sorts of

\Sigma
, such that the edges go either from an output sort of a constructor into one of its input sorts, or from an array sort to its corresponding element sort.
Formally:

Definition B.76
.

Let
\Sigma
be an NDT signature.
We define
G_{\Sigma}=(V,E)
to be the directed graph with

V={\cal S}_{\Sigma},\quad E=\{(\sigma,\sigma_{j})\mid\exists c\colon\sigma_{1}\times\cdots\times\sigma_{k}\to\sigma\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{n+1}},\;j\in[1,k]\}\;\cup\;\{(A_{i},E_{i})\mid j\in[1,n]\}.

A sort
\sigma
is called
cyclic
if there is a path with at least one edge in

G_{\Sigma}
that starts and ends with
\sigma
.
A sort is called
eventually cyclic
if there is a nonempty path in
G_{\Sigma}
that starts with it and ends with a cyclic sort.

Note, that by definition every cyclic sort is also eventually cyclic.

Example B.77
.

Consider
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}
from
Table

1
.
Then the nodes in the graph are
\{I,E,A\}
and the edges are:

•

E\to A
(from the constructor
{\mathbf{c}}
)

•

E\to I
(from the constructor
{\mathbf{c}}
)

•

A\to E
(from an array sort to its element sort)

Thus, the cyclic sorts are
E
and
A
(and
I
is not eventually cyclic as it has no outgoing edges).

Lemma B.78
.

Let there be
\sigma,\tau\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
such that there is a path in
G_{\Sigma}
from
\sigma
to
\tau
. Then
|\sigma^{\cal A}|\geq|\tau^{\cal A}|
.

Proof B.79
.

Write the (finite) path in
G_{\Sigma}
from
\sigma
to
\tau
as

\sigma=\rho_{0}\;\longrightarrow\;\rho_{1}\;\longrightarrow\;\dots\;\longrightarrow\;\rho_{l}=\tau\qquad(l\geq 0).

It is sufficient to show that for every
i\in[0,l-1].|\rho_{i}^{\cal A}|\geq|\rho_{i+1}^{\cal A}|
.

By the definition of the graph, the edge
(\rho_{i},\rho_{i+1})
can be justified due to one of the following two cases:

(1)

There is a constructor
c:\tau_{1}\,\dots\,\tau_{k}\to\rho_{i}
such that
\rho_{i+1}=\tau_{j}
for some
j
.

(2)

\rho_{i}=A_{k}
and
\rho_{i+1}=E_{k}
for some
k\in[1,n]
.

In both cases, there exists an injective function

f_{i}:\rho_{i+1}^{\cal A}\to\rho_{i}^{\cal A}
.

1.

In case (1), we fix for each
s\in[1,k]\setminus\{j\}
some
u_{s}\in\tau_{s}^{\cal A}
and define:

f_{i}(x)=c(u_{1},\dots,u_{j-1},x,u_{j+1},\dots,u_{k}).

2.

In case (2), we define
f_{i}(x)
as the constant array from
I_{k}^{\cal A}
to
E_{k}^{\cal A}
whose image is the singleton
\{x\}
.

Definition B.80
.

Let
\Sigma_{\text{DT}}
be a datatype signature and
{\cal C}
be a
\Sigma_{\text{DT}}
-interpretation.
For every
x\in\sigma^{\cal C}
for some
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}
, we
define the minimum index of
x
, denoted
\text{dtIdx}(x)
, as follows:

\text{dtIdx}(x)=\min\{i\in\mathbb{N}\mid x\in T_{\sigma,i}({\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}},B)\}

where
B
is the
{\bf Elem}
-sorted set with
B_{\tau}=\tau^{\cal C}
for every
\tau\in{\bf Elem}
.

The underlying signature and interpretation in
Definition

B.80
will always be clear from context.

We will make use of the following lemma concerning
\text{dtIdx}(\cdot)
and we include its routine proof for completeness.

Lemma B.81
.

Let
\Sigma_{\text{DT}}
be a datatype signature and
{\cal C}
a
\Sigma_{\text{DT}}
-interpretation.
If
x=c(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})\in\sigma^{\cal C}
for some constructor

c:\sigma_{1}\times\cdots\times\sigma_{r}\to\sigma\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}
, then

\text{dtIdx}(x)\;=\;1+\max\{\text{dtIdx}(x_{1}),\dots,\text{dtIdx}(x_{r})\}.

Proof B.82
.

Let’s assume towards a contradiction that
\text{dtIdx}(x)=0
. Since
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}
,
T_{\sigma,0}({\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}},B)=\emptyset
, in contradiction.

Hence,
\text{dtIdx}(x)\geq 1
. Let’s denote
\text{dtIdx}(x)
as
k+1
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\hat{k}=\max\{\text{dtIdx}(x_{1}),\ldots,\text{dtIdx}(x_{r})\}
.
By definition of
\text{dtIdx}(\cdot)
,

x\in T_{\sigma,k+1}({\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}},B)\setminus T_{\sigma,k}({\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}},B)
.

Note that, by definition,

\displaystyle T_{\sigma,k+1}({\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}},B)\;=\;

\displaystyle T_{\sigma,k}({\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}},B)\;\cup

\displaystyle\Bigl\{\,c(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\;\Big|\;c:\sigma_{1}\times\cdots\times\sigma_{k}\to\sigma\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{\text{DT}}},

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464ptt_{i}\in T_{\sigma_{i},k}({\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}},B)\Bigr\}.

Since
x\notin T_{\sigma,k}({\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}},B)
,
x_{i}\in T_{\sigma_{i},k}({\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}},B)
for every
i\in[1,r]
. Since
\text{dtIdx}(x_{i})
is the minimum index
j
such that
x_{i}\in T_{\sigma_{i},j}({\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}},B)
and
x_{i}\in T_{\sigma_{i},k}({\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}},B)
,

\text{dtIdx}(x_{i})\leq k
. Thus, by definition of
\hat{k}
,
\hat{k}\leq k
.

Since
x_{i}\in T_{\sigma_{i},\text{dtIdx}(x_{i})}({\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}},B)
for every
i\in[1,r]
,
we know that
x_{i}\in T_{\sigma_{i},\hat{k}}({\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}},B)
for every
i\in[1,r]
.
Hence,
x\in T_{\sigma,\hat{k}+1}({\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}},B)
and
k+1=\text{dtIdx}(x)\leq 1+\hat{k}
.

Thus
k=\hat{k}
and
\text{dtIdx}(x)=1+\max\{\text{dtIdx}(x_{1}),\ldots,\text{dtIdx}(x_{r})\}
.

Lemma B.83
.

Let
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
be
eventually cyclic
.
Then
|\sigma^{{\cal A}}|=\infty
.

Proof B.84
.

Because
\sigma
is eventually cyclic, there is a path

\sigma=\sigma_{k+\ell}\;\to\;\sigma_{k+\ell-1}\;\to\;\cdots\;\to\;\sigma_{\ell}

ending at a node
\sigma_{\ell}
that belongs to a directed cycle of some length
\ell
:

\sigma_{0}\;\to\;\sigma_{1}\;\to\;\cdots\;\to\;\sigma_{\ell}\quad(\sigma_{\ell}=\sigma_{0}).

Due to
Lemma

B.78
, it is sufficient to show that
|\sigma_{\ell}^{{\cal A}}|=\infty
in order to prove that
|\sigma^{{\cal A}}|=\infty
.

Consider the sorts
\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{\ell}
that form a directed cycle in
G_{\Sigma}
.

Let there be
m\in\mathbb{N}
, traversing the cycle
m
additional times yields a path of length
M:=m\ell
.

We use induction over
i\in[0,M]
to define
x_{i}\in\sigma_{i}^{{\cal A}}
.

Base Case:
Since all domains are not empty, there exists
x_{0}\in\sigma_{0}^{{\cal A}}
.

Inductive Step:
We define
x_{i+1}\in\sigma_{i+1}^{{\cal A}}
depending on the edge
(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{i+1})
in
G_{\Sigma}
.

The edge
(\sigma_{i+1},\sigma_{i})
occurs in the graph
G_{\Sigma}
because of one of two options (Since
{\bf{Array}}\cap{\bf Struct}=\emptyset
, only one of the two cases can hold):

•

Constructor edge:

there exists a constructor

c:\tau_{1}\times\!\cdots\!\times\tau_{k}\to\sigma_{i+1}

with
\tau_{j}=\sigma_{i}
for some
1\leq j\leq k
.
As
\tau_{1}^{\cal A},\ldots,\tau_{k}^{\cal A}
are all non-empty, we can choose arbitrary
u_{r}\in\tau_{r}^{\cal A}
for
r\in[1,k]\setminus\{j\}
.
We define:

x_{i+1}:=c(u_{1},\dots,u_{j-1},x_{i},u_{j+1},\dots,u_{k})\in T_{\sigma_{i+1}}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)=\sigma_{i+1}^{{\cal A}}

Where the equality holds due to
Lemma

B.74
as
\sigma_{i+1}\in{\bf Struct}
.

•

Array edge:

the edge
(\sigma_{i+1},\sigma_{i})
occurs in the graph because there exists some
1\leq j\leq n
such that
\sigma_{i+1}=A_{j}
,
\sigma_{i}=E_{j}
.
We define
x_{i+1}
as the constant array from
I_{j}^{\cal A}
to
E_{j}^{\cal A}
whose image is the singleton
\{x_{i}\}
.

We now show that
\{x_{j\ell}\mid j\in[0,M]\}
are distinct.
Consider two possibilities. If all the edges are constructor edges then we make use of
\text{dtIdx}(\cdot)
. Otherwise (i.e. at least one edge is an array edge) we make use of
\text{idx}(\cdot)
(see
Definitions

B.80
and
2
).

•

If all edges
(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1}),\ldots,(\sigma_{\ell-1},\sigma_{\ell})
are constructor edges, then by
Lemma

B.81
we have
\text{dtIdx}(x_{i+1})>\text{dtIdx}(x_{i})
for every
i\in[0,M-1]
. Consequently, each full traversal of the cycle (of length
\ell
) strictly increases the minimum index, i.e.

\forall k\in[0,M-1].\quad\text{dtIdx}(x_{(k+1)\ell})>\text{dtIdx}(x_{k\ell}).

Therefore the elements
\{x_{k\ell}\mid 0\leq k<m\}
are distinct.

•

Suppose some edge in the cycle is an array edge. Fix any traversal index
k\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}
and consider the
(k\ell)
-th segment. For the specific position
j\in[0,\ell-1]
where
(\sigma_{j},\sigma_{j+1})
is an array edge,
Lemma

B.110
gives a strict increase

\text{idx}(x_{k\ell+j+1})>\text{idx}(x_{k\ell+j}).

For every other step in the same traversal a constructor edge does not decrease the minimum index by
Lemma

B.108
,

\text{idx}(x_{k\ell+i+1})\geq\text{idx}(x_{k\ell+i}),

and an array edge again yields a strict increase by
Lemma

B.110
. Hence each full cycle raises the minimum index strictly, i.e.

\forall k\in[0,M-1].\qquad\text{idx}(x_{(k+1)\ell})>\text{idx}(x_{k\ell}).

It follows that

\text{idx}(x_{m\ell})>\text{idx}(x_{(m-1)\ell})>\cdots>\text{idx}(x_{0}),

so the elements
x_{0},x_{\ell},\dots,x_{(m-1)\ell}
have pairwise distinct
\text{idx}(\cdot)
values and therefore are distinct.

Since
m
can be chosen arbitrarily large, we conclude that
|\sigma_{\ell}^{{\cal A}}|=\infty
.

Now we can prove the intended lemma:

Lemma B.85
.

For every
i\in[1,n]
the domain
E_{i}^{\cal A}
is infinite.

Proof B.86
.

Assume, for the sake of contradiction, that there exists an index

i_{0}\in[1,n]
such that

|E_{i_{0}}^{\cal A}|<\infty
.

Step 0.
\forall\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}\setminus({\bf{Array}}\cup{\bf Struct}).|\sigma^{\cal A}|=\infty
.

By construction,

\sigma^{\cal A}={\sigma^{*}}^{{\cal B}},

and we assume w.l.o.g. according to
Lemma

B.64
that
{\sigma^{*}}^{{\cal B}}
is infinite.

Step 1. Single Signature and cyclicity.

If the sort
E_{i_{0}}
does not belong to any other signature other than
\Sigma_{i_{0}}
, as all the sorts of
\Sigma_{i_{0}}
are unique,

E_{i_{0}}\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}\setminus({\bf{Array}}\cup{\bf Struct})
and thus
|\sigma^{\cal A}|=\infty
according to Step 0, contradicting our assumption.
Hence
E_{i_{0}}
belongs to at least one other signature.

Consider
G_{\Sigma}=(V,E)
as defined in

Definition

B.76
.
If
E_{i_{0}}
were
eventually cyclic
in
G_{\Sigma}
,

Lemma

B.83
would entail

|E_{i_{0}}^{{\cal A}}|=\infty
.
Therefore
E_{i_{0}}
is
not
eventually cyclic.

Step 2. The reachable sub‑graph
G_{\Sigma}^{\prime}
is a tree.

Let

G_{\Sigma}^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})

be the sub‑graph of
G_{\Sigma}
that contains every sort reachable from

E_{i_{0}}
.
Because
E_{i_{0}}
is not eventually cyclic,
G_{\Sigma}^{\prime}
is a DAG.
Moreover, by
Lemma

B.78
,
every sort
\sigma\in V^{\prime}
satisfies
|\sigma^{\cal A}|<\infty
.

Step 3. Pick
E_{i_{1}}
.

Note that there exists some
E_{i_{1}}
from which no other sort in
\{E_{i}\mid i\in[1,n]\}
is reachable.
Assume towards a contradiction that there is no such sort, then from
E_{i_{0}}
we can reach some
E^{2}\in\{E_{i}\mid i\in[1,n]\}
, and from
E^{2}
we can reach some
E^{3}\in\{E_{i}\mid i\in[1,n]\}
and so on.
Thus when we look at
E^{1}=E_{i_{0}},E^{2},\ldots,E^{n+1}
the pigeonhole principle suggests that there must be a sort appearing twice, as there are only
n
elements in
\{E_{i}\mid i\in[1,n]\}
, which contradicts the acyclicity of
G_{\Sigma}^{\prime}
.

We hereby denote as
E_{i_{1}}
a sort in
V^{\prime}
from which no other sort in
\{E_{i}\mid i\in[1,n]\}
is reachable.

Let

G_{\Sigma}^{\prime\prime}=(V^{\prime\prime},E^{\prime\prime})

be the sub‑graph of
G_{\Sigma}^{\prime}
that contains every sort reachable from

E_{i_{1}}
.
As a subgraph of
G_{\Sigma}^{\prime}
,
G_{\Sigma}^{\prime\prime}
is itself a DAG.

Step 4.
V^{\prime\prime}\subseteq{\bf Struct}
.

Note that:

•

Since
V^{\prime\prime}\subseteq V^{\prime}
every sort
\sigma\in V^{\prime\prime}
satisfies
|\sigma^{\cal A}|<\infty
.

•

By definition of
E_{i_{1}}
, no other sort in
\{E_{i}\mid i\in[1,n]\}
except
E_{i_{1}}
itself belongs to
V^{\prime\prime}
.

Let there be
\sigma\in V^{\prime\prime}
. Since

V^{\prime\prime}\subseteq{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
and
{\cal S}_{\Sigma}={\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\uplus{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}\uplus({\cal S}_{\Sigma}\setminus({\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}\cup{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}))
,

\sigma
belongs to one of the three disjoint sets. (
{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}
and
{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
are disjoint by
Definition

12
).

1.

If
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}\setminus({\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}\cup{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}})
,
step 0 suggests that every such sort has an infinite domain, contradicting the fact that every sort in
V^{\prime\prime}
has a finite domain.

2.

If
\sigma=A_{k}
for some
k\in[1,n]
,
then its element sort
E_{k}
would be reachable from
A_{k}
inside
G_{\Sigma}^{\prime\prime}
,
contradicting the definition of
E_{i_{1}}
.

Hence
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
and thus
V^{\prime\prime}\subseteq{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
.

Step 5. The sorts in
V^{\prime\prime}
must be interpreted as in
{\cal A}
.

For every
T_{n+1}
-interpretation
{\cal C}
and every

\sigma\in V^{\prime\prime}
we have
\sigma^{{\cal C}}=\sigma^{\cal A}
.

In order to prove this claim, we first remember that according to
Lemma

B.74
, for every
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}
,
\sigma^{\cal A}=T_{\sigma}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)
where
B
is the
{\bf Elem}
-sorted set with
B_{\tau}=\tau^{\cal A}
for every
\tau\in{\bf Elem}
.

Since
{\cal C}
is a
T_{n+1}
-interpretation, it follows that for every
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}
,
\sigma^{{\cal C}}=T_{\sigma}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}\widehat{B})
where
\widehat{B}
is the
{\bf Elem}
-sorted set with
\widehat{B}_{\tau}=\tau^{{\cal C}}
for every
\tau\in{\bf Elem}
.

We use induction to show that
\forall k\in\mathbb{N}.\forall\sigma\in V^{\prime\prime}.T_{\sigma,k}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)=T_{\sigma,k}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}\widehat{B})

(In other words: the elements of
B
and
\widehat{B}
do not occur in any of the terms of
T_{\sigma,k}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)
or
T_{\sigma,k}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}\widehat{B})
for every
\sigma\in V^{\prime\prime}
).

1.

Base case:

k=0
. Then

T_{\sigma,0}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)=\emptyset=T_{\sigma,0}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}\widehat{B}),

since
\sigma\in V^{\prime\prime}\subseteq{\bf Struct}
.

2.

Step:
Assume

T_{\tau,k}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)=T_{\tau,k}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}\widehat{B})

for every
\tau\in V^{\prime\prime}
. Then, let
\sigma\in V^{\prime\prime}
:

T_{\sigma,k+1}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)=T_{\sigma,k}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)\cup\bigl\{\,c(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})\;\bigm|\;c:\tau_{1}\times\cdots\times\tau_{m}\to\sigma\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{n+1}},\;t_{i}\in T_{\tau_{i},k}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)\,\bigr\}.

Since
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{m}\in V^{\prime\prime}
, by the IH we have
T_{\tau_{i},k}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)=T_{\tau_{i},k}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}\widehat{B})
for every
i\in[1,m]
.
Thus,

T_{\sigma,k+1}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)=T_{\sigma,k+1}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}\widehat{B}).

Thus,
\sigma^{\cal C}=T_{\sigma}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}\widehat{B})=\cup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}T_{\sigma,i}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}\widehat{B})=\cup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}T_{\sigma,i}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)=T_{\sigma}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)=\sigma^{\cal A}
.

Step 6. Final contradiction.

Since for every
T_{n+1}
-interpretation
{\cal C}
,
E_{i_{1}}^{\cal C}
=
E_{i_{1}}^{\cal A}
and
E_{i_{1}}^{\cal A}
is finite, we get that
T_{n+1}
is not stably infinite over
E_{i_{1}}
even though
E_{i_{1}}\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cap{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{i_{1}}}\subseteq{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cap\big({\cal S}_{\Sigma_{1}}\cup\ldots\cup{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n}}\big)
contradicting the assumption in
Theorem

B.60
.

B.5
Defining the Interpretation of Variables

We now define the interpretation of variables in
{\cal A}
.
For that, we first introduce several helper definitions.

S
(
Definition

B.87
) collects the interpretations of variables in
{\cal B}
and closes
it under the sub-term relation.
Then, we define functions
{\mathbf{tl}}
(
Definition

B.93
) that restricts the domains of constant arrays, and use it
to construct, for each
1\leq i\leq n
, a function
\mathbf{tail}_{i}
over subsets of
S
(
Lemma

B.94
).
Finally, these are used to map
S
to elements in the domains of
{\cal A}
(
Definition

B.101
), which
allows us to assign a value to each variable (
Definition

B.107
).

We start with the definition of
S
.

Definition B.87
.

For each
i
, let
S_{i}
be defined as follows.

\displaystyle S_{0}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\,x^{\cal B}\;\bigm|\;x\in{\it vars}_{{\sigma^{*}}}({\bf{trFormula}({{\phi}})}),\;\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}\bigr\},

\displaystyle S_{i+1}

\displaystyle=S_{i}\;\cup\;\Bigl\{\,t_{j}\;\Bigm|\;\begin{aligned} c\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}},c(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})\in S_{i},j\in[1,k]\\
\end{aligned}\Bigr\}

Then,

S=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}S_{i}
.

Example B.88
.

Consider the interpretation
{\cal B}_{1}
in
Table

21
and set
{{\phi}}
to be
{{\psi}}
from
Table

12
.
Then the set
S_{0}
consists of the following
elements:

{{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}(3,{c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}}))
,

{c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}(3,{c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}})))
,
1
and

{{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}(1,{c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}(3,{c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}}))))
.
Then the set
S_{1}
adds

3
,

{c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}})
and

\gamma

to the elements of
S_{0}
.
The set
S=S_{2}
is obtained
by adding
{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}}
.

Lemma B.89
.

S
is finite.

Proof B.90
.

First, we use induction to show that
\forall i\in\mathbb{N}.S_{i}
is finite.

•

Base case:
For
i=0
,
S_{0}
is finite by definition, as it contains only interpretations of free variables in
\bf{trFormula}({{\phi}})
, which are finitely many.

•

Inductive step:
Assume
S_{i}
is finite. We show that
S_{i+1}
is finite.
By definition,
S_{i+1}=S_{i}\cup\{t_{j}\mid c:\sigma_{1}\times\cdots\times\sigma_{k}\to\sigma\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}},c(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\in S_{i},j\in[1,k]\}
.
Since
S_{i}
is finite, the set
\{t_{j}\mid c:\sigma_{1}\times\cdots\times\sigma_{k}\to\sigma\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}},c(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\in S_{i},j\in[1,k]\}
is also finite, as it contains only finitely many elements for each term in
S_{i}
.

Now, we show that there is some
\hat{l}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
S_{\hat{l}}=S
and hence
S
is finite.

We define
\hat{l}=\max\{\text{dtIdx}(t)\mid t\in S_{0},t\in\sigma^{\cal B},\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}\}
.

We use induction on
i\in\{0,\ldots,\hat{l}-1\}
to show that
\forall s\in S_{i+1}\setminus S_{i}.\text{dtIdx}(s)\leq\hat{l}-i-1
.

Base case
i=0
.

We want to show that
\forall s\in S_{1}\setminus S_{0}.\text{dtIdx}(s)\leq\hat{l}-1
.

Since
s\in S_{1}\setminus S_{0}
, there is some
x\in S_{0}
such that
x=c(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})
for some constructor
c:\sigma_{1}\times\cdots\times\sigma_{k}\to\sigma
and
s=x_{j}
for some
j\in[1,k]
.
Thus, according to
Lemma

B.81
,
\hat{l}\geq\text{dtIdx}(x)=1+\max\{\text{dtIdx}(x_{1}),\ldots,\text{dtIdx}(x_{k})\}\geq 1+\text{dtIdx}(s)
, and therefore
\text{dtIdx}(s)\leq\hat{l}-1
.

Inductive step.

Assume the claim holds for
i
, we now show that it holds for
i+1
.
Let
t\in S_{i+2}\setminus S_{i+1}
Then there is some
x\in S_{i+1}
such that
x=c(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})
for some constructor
c:\sigma_{1}\times\cdots\times\sigma_{k}\to\sigma
and
t=x_{j}
for some
j\in[1,k]
.
Assume towards a contradiction that
x\in S_{i}
, then
t\in S_{i+1}
, in contradiction.

Hence,
x\in S_{i+1}\setminus S_{i}
, and by the inductive hypothesis,
\text{dtIdx}(x)\leq\hat{l}-i-1
.
Due to
Lemma

B.81
,
\text{dtIdx}(t)+1\leq\text{dtIdx}(x)
, thus
\text{dtIdx}(t)\leq\hat{l}-(i+1)-1
.

Let there be some
s\in S_{\hat{l}}\setminus S_{\hat{l}-1}
.
By the above,
\text{dtIdx}(s)\leq\hat{l}-\hat{l}=0
.
By definition of
\text{dtIdx}(\cdot)
,
s\in T_{\sigma,0}({\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}}},B)
for some
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}
.
Since
T_{\sigma,0}({\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}}},B)\neq\emptyset
, we know that
\sigma\in{\bf Elem}_{\Sigma_{\text{DT}}}
.

That implies that no new elements can be added in
S_{\hat{l}+1}
, hence
S_{\hat{l}+1}=S_{\hat{l}}
, and therefore
S_{\hat{l}}=S
.

We partition the elements of
S\cap\bigcup_{i\in[1,n]}{A_{i}^{*}}^{\cal B}
into two disjoint sets: in one, we collect those elements that are the interpretations
of array variables that occur in store literals, and in the other,
the remaining elements reside.

Definition B.91
.

The set
\text{SE}_{{{\phi}}}({{\cal B}})
consists of the elements
s\in S\cap\bigcup_{i\in[1,n]}{A_{i}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}
for which there exist some
a,b,i,x\in{\it vars}({{{\phi}}})

such that either

s={a^{*}}^{\cal B}
or
s={b^{*}}^{{\cal B}}
,
and

a=\textbf{store}_{A_{j}}(b,i,x)
is a literal
of
{{\phi}}
.

The set

\text{NSE}_{{{\phi}}}({{\cal B}})

is its complement, that is,

\text{NSE}_{{{\phi}}}({{\cal B}})=\big(S\cap\bigcup_{i\in[1,n]}{A_{i}^{*}}^{\cal B}\big)\setminus\text{SE}_{{{\phi}}}({{\cal B}})
.

Note that by definition,
\text{SE}_{{{\phi}}}({{\cal B}})\cup\text{NSE}_{{{\phi}}}({{\cal B}})=S\cap\bigcup_{i\in[1,n]}{A_{i}^{*}}^{\cal B}
and
\text{SE}_{{{\phi}}}({{\cal B}})\cap\text{NSE}_{{{\phi}}}({{\cal B}})=\emptyset
.

Example B.92
.

Consider the formula
{{\psi}}
from
Table

12
, and its translation
\bf{trFormula}({{\psi}})
, also from
Table

12
.
Consider as well the interpretation
{\cal B}
in
Table

18
.
We have a single array sort,
A
. Thus:

S\cap{A^{*}}^{\cal B}=\{{c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}}),{c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}(3,{c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}})))\}
.

Moreover,
\text{SE}_{{{\psi}}}({{\cal B}})=\emptyset
and
\text{NSE}_{{{\psi}}}({{\cal B}})=S\cap{A^{*}}^{\cal B}
.

We shall make use of the following function
{\mathbf{tl}}
.
For each almost constant function
a\in A_{i}^{\prime{\cal B}}
,
it removes from its domain the interpretations of variables
of sort
I_{i}
that correspond to occurrences under a select or store operation in
\phi
, that is, we remove the interpretations of

{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}
.
Formally:

Definition B.93
.

Let
a\in A_{i}^{\prime{\cal B}}
for some
i\in[1,n]
.
Then we define:

{\mathbf{tl}}(a)=a|_{{I_{i}^{*}}^{\cal B}\setminus\{{j^{*}}^{\cal B}\mid j\in{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}\}}
.

We then use
{\mathbf{tl}}
in order to prove the existence
of another function
\mathbf{tail}_{i}
, for each
1\leq i\leq n
.
This function takes an object
s
from
the datatype

{A_{i}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}
, such that

a
is also in
S
,
and returns some element in
E_{i}^{\cal A}
.

Recall that
{A_{i}^{*}}
is a datatype sort
that is intended to model the original array sort

A_{i}
.

The element returned by
\mathbf{tail}_{i}
belongs
to the interpretation of the corresponding element sort
E_{i}

in
{\cal A}
.

We require that
{\mathbf{tl}}
assigns two objects

s_{1}
and
s_{2}
to the same element, if and only if
the following two conditions are met:
first,
both
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
are in

\text{SE}_{{{\phi}}}({{\cal B}})
, that is,
they are interpretations of variables that occur in
a literal with an array store operation;
and second: recall that

f_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}:{A_{i}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}\to A_{i}^{\prime{\cal B}}
,
and thus it assigns
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
to two almost constant
functions (see
Section

B.1
).
These functions must agree on the restriction given by
{\mathbf{tl}}
.

The purpose of
\mathbf{tail}_{i}
is to ensure that if an array is obtained from another array
by a single store operation in the formula, then they will have the same
{\mathbf{tl}}
.

Lemma B.94
.

For every
i\in[1,n]
, there exists a function
\mathbf{tail}_{i}:S\cap{A_{i}^{*}}^{\cal B}\to E_{i}^{\cal A}
, such that for every
s_{1},s_{2}\in S\cap{A_{i}^{*}}^{\cal B}
:

\mathbf{tail}_{i}(s_{1})=\mathbf{tail}_{i}(s_{2})\Leftrightarrow\big(s_{1},s_{2}\in\text{SE}_{{{\phi}}}({{\cal B}})\wedge{\mathbf{tl}}({f_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}}(s_{1}))={\mathbf{tl}}({f_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}}(s_{2}))\big)

Proof B.95
.

Define a relation
R
on
S\cap{A_{i}^{*}}^{\cal B}
by

R=\{(s_{1},s_{2})\mid s_{1},s_{2}\in\text{SE}_{{{\phi}}}({{\cal B}}),\ {\mathbf{tl}}(f_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}(s_{1}))={\mathbf{tl}}(f_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}(s_{2}))\}\cup\{(s,s)\mid s\in S\cap{A_{i}^{*}}^{\cal B}\}.

We check that
R
is an equivalence relation:

1.

Reflexivity:
For every
s\in S\cap{A_{i}^{*}}^{\cal B}
,
(s,s)\in R
by definition.

2.

Symmetry:
Assume
(s_{1},s_{2})\in R
. If
s_{1}=s_{2}
, then
(s_{2},s_{1})=(s_{1},s_{2})\in R
. Otherwise,
s_{1},s_{2}\in\text{SE}_{{{\phi}}}({{\cal B}})
and
{\mathbf{tl}}({f_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}}(s_{1}))={\mathbf{tl}}({f_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}}(s_{2}))
. By symmetry of equality,
{\mathbf{tl}}({f_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}}(s_{2}))={\mathbf{tl}}({f_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}}(s_{1}))
, hence
(s_{2},s_{1})\in R
.

3.

Transitivity:
Assume
(s_{1},s_{2}),(s_{2},s_{3})\in R
. If
s_{1}=s_{2}
or
s_{2}=s_{3}
, then by substitution we obtain
(s_{1},s_{3})\in R
. Otherwise,
s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\in\text{SE}_{{{\phi}}}({{\cal B}})
and
{\mathbf{tl}}({f_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}}(s_{1}))={\mathbf{tl}}({f_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}}(s_{2}))
and
{\mathbf{tl}}({f_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}}(s_{2}))={\mathbf{tl}}({f_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}}(s_{3}))
. By transitivity of equality,
{\mathbf{tl}}({f_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}}(s_{1}))={\mathbf{tl}}({f_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}}(s_{3}))
, hence
(s_{1},s_{3})\in R
.

Thus
R
partitions
S\cap{A_{i}^{*}}^{\cal B}
into equivalence classes.
Since this set is finite (
Lemma

B.89
) and
E_{i}^{\cal A}
is infinite (
Lemma

B.85
), there exists an injection
f_{\text{class}}
from the classes of
R
into
E_{i}^{\cal A}
.

For
s\in S\cap{A_{i}^{*}}^{\cal B}
, let
[s]_{R}
be its equivalence class.
Define

\mathbf{tail}_{i}(s):=f_{\text{class}}([s]_{R}).

This satisfies the required property.

Example B.96
.

Consider the formula
{{\psi}}
from
Table

12
, and its translation
\bf{trFormula}({{\psi}})
, also from
Table

12
.
Consider as well the interpretation
{\cal B}_{1}
in
Table

21
.
As mentioned in
Example

B.92
,
\text{SE}_{{{\psi}}}({{\cal B}})=\emptyset
and
\text{NSE}_{{{\psi}}}({{\cal B}})=S\cap{A^{*}}^{\cal B}=\{c_{1}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}}),c_{1}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}(3,c_{1}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}})))\}
.
Thus,
\mathbf{tail}_{1}
can be any injection from
S\cap{A^{*}}^{\cal B}
to
{E^{\cal A}}
.
For example, we can define it as follows:

•

\mathbf{tail}_{1}(c_{1}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}}))={\mathbf{emp}}
.

•

\mathbf{tail}_{1}(c_{1}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}(3,c_{1}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}}))))={\mathbf{c}}(3,\textit{const}({{\mathbf{emp}}}))
.

Definition B.97
.

For each
i\in[1,n]
and
s\in S\cap{A_{i}^{*}}^{\cal B}
,
define the set of associated indices as

\displaystyle\mathit{AssocInd}(s,i)

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\,j\in{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}\;\bigm|\;\exists a\in{\it vars}_{A_{i}}({{{\phi}}}).\;\exists x\in{\it vars}_{{E_{i}^{*}}}({\bf{trFormula}({{\phi}})}).\;{a^{*}}^{{\cal B}}=s\land{}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad x=\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}(a^{\prime},{j^{*}})\in\bf{trFormula}({{\phi}})\bigr\}.

In the following lemma we clarify the connection between
{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}

and
\mathit{AssocInd}(s,i)
.

Lemma B.98
.

For every
i\in[1,n]
it holds that
{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}=\bigcup_{s\in S\cap{A_{i}^{*}}^{\cal B}}\mathit{AssocInd}(s,i)
.

Proof B.99
.

(
\subseteq
):
Let
j\in{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}
.
Then there exists some
a\in{\it vars}_{A_{i}}({{{\phi}}})
such that one of the following holds:

•

There exists some
x\in{\it vars}_{{E_{i}^{*}}}({\bf{trFormula}({{\phi}})})
such that
x=\textbf{select}_{A_{i}}(a,j)\in{{\phi}}
.
In this case,
\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}(a^{\prime},{j^{*}})={x^{*}}\in\bf{trFormula}({{\phi}})
.

•

There exists some
x\in{\it vars}_{{E_{i}^{*}}}({\bf{trFormula}({{\phi}})})
and
b\in{\it vars}_{A_{i}}({{{\phi}}})
such that
b=\textbf{store}_{A_{i}}(a,j,x)\in{{\phi}}
.
In this case, according to
\mathbf{L_{2}}({{{\phi}}})
,
u_{(a,o_{(A_{j},{{\phi}})}(i))}=\textbf{select}_{A_{j}^{\prime}}(a^{\prime},{i^{*}})\in\bf{trFormula}({{\phi}})
.

Hence, in both cases,
j\in\mathit{AssocInd}({a^{*}}^{\cal B},i)
, and thus
j\in\bigcup_{s\in S\cap{A_{i}^{*}}^{\cal B}}\mathit{AssocInd}(s,i)
.

\supseteq
:
Let there be
j\in\bigcup_{s\in S\cap{A_{i}^{*}}^{\cal B}}\mathit{AssocInd}(s,i)
.
Then there exists some
s\in S\cap{A_{i}^{*}}^{\cal B}
such that
j\in\mathit{AssocInd}(s,i)
.
By definition of
\mathit{AssocInd}(s,i)
, there exists some
a\in{\it vars}_{A_{i}}({{{\phi}}})
such that
{a^{*}}^{\cal B}=s
and there exists some
x\in{\it vars}_{{E_{i}^{*}}}({\bf{trFormula}({{\phi}})})
such that
x=\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}(a^{\prime},{j^{*}})\in\bf{trFormula}({{\phi}})
.

There are three cases to consider:

•

x=\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}(a^{\prime},{j^{*}})\in\bf{trFormula}({{\phi}})
because it is a translation of a literal of the form
x=\textbf{select}_{A_{i}}(a,j)\in{{\phi}}
. Note, that in that case, there exists some
y\in{\it vars}_{E_{i}}({{{\phi}}})
such that
y=\textbf{select}_{A_{i}}(a,j)\in{{\phi}}
. Therefore,
j\in{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}
.

j\in{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}
.

•

x=\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}(a^{\prime},{j^{*}})\in\bf{trFormula}({{\phi}})
because of
\mathbf{L_{1}}({{{\phi}}})
. Note, that in that case, there exists some
y\in{\it vars}_{E_{i}}({{{\phi}}})
such that
y=\textbf{select}_{A_{i}}(a,j)\in{{\phi}}
. Therefore,
j\in{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}
.

•

x=\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}(a^{\prime},{j^{*}})\in\bf{trFormula}({{\phi}})
because of
\mathbf{L_{2}}({{{\phi}}})
. Note, that in that case, there exists some
b,c\in{\it vars}_{A_{i}}({{{\phi}}})
and some
y\in{\it vars}_{E_{i}}({{{\phi}}})
such that
b=\textbf{store}_{A_{i}}(c,j,y)\in{{\phi}}
. Therefore,
j\in{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}
.

Hence, in all cases,
j\in{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}
.

Example B.100
.

Consider the formula
{{\psi}}
from
Table

12
, and its translation
\bf{trFormula}({{\psi}})
, also from
Table

12
.
Consider as well the interpretation
{\cal B}_{1}
in
Table

21
.
Thus:

•

\mathit{AssocInd}((c_{1}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}})),1)=\emptyset
.

•

\mathit{AssocInd}((c_{1}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}(3,c_{1}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}})))),1)=\{j\}
.

Finally, we define a function
\Psi
,
whose goal is to map elements of
S

(and in particular, interpretations of variables
that appear in
{{\phi}}
)
to elements in the domains of
{\cal A}
.
For technical reasons, we first define it as
a partial function, but later
(
Lemma

B.103
) prove that it is total;
and, we define its range to be a superset of
the domains of
{\cal A}
.
Later on we show that for every
x\in{\it vars}_{\sigma}({{{\phi}}})
, it hods that
\Psi({x^{*}}^{\cal B})\in\sigma^{\cal A}
(also in
Lemma

B.103
).
The definition relies on notation

o
from
Definition

28
.

Definition B.101
.

We define the partial function
\Psi\colon S\to\cup\{\sigma^{\cal A}\mid\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}\}\cup\big(\bigcup_{i\in[1,n]}{{A_{i}}^{\circ}}^{\cal B}\big)
as follows:

1.

If
x\in\sigma^{\cal B}
for some
\sigma\notin{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}}

and
x\in\cup\{\sigma^{\cal A}\mid\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}\}\cup\big(\bigcup_{i\in[1,n]}{{A_{i}}^{\circ}}^{\cal B}\big)
, then
\Psi(x)=x
.

2.

If
x={c^{*}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})
for some
c\colon\sigma_{1}\times\cdots\times\sigma_{k}\to\sigma\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
,

\Psi
is defined for
t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}
,
and
\forall i\in[1,k].\Psi(t_{i})\in\sigma_{i}^{\cal A}

then

\Psi(x)=c(\Psi(t_{1}),\ldots,\Psi(t_{k}))
.

3.

If
x=c_{i}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})
for some
i\in[1,n]
,
\Psi
is defined over
t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}
and

\forall j\in{2,\ldots,k}.\Psi(t_{j})\in E_{i}^{\cal A}
,
then we define
\Psi(x)
as follows:

\forall j\in I_{i}^{\cal A}.\Psi(x)(j)=\begin{cases}\Psi(t_{\min\{o(k)\mid k\in\mathit{AssocInd}(x,i).{k^{*}}^{\cal B}=j\}+1})&\text{if }\exists k\in\mathit{AssocInd}(x,i).{k^{*}}^{\cal B}=j\\
\mathbf{tail}_{i}(x)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Example B.102
.

Consider the formula
{{\psi}}
and its translation in
Table

12
and
{\cal B}_{1}
as defined in
Table

21
.

The set
S
is given in
Example

B.88
, and is repeated here:

S=\{{{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}(3,{c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}})),{c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}(3,{c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}}))),{{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}(1,{c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}(3,{c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}})))),3,{c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}}),\gamma,1,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}}\}

Since the computation of
\Psi
is done recursively, we order the elements of
S
according to
\text{dtIdx}(\cdot)
.

Note that we use the function
\mathbf{tail}_{1}
defined in
Example

B.96
to compute
\Psi
for the elements in
S\cap{A^{*}}^{\cal B}
.
First, we note that
{{A}^{\circ}},{I^{*}}\notin{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma^{{\psi}}_{n+1}}
.
Thus, for every
x\in S
such that
x\in{{{A}^{\circ}}}^{\cal B}
or
x\in I^{\cal B}
, we have
\Psi(x)=x
.

•

\Psi(3)=3
(Note that
3\in I^{\cal A}
)

•

\Psi(\gamma)=\gamma

•

\Psi(1)=1

Since
{\mathbf{emp}}\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{2}}
, we have
\Psi({{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}})={\mathbf{emp}}
.

Note that by
Definition

19
,
{\mathbb{I}}_{(A,{{\psi}})}=\{j\}
,
{j^{*}}^{\cal B}=1
and
o(j)=1
.
Thus,
\min\{o(k)\mid k\in{\mathbb{I}}_{(A,{{\psi}})},{k^{*}}^{\cal B}=1\}=1
.
Hence, according to the definition of
\mathbf{tail}_{1}
in
Example

B.96
we have:

\Psi({c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}}))(i)=\begin{cases}\Psi({{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}})&\text{if }i=1\\
{\mathbf{emp}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

So
\Psi({c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}}))=\textit{const}({{\mathbf{emp}}})
.

Once again, since
{\mathbf{c}}\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{2}}
, we have:

\Psi({{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}(3,{c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}})))
:

\Psi({{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}(3,{c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}})))={\mathbf{c}}(\Psi(3),\Psi({c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}})))={\mathbf{c}}(3,\textit{const}({{\mathbf{emp}}}))

Similarly, we have:

\Psi({c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}(3,{c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}}))))(i)=\begin{cases}\Psi({{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}(3,{c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}})))&\text{if }i=1\\
{\mathbf{c}}(3,\textit{const}({{\mathbf{emp}}}))&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

So
\Psi({c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}(3,{c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}}))))=\textit{const}({{\mathbf{c}}(3,\textit{const}({{\mathbf{emp}}}))})
.

Finally, since
{\mathbf{c}}\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{2}}
, we have

\displaystyle\Psi({{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}(1,{c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}(3,{c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}})))))

\displaystyle={\mathbf{c}}(\Psi(1),\Psi({c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}(3,{c_{1}}(\gamma,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}})))))

\displaystyle={\mathbf{c}}(1,\textit{const}({{\mathbf{c}}(3,\textit{const}({{\mathbf{emp}}}))}))

Lemma B.103
.

The function
\Psi:S\to\cup\{\sigma^{\cal A}\mid\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}\}\cup\big(\bigcup_{i\in[1,n]}{{A_{i}}^{\circ}}^{\cal B}\big)
is total and
\forall\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}.\forall x\in S\cap{\sigma^{*}}^{\cal B}.\Psi(x)\in\sigma^{\cal A}
.

Proof B.104
.

First note that for every
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}}\setminus{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}}
,
and every
x\in\sigma^{\cal B}
, there are 2 options:

1.

\sigma={{A_{i}}^{\circ}}
for some
i\in[1,n]
. In that case
{{A_{i}}^{\circ}}^{\cal B}\subset\cup\{\sigma^{\cal A}\mid\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}\}\cup\big(\bigcup_{i\in[1,n]}{{A_{i}}^{\circ}}^{\cal B}\big)
, and thus
\Psi(x)=x
.

2.

\sigma={\tau^{*}}
for some
\tau\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}\setminus({\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma})
. In that case,
\sigma^{\cal B}=\tau^{\cal A}
, and thus, according to the first case in
Definition

B.101
,
\Psi(x)=x\in\tau^{\cal A}
.

We are left to prove the claim for the interpretations of the sorts of
{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}
. We prove the claim by induction on
\text{dtIdx}(x)
.

Base case
\text{dtIdx}(x)=0
.

Since
\forall\sigma\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}.T_{\sigma,0}({\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}}},B)=\emptyset
, the claim holds vacuously for all
x
such that
\text{dtIdx}(x)=0
.

Inductive step.

Assume the claim holds for all
x
such that
\text{dtIdx}(x)\leq k
(whether its sort is in
{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}
or not). We show that it holds for
\text{dtIdx}(x)=k+1
. Let
x\in S
such that
\text{dtIdx}(x)=k+1
.
Then there are two cases:

•

x={c^{*}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})
for some
c:\sigma_{1}\times\cdots\times\sigma_{k}\to\sigma\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
.
Note that since
\text{dtIdx}(x)=k+1
,

Lemma

B.81
implies that
\text{dtIdx}(t_{i})\leq k
for all
i\in[1,m]
. Thus,
\forall i\in[1,m].\Psi(t_{i})
is
defined by the inductive hypothesis, and also
\Psi(t_{i})\in\sigma_{i}^{\cal A}
for all
i\in[1,m]
.
Thus,
\Psi(x)=c(\Psi(t_{1}),\ldots,\Psi(t_{m}))\in\sigma^{\cal A}
.

•

x=c_{i}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})
for some
i\in[1,n]
.
Note that since
\text{dtIdx}(x)=k+1
,
Lemma

B.81
suggests that
\text{dtIdx}(t_{j})\leq k
for all
j\in[0,k]
. Thus
\forall j\in[0,k].\Psi(t_{j})
is defined and
\forall j\in[2,k].\Psi(t_{j})\in E_{i}^{\cal A}
.
Hence, according to the third case in the
Definition

B.101
,
\Psi(x)
is defined by the inductive hypothesis, and
\Psi(x)\in\text{AC}({I_{i}^{\cal A}},{E_{i}^{\cal A}})=\sigma^{\cal A}
(the equality holds by
Lemma

B.72
).

Lemma B.105
.

The function
\Psi
is injective.

Proof B.106
.

Let there be
x,y\in S
such that
x\neq y
.
If
x\in\sigma_{1}^{\cal B}
and
y\in\sigma_{2}^{\cal B}
where
\sigma_{1}\neq\sigma_{2}
, there are two options:

•

WLOG, if
\sigma_{1}\notin{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}}
, then
\Psi(x)=x
. Thus the only way to have
\Psi(x)=\Psi(y)
is if
x=y
, contradicting our assumption.

•

If
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}}
, then we can denote
\sigma_{1}={\tau_{1}^{*}}
and
\sigma_{2}={\tau_{2}^{*}}
for some
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
.
Since
\Psi(x)\in\tau_{1}^{\cal A}
and
\Psi(y)\in\tau_{2}^{\cal A}
, we have
\Psi(x)\neq\Psi(y)
.

Hence, we can assume
x,y\in\sigma^{\cal B}
for some
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}}
.

If
\sigma\notin{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}}
, then
\Psi(x)=x\neq y=\Psi(y)
.

We are left to prove the claim where
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}}
.

We prove our claim by induction on the
l=\max\{\text{dtIdx}(x),\text{dtIdx}(y)\}
.

Base Case
: If
\text{dtIdx}(x)=\text{dtIdx}(y)=0
, since
\sigma^{\cal A}=\emptyset
, the claim holds vacuously.

Inductive Case
: Assume the claim holds for all
x,y\in S\cap\tau^{\cal B}

such that
\max\{\text{dtIdx}(x),\text{dtIdx}(y)\}<k
for every
\tau\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}
. We show that it holds for
\max\{\text{dtIdx}(x),\text{dtIdx}(y)\}=k
.
There are 2 cases to consider:

•

If
x,y\in{\sigma^{*}}^{\cal B}
where
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}
, there are two subcases:

1.

If
x={c^{*}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})
and
y={c^{*}}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{m})
for some
c:\sigma_{1}\times\cdots\times\sigma_{k}\to\sigma\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
, then by definition there exists some
j\in[1,k]
such that
t_{j}\neq s_{j}
. According to the IH,
\Psi(t_{j})\neq\Psi(s_{j})
, and thus

\Psi(x)=c(\Psi(t_{1}),\ldots,\Psi(t_{m}))\neq c(\Psi(s_{1}),\ldots,\Psi(s_{m}))=\Psi(y).

2.

If
x={\hat{c}^{*}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})
and
y={\tilde{c}^{*}}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{l})
, then

\Psi(x)=\hat{c}(\Psi(t_{1}),\ldots,\Psi(t_{m}))\neq\tilde{c}(\Psi(s_{1}),\ldots,\Psi(s_{l}))=\Psi(y),

since
\hat{c}\neq\tilde{c}
.

•

If
x,y\in{A_{i}^{*}}^{\cal B}
for some
i\in[1,n]
, then we can denote
x=c_{i}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})
and
y=c_{i}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{l})
.
There are 2 subcases:

1.

If

\neg\big(x,y\in\text{SE}_{{{\phi}}}({{\cal B}})\wedge{\mathbf{tl}}({f_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}}(x))={\mathbf{tl}}({f_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}}(y))\big)

according to
Lemma

B.94
,
\mathbf{tail}_{i}(x)\neq\mathbf{tail}_{i}(y)
. Hence, for every
k\in{I_{i}^{*}}\setminus\{{l^{*}}^{\cal B}\mid l\in{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}\}
we have that
\Psi(x)(k)=\mathbf{tail}_{i}(x)\neq\mathbf{tail}_{i}(y)=\Psi(y)
.

2.

Otherwise, we have that
\big(x,y\in\text{SE}_{{{\phi}}}({{\cal B}})\wedge{\mathbf{tl}}({f_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}}(x))={\mathbf{tl}}({f_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}}(y))\big)
.

Since
x,y\in\text{SE}_{{{\phi}}}({{\cal B}})
there are
a,b\in{\it vars}_{A_{i}}({{{\phi}}})
with
{a^{*}}^{\cal B}=x
and
{b^{*}}^{\cal B}=y
.

{\cal B}
satisfies
\mathbf{L_{3}}({{{\phi}}})
:

–

{a^{*}}=g_{A_{i}}(a^{\prime})
,

–

{b^{*}}=g_{A_{i}}(b^{\prime})
,

–

a^{\prime}=f_{A_{i}}({a^{*}})
,

–

b^{\prime}=f_{A_{i}}({b^{*}})
.

Thus, we have
a^{\prime{\cal B}}=f_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}(x)
and
b^{\prime{\cal B}}=f_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}(y)
.

Suppose towards contradiction that
a^{\prime{\cal B}}=b^{\prime{\cal B}}
.

Using the first 2 equalities, we have:

x={a^{*}}^{\cal B}=g_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}(a^{\prime{\cal B}})=g_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}(b^{\prime{\cal B}})={b^{*}}^{\cal B}=y,

contradicting
x\neq y
.

Thus,
a^{\prime{\cal B}}\neq b^{\prime{\cal B}}
, meaning there exists some
j\in{I_{i}^{*}}^{\cal B}
such that

a^{\prime{\cal B}}(j)\neq b^{\prime{\cal B}}(j)
. Since
{\mathbf{tl}}({f_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}}(x))={\mathbf{tl}}({f_{A_{i}}^{\cal B}}(y))
, there exists some
k\in{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}
such that
{k^{*}}^{\cal B}=j
.

WLOG, for every
l\in{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}
with
{l^{*}}^{\cal B}=j
we have
o_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}(k)\leq o_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}(l)
.

Note, that since
x,y\in\text{SE}_{{{\phi}}}({{\cal B}})
and according to the definition of
\mathbf{L_{2}}({{{\phi}}})
we know that
k\in\mathit{AssocInd}(x,i)
and
k\in\mathit{AssocInd}(y,i)
.

Since
x,y\in{A_{i}^{*}}^{\cal B}
, since there is a single constructor whose target sort is
{A_{i}^{*}}
, we can denote
x=c_{i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})
and
y=c_{i}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m})
.

\displaystyle\Psi(x)(j)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;\Psi\bigl(x_{o(k)+1}\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;\Psi\bigl(s_{(c_{i},o(k)+1)}(x)\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(3)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;\Psi\bigl(s_{(c_{i},o(k)+1)}({a^{*}}^{\cal B})\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(4)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;\Psi\bigl(\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}^{\cal B}(a^{\prime{\cal B}},{k^{*}}^{\cal B})\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(5)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;\Psi\bigl(\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}^{\cal B}(a^{\prime{\cal B}},j)\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(6)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;\Psi\bigl(a^{\prime{\cal B}}(j)\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(7)}}{{\neq}}

\displaystyle\;\Psi\bigl(b^{\prime{\cal B}}(j)\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(8)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;\Psi\bigl(\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}^{\cal B}(b^{\prime{\cal B}},j)\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(9)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;\Psi\bigl(\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}^{\cal B}(b^{\prime{\cal B}},{k^{*}}^{\cal B})\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(10)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;\Psi\bigl(s_{(c_{i},o(k)+1)}({b^{*}}^{\cal B})\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(11)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;\Psi\bigl(s_{(c_{i},o(k)+1)}(y)\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(12)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;\Psi(y_{o(k)+1})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(13)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;\Psi(y)(j)

and
\Psi(x)\neq\Psi(y)
.

(1) According to the first subcase in the third case in the definition of
\Psi
(See
Definition

B.101
)

(2) According to the interpretation of selectors in
{\cal B}
and since
x=c_{i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})
;

(3) Substitute
x={a^{*}}^{\cal B}
;

(4) Since
k\in{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}
and
{a^{*}}^{\cal B}=x\in\text{SE}_{{{\phi}}}({{\cal B}})
, since
{\cal B}
satisfies
\mathbf{L_{2}}({{{\phi}}})
, we have that
s_{(c_{i},o(k)+1)}({a^{*}}^{\cal B})=\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}^{\cal B}(a^{\prime{\cal B}},{k^{*}}^{\cal B})
;

(5) Substitute
{k^{*}}^{\cal B}=j
;

(6) According to the interpretation of
\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}
in
{\cal B}
;

(7) Since
a^{\prime{\cal B}}(j)\neq b^{\prime{\cal B}}(j)
, and due to the IH regarding injectivity of
\Psi
(Note that
\text{dtIdx}(x_{o(k)+1})<\text{dtIdx}(x)
and
\text{dtIdx}(y_{o(k)+1})<\text{dtIdx}(y)
by
Lemma

B.81
);

(8) - (13) Reverse of (6) - (1).

Definition B.107
.

For every
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
, and every
x\in{\it vars}_{\sigma}({{{\phi}}})
its interpretation in
{\cal A}
is defined as follows:

x^{\cal A}=\Psi({x^{*}}^{\cal B}).

Note that for every
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
, and every
x\in{\it vars}_{\sigma}({{{\phi}}})
, we have that
\Psi({x^{*}}^{\cal B})
is well defined and
\Psi({x^{*}}^{\cal B})\in\sigma^{\cal A}

by
Lemma

B.103
.

B.6
Interpretation of Functions and Predicates

Array operators

Standard read/update operations

Constructors

Fixed by the theory

Testers

Fixed by the theory

Selectors

s_{(c,i)}^{{\cal A}}(t)=\begin{cases}t_{i}&\text{if }t=c(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})\text{ for some }t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\\[4.0pt]
\Psi\!\bigl({s_{({c^{*}},i)}^{\cal B}}(x)\bigr)&\text{otherwise, if there is }x\in S\text{ with }\Psi(x)=t\\[4.0pt]
\mathbf{d}_{\sigma}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Table 23:
Interpretation of function and predicate symbols,
where
\mathbf{d}_{\sigma}
is an arbitrary (but fixed) element of

\sigma^{{\cal A}}
.
Note that the interpretation of selectors is well defined as
\Psi
is injective
(
Lemma

B.105
).

The interpretation of function and predicate symbols
in
{\cal B}
is defined in
Table

23
and
described as follows.

Array operations.

The symbols
\textbf{select}_{A_{j}}
and

\textbf{store}_{A_{j}}
are interpreted in the standard way,
as read and update operations over functions.

Datatype symbols.

The interpretation of
constructors and testers is fixed, as we require

{\cal A}^{\Sigma_{n+1}}
to be a datatypes structure.
As for selectors, their interpretation is fixed when applied
to terms that are constructed by their corresponding constructors.
When applied to different constructors, we maintain the connection
to
{\cal B}
as induced by
\Psi
, or set them
arbitrarily if no such connection is induced.

B.7
{\cal A}
is a
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-interpretation

In order to show that
{\cal A}
is a
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-interpretation, we need to show that
the relation
{\cal R}_{{\cal A}}
is well-founded.

Recall that for every sort
\sigma
,

\sigma^{\cal A}
is defined to be

\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\sigma^{i}
.
Thus, each element of
\sigma^{\cal A}
belongs
to
\sigma^{i}
for some minimal
i
.
For the next lemma, as well as for several
subsequent lemmas, it will be useful to
have a special notation for that minimal
i
.

Notation 2

For every
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
and every
x\in\sigma^{\cal A}
, we define
\text{idx}(x)=\min\{l\in\mathbb{N}\mid x\in\sigma^{l}\}
.

In order to show that
{\cal R}_{{\cal A}}
is well-founded, we prove the following lemmas about the function
\text{idx}(\cdot)
.

Lemma B.108
.

Let
x=c(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})
for some constructor
c:\sigma_{1}\times\cdots\times\sigma_{m}\to\sigma\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
and
t_{1}\in\sigma_{1}^{\cal A},\ldots,t_{m}\in\sigma_{m}^{\cal A}
. Then,

\text{idx}(x)\geq\max\{\text{idx}(t_{1}),\ldots,\text{idx}(t_{m})\}
.

Proof B.109
.

We can denote as
k=\text{idx}(x)
and
k_{j}=\text{idx}(t_{j})
for every
j\in[1,m]
.

Since
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
, we know that
\sigma^{0}=\emptyset
and hence
k\geq 1
.
By minimality of
k
, we know that
x\in\sigma^{k}\setminus\sigma^{k-1}
.
Since
\sigma^{k}=\sigma^{k-1}\cup T_{\sigma}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B^{k-1})
, we have that
x\in T_{\sigma}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B^{k-1})
. Hence, by the definition of
T_{\sigma}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B^{k-1})
, we have that for every
j\in[1,m]
,
t_{j}\in\sigma_{j}^{k-1}
. Therefore, by the definition of
\text{idx}(t_{j})
, we have that
k_{j}\leq k-1<k
for every
j\in[1,m]
, and hence
\max\{\text{idx}(t_{1}),\ldots,\text{idx}(t_{m})\}=\max\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}\}\leq k
.

Lemma B.110
.

Let
x\in A_{i}^{\cal A}
for some
i\in[1,n]
and
j\in I_{i}^{\cal A}
. Then,

\text{idx}(\textbf{select}_{A_{i}}^{\cal A}(x,j))<\text{idx}(x)
.

Proof B.111
.

We denote
k=\text{idx}(x)
.
Since
A_{i}^{0}=\emptyset
, we have that
k\geq 1
.
By the definition of the subdomains, we have
A_{i}^{k}=\text{AC}({I_{i}^{k-1}},{E_{i}^{k-1}})
. Thus
\textbf{select}_{A_{i}}^{\cal A}(x,j)\in E_{i}^{k-1}
, and hence by the definition of
\text{idx}(\cdot)
, we have that

\text{idx}(\textbf{select}_{A_{i}}^{\cal A}(x,j))\leq k-1<k
.

Lemma B.112
.

The relation
{\cal R}_{{\cal A}}
is well-founded.

Proof B.113
.

Suppose, toward contradiction, there is an infinite descending sequence

x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},\ldots

such that
\forall i\in\mathbb{N}.x_{i+1}{\cal R}_{{\cal A}}x_{i}
.
Let
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots
be a sequence of sorts such that for each
i
, we have
x_{i}\in\sigma_{i}^{\cal A}
. We denote by
k_{i}=\text{idx}(x_{i})
.
According to
Definition

16
, there are two cases for each
i
:

•

x_{i}=c(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})
for some

t_{1}\in\sigma_{1}^{k_{i}-1},\ldots,t_{n}\in\sigma_{m}^{k_{i}-1}
and
x_{i+1}=t_{j}
for some
j\in[1,m]
.

•

x_{i+1}=\textbf{select}_{\sigma_{i}}^{\cal A}(x_{i},j)
for some
j\in I_{i}^{\cal A}
.

1. If
x_{i+1}{\cal R}_{{\cal A}}x_{i}
by the first case then
k_{i+1}\leq k_{i}
:

If
x_{i+1}{\cal R}_{{\cal A}}x_{i}
by the first case, then by
Lemma

B.108
, we have that
k_{i}\geq\max\{\text{idx}(t_{1}),\ldots,\text{idx}(t_{m})\}
. Since
x_{i+1}=t_{j}
for some
j\in[1,m]
, we have that
k_{i+1}=\text{idx}(t_{j})\leq k_{i}
.

2. If
x_{i+1}{\cal R}_{{\cal A}}x_{i}
by the second case then
k_{i+1}<k_{i}
:

We can assume that
\sigma_{i}=A_{m}
for some
m\in[1,n]
.
By
Lemma

B.110
, we have that

k_{i+1}=\text{idx}(\textbf{select}_{A_{m}}^{\cal A}(x_{i},j))<\text{idx}(x_{i})=k_{i}
.

3. There are infinitely many indices
i
for which the second case holds:

Suppose there are finitely many indices
i
for which the second case holds, then there is some index
i_{0}
such that for every
i\geq i_{0}
, the first case holds.
WLOG, we can assume that for every
i\geq 0
,
x_{i+1}{\cal R}_{{\cal A}}x_{i}
by the first case (by renaming the indices).
Thus, for every
i>0
, the first case holds and
\sigma_{i}\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
. According to
Lemma

B.74
,

\sigma_{0}^{\cal A}=T_{\sigma_{0}}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)=\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}T_{\sigma_{0},j}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)
.

Therefore, there is some
j_{0}
such that
x_{0}\in T_{\sigma_{0},j_{0}}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)
.
We prove by induction on
i
that for every
i\geq 0
,
x_{i}\in T_{\sigma_{i},j_{0}-i}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)
.

Base case
i=0
.
By the choice of
j_{0}
,
x_{0}\in T_{\sigma_{0},j_{0}}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)
.

Inductive step.

Assume
x_{i}\in T_{\sigma_{i_{0}+i},j_{0}-i}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)
.
We denote by
l=\min\{k\in\mathbb{N}\mid x_{i_{0}+i}\in T_{\sigma_{i_{0}+i},k}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)\}
. It is clear that
l\leq j_{0}-i
.

Since
x_{i_{0}+i}\in T_{\sigma_{i_{0}+i},l}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)\setminus T_{\sigma_{i_{0}+i},l-1}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)
, we get that
x_{i_{0}+i}=c(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})
for some constructor
c:\sigma_{1}\times\ldots\times\sigma_{m}\to\sigma_{i_{0}+i}
and
t_{1}\in T_{\sigma_{1},l-1}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B),\ldots,t_{m}\in T_{\sigma_{m},l-1}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)
.
There is some
j\in[1,m]
such that
x_{i_{0}+i+1}=t_{j}
, and hence
x_{i_{0}+i+1}\in T_{\sigma_{j},l-1}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)
.

Note that
l-1<l\leq j_{0}-i
. Hence,
x_{i_{0}+i+1}\in T_{\sigma_{j},j_{0}-(i+1)}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)
.

Thus, for
i=j_{0}
, since
\sigma_{j_{0}}\in{\bf Struct}
we have
x_{j_{0}}\in T_{\sigma_{i},0}({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}B)=\emptyset
, which is a contradiction.

4. Contradiction:

Therefore, there are infinitely many indices
i
for which the second case holds, which contradicts the well foundedness of the natural numbers with the standard less-than relation.

We can now prove that
{\cal A}
is indeed a
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-interpretation.

Lemma B.114
.

{\cal A}
is a
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-interpretation.

Proof B.115
.

We have already seen that
{\cal R}_{{\cal A}}
is well founded in
Lemma

B.112
.

Hence, we only need to show that

•

{\cal A}^{\Sigma_{j}}
is an arrays structure for
j\in[1,n]
.

•

{\cal A}^{\Sigma_{n+1}}
is a datatype structure.

For each
j\in[1,n]
,
Lemma

B.72
shows that

A_{j}^{\cal A}
is the standard functional interpretation of arrays, and we
interpret

\textbf{select}_{A_{j}}
and
\textbf{store}_{A_{j}}
in the standard way for an arrays structure.
As for
\Sigma_{n+1}
,
Lemma

B.74
shows
that
\forall\sigma\in{\bf Struct}
,
\sigma^{\cal A}
is
interpreted in the standard way. Likewise,

{\cal A}
uses the usual tree‐based interpretation of
constructors, selectors and testers (see Definition
6
). By
construction, each selector and tester in
\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}
obeys the requirements of datatypes structures.

B.8
Satisfaction of the original formula

Lemma B.116
.

{\cal A}\models{{\phi}}
.

Proof B.117
.

Recall that we are under the assumption that

{{\phi}}
is a flat
\Sigma
-cube.
Let
\ell
be a literal of
{{\phi}}
.
We prove that
{\cal A}\models\ell
by considering
every possible shape
\ell
may take, and its corresponding translation
(given in
Table

9
).
Note that since
\ell
is a literal of
{{\phi}}
,

\bf{tr}(\ell)
is a literal of
\bf{tr}({{\phi}})
and is therefore
satisfied by
{\cal B}
.
Further, the lemmas in

\mathbf{L_{1}}({{{\phi}}})
,
\mathbf{L_{2}}({{{\phi}}})
and

\mathbf{L_{3}}({{{\phi}}})
are also satisfied by

{\cal B}

(see
Table

).

1.

For
\ell
of the form

x=y
,
we know that
{\cal B}\models{x^{*}}={y^{*}}
. Hence,

x^{\cal A}=\Psi({x^{*}}^{\cal B})=\Psi({y^{*}}^{\cal B})=y^{\cal A}
, and so
{\cal A}\models\ell
.

2.

For
\ell
of the form

x\neq y,

we know that
{\cal B}\models{x^{*}}\neq{y^{*}}
.
Hence, by the injectivity of
\Psi
(
Lemma

B.105
), we have:

x^{\cal A}=\Psi({x^{*}}^{\cal B})\neq\Psi({y^{*}}^{\cal B})=y^{\cal A}
, and so
{\cal A}\models\ell
.

3.

For
\ell
of the form

x=\textbf{select}_{A_{i}}(a,j)
,
we know that
{\cal B}\models{x^{*}}=\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}(a^{\prime},{j^{*}})
.
According to
Definition

B.97
,
j\in\mathit{AssocInd}({a^{*}}^{\cal B},i)
.
Pick some

k\in\mathit{AssocInd}({a^{*}}^{\cal B},i)
with

{k^{*}}^{\cal B}={j^{*}}^{\cal B}

which is minimal w.r.t.

o_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}
, that is,
for
every
l\in\mathit{AssocInd}({a^{*}}^{\cal B},i)
with

{l^{*}}^{\cal B}={j^{*}}^{\cal B}
we have

o_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}(k)\leq o_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}(l)
.

Notice that we must have
{a^{*}}^{{\cal B}}=c_{i}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})
for some
t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}
,
as
c_{i}
is the only constructor of output sort
{A_{i}^{*}}
.
Now:

\displaystyle\textbf{select}_{A_{i}}^{\cal A}(a^{\cal A},j^{\cal A})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;a^{\cal A}(j^{\cal A})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;\Psi({a^{*}}^{\cal B})(\Psi({j^{*}}^{\cal B}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(3)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;\Psi({a^{*}}^{\cal B})({j^{*}}^{\cal B})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(4)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;\Psi({t_{o(k)+1}^{*}}^{{\cal B}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(5)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;\Psi(s_{(c_{i},o(k)+1)}^{\cal B}({a^{*}}^{\cal B}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(6)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;\Psi(\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}^{\cal B}(a^{\prime{\cal B}},{k^{*}}^{\cal B}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(7)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;\Psi(\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}^{\cal B}(a^{\prime{\cal B}},{j^{*}}^{\cal B}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(8)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;\Psi({x^{*}}^{\cal B})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(9)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;x^{\cal A}

(1) According to the interpretation of the select operation in
{\cal A}
(see
Table

23
).

(2) According to the interpretation of
a
and
j
in
{\cal A}
(
Definition

B.107
).

(3) By the first case in the definition of
\Psi
(see

Definition

B.101
), as for every

, according to

we have that

and therefore

{I_{i}^{*}}\notin{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}
(see
Table

).

(4) Due to the first subcase in the third case in the definition of
\Psi
(see
Definition

B.101
)

(5) Since
{a^{*}}^{\cal B}=c_{i}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})
and by the interpretation of selectors in
{\cal B}
(see
Table

23
).

(6) Since
{\cal B}\models\mathbf{L_{1}}({{{\phi}}})
.

(7) Since

.

(8) It holds that
{\cal B}\models{x^{*}}=\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}(a^{\prime},{j^{*}})
.

(9) The last equality holds by the interpretation of

in
{\cal A}
(see

B.107
).

4.

For
\ell
of the form

,
we know that

.
Since
{\cal B}^{{\Sigma_{i}^{*}}}

is an arrays structure, the followings hold:

\displaystyle{\cal B}\models\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}(b^{\prime},{k^{*}})={v^{*}}

(
\ast
)

\displaystyle\forall j\in({I_{i}^{*}}^{\cal B}\setminus\{{k^{*}}^{\cal B}\}).\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}^{\cal B}(b^{\prime{\cal B}},j)=\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}^{\cal B}(a^{\prime{\cal B}},j)

(
\ast\ast

In addition, since

\displaystyle\forall j\in{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}.\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}^{\cal B}(a^{\prime{\cal B}},{j^{*}}^{\cal B})=s_{(c_{i},o(j)+1)}^{\cal B}({a^{*}}^{\cal B})

(

\displaystyle\forall j\in{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}.\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}^{\cal B}(b^{\prime{\cal B}},{j^{*}}^{\cal B})=s_{(c_{i},o(j)+1)}^{\cal B}({b^{*}}^{\cal B})

Since

Note that since there is a single constructor

whose target sort is

(1)

Finally, before showing the three cases, we observe that

(2)

We now prove the aforementioned cases one by one:

.

\displaystyle b^{\cal A}(k^{\cal A})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;\Psi({b^{*}}^{\cal B})(\Psi({k^{*}}^{\cal B}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(5)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(6)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;\Psi(\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}^{\cal B}(b^{\prime{\cal B}},{k^{*}}^{\cal B}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(7)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(9)}}{{=}}

(1) According to the interpretation of

(b)

Pick

t\in{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}
with

{t^{*}}^{\cal B}={j^{*}}^{\cal B}
