Appendix C
Decidability

In this section, we prove

Theorem

32
.
Let
\Sigma
be an NDT-signature and

{{\phi}}
a flat
\Sigma
-cube, and
recall the graph
G_{\Sigma}
from

Definition

B.76
.
We prove a lemma similar to
Lemma

B.78
,
but for
T_{\text{new}}^{{{\phi}}}
-interpretations.

Lemma C.118
.

If there is a path from
\sigma
to
\tau
in the graph
G_{\Sigma}
(see
Definition

B.76
),
then for every
T_{\text{new}}^{{{\phi}}}
-interpretation
{\cal B}
,
|{\tau^{*}}^{{\cal B}}|\leq|{\sigma^{*}}^{{\cal B}}|
.

Proof C.119
.

In this proof we assume w.l.o.g. that for every
i
in
[1,n]
, there exists a literal of the form
x=\textbf{select}_{A_{i}}(a,j)
in
{{\phi}}

(we can always add such a literal with fresh variables without changing the
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-satisfiability of
{{\phi}}
).

Write the (finite) path in
G_{\Sigma}
from
\sigma
to
\tau
as

\sigma=\rho_{0}\;\longrightarrow\;\rho_{1}\;\longrightarrow\;\dots\;\longrightarrow\;\rho_{l}=\tau\qquad(l\geq 0).

It is sufficient to show that for every
i\in[0,l-1].|{\rho_{i}^{*}}^{\cal B}|\geq|{\rho_{i+1}^{*}}^{\cal B}|
.

By the definition of the graph, the edge
(\rho_{i},\rho_{i+1})
exists due to one of the following two cases:

(1)

There is a constructor
c:\tau_{1}\,\dots\,\tau_{k}\to\rho_{i}
such that
\rho_{i+1}=\tau_{j}
for some
j
.

(2)

\rho_{i}=A_{m}
and
\rho_{i+1}=E_{m}
for some
m\in[1,n]
.

In both cases, there exists an injective function

f_{i}:{\rho_{i+1}^{*}}^{\cal B}\to{\rho_{i}^{*}}^{\cal B}
:

1.

In case (1), we fix for every
l\in[1,k]\setminus\{j\}
some
u_{l}\in{\tau_{l}^{*}}^{\cal B}

and define:

f_{i}(x)={c^{*}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{j-1},x,u_{j+1},\dots,u_{k}).

2.

In case (2), we fix
y\in{{{A_{m}}^{\circ}}}^{\cal B}
and define:

f_{i}(x)=c_{m}(y,x,\ldots,x).

(Since by our assumption at the beginning of the proof, there is at least one literal of the form
x=\textbf{select}_{A_{i}}(a,j)
in
{{\phi}}
,
n_{(A_{i},{{\phi}})}>0
and so the constructor
c_{m}
has at least one argument of the element sort
{E_{m}^{*}}
.)

Next, we prove a lemma that is similar to
Lemma

B.83
.

Lemma C.120
.

Let
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
be
eventually cyclic
(see
Definition

B.76
).
For every
T_{\text{new}}^{{{\phi}}}
-interpretation
{\cal B}
,

|{\sigma^{*}}^{{\cal B}}|=\infty
.

Proof C.121
.

As before, we may assume without loss of generality that
{{\phi}}
contains a literal of the form
x=\textbf{select}_{A_{i}}(a,j)
for each
i
in
[1,n]
.

Since
\sigma
is eventually cyclic, there exists in particular a sort
\hat{\tau}
such that the edge
(\sigma,\hat{\tau})
is in
G_{\Sigma}
.
By
Definition

B.76
, this implies
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}
, and according to
Table

6
, we have
{\sigma^{*}}\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}\subseteq{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}
.

Because
{\sigma^{*}}\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}
and
{\cal B}^{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{{\phi}}}}
is a datatypes structure, we can compute
\text{dtIdx}(\cdot)
(see
Definition

B.80
) for elements in
{\sigma^{*}}^{{\cal B}}
under the interpretation
{\cal B}^{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{{\phi}}}}
.

To prove the lemma, it suffices to show that
\forall N\in\mathbb{N}.\exists x\in{\sigma^{*}}^{{\cal B}}.\,\text{dtIdx}(x)\geq N
.

1. Obtaining a long path.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
We define a path of length at least
N
.
Because
\sigma
is eventually cyclic, there is a path

\sigma=\tau_{0}\;\to\;\tau_{1}\;\to\;\cdots\;\to\;\tau_{\ell}

ending at a node
\tau_{\ell}
that belongs to a directed cycle

\tau_{\ell}\;\to\;\tau_{\ell+1}\;\to\;\cdots\;\to\;\tau_{\ell+L}\quad(\tau_{\ell+L}=\tau_{\ell}).

Choose
t\in\mathbb{N}
so that

\ell+tL\geq N
.
Traversing the cycle
t
additional times yields a (possibly repeating)
path of length
T:=\ell+tL\geq N
:

\sigma=\tau_{0}\;\to\;\tau_{1}\;\to\;\cdots\;\to\;\tau_{T}.

For convenience, we represent this path with reverse indices as

\sigma=\sigma_{T}\;\to\;\sigma_{T-1}\;\to\;\cdots\;\to\;\sigma_{0}=\tau_{T},

where
\sigma_{i}=\tau_{{T-i}}
for each
i\in[0,T]
.

We now show that for every
i\in[0,T-1]
,
{\sigma_{i}^{*}}\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}
, which ensures that
\text{dtIdx}(\cdot)
is defined on
{\sigma_{i}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}
. (We already know that
{\sigma_{T}^{*}}={\sigma^{*}}\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}
.)

For every
i\in[0,T-1]
, the edge
(\sigma_{i+1},\sigma_{i})
occurs in the graph
G_{\Sigma}
.
By
Definition

B.76
, this edge arises from one of two cases:

•

There exists a constructor

c:\rho_{1}\times\!\cdots\!\times\rho_{k}\to\sigma_{i+1}

with
\rho_{r}=\sigma_{i}
for some
1\leq r\leq k
. In this case,
\sigma_{i}\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
, and according to
Table

6
,
{\sigma_{i}^{*}}\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}
.

•

There exists some
1\leq m\leq n
such that
\sigma_{i+1}=A_{m}
and
\sigma_{i}=E_{m}
. By our assumption at the beginning of the proof,
{{\phi}}
contains a literal
x=\textbf{select}_{A_{m}}(a,j)
. Therefore, by
Table

6
, the constructor
c_{m}:{{A_{m}}^{\circ}}\times{E_{m}^{*}}\times\cdots\times{E_{m}^{*}}\to{A_{m}^{*}}
belongs to
{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}
. Consequently,
{\sigma_{i}^{*}}={E_{m}^{*}}\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}
.

2. From a path to an element of large
\text{dtIdx}(\cdot)
.

We use induction over
i
to show that
\forall i\in[0,T].\exists x_{i}\in{\sigma_{i}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}.\text{dtIdx}(x_{i})\geq i
.
When
i=T
, we get an element
x_{T}\in{\sigma_{T}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}={\sigma^{*}}^{{\cal B}}
with
\text{dtIdx}(x_{T})\geq T\geq N
, which concludes the proof.

Base Case:
Since all domains are not empty, there exists
x_{0}\in{\sigma_{0}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}
and by definition
\text{dtIdx}(x_{0})\geq 0
.

Inductive Step:
Assume that for some
i\in[0,T-1]

there exists
x_{i}\in{\sigma_{i}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}
such that
\text{dtIdx}(x_{i})\geq i
. We show that there exists
x_{i+1}\in{\sigma_{i+1}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}

such that
\text{dtIdx}(x_{i+1})\geq i+1
.

The edge
(\sigma_{i+1},\sigma_{i})
occurs in the graph
G_{\Sigma}
because of one of two reasons:

•

Constructor edge:

there exists a constructor

c:\rho_{1}\times\!\cdots\!\times\rho_{k}\to\sigma_{i+1}

with
\rho_{r}=\sigma_{i}
for some
1\leq r\leq k
.
As
{\rho_{1}^{*}}^{\cal B},\ldots,{\rho_{k}^{*}}^{\cal B}
are all non-empty, we can choose arbitrary
u_{s}\in{\rho_{s}^{*}}^{\cal B}
for
s\in[1,k]\setminus\{r\}
.
Thus,

x_{i+1}:={c^{*}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{r-1},x_{i},u_{r+1},\dots,u_{k})\in{\sigma_{i+1}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}

\displaystyle\text{dtIdx}(x_{i+1})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;1+\max\{\text{dtIdx}(u_{1}),\ldots,\text{dtIdx}(u_{r-1}),\text{dtIdx}(x_{i}),\text{dtIdx}(u_{r+1}),\ldots,\text{dtIdx}(u_{k})\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle\;1+\text{dtIdx}(x_{i})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(3)}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle\;i+1

(1) According to
Lemma

B.81
.

(2) From the definition of
\max
.

(3) According to the IH
\text{dtIdx}(x_{i})\geq i
.

•

Array edge:

the edge
(\sigma_{i+1},\sigma_{i})
occurs
in the graph because
there exists some
1\leq m\leq n
such that
\sigma_{i+1}=A_{m}
,
\sigma_{i}=E_{m}
.
Fix some
r\in{{{A_{m}}^{\circ}}}^{\cal B}
and set

x_{i+1}:=c_{m}(r,x_{i},\dots,x_{i})\in{\sigma_{i+1}^{*}}^{{\cal B}}

\displaystyle\text{dtIdx}(x_{i+1})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\;1+\max\{\text{dtIdx}(r),\text{dtIdx}(x_{i}),\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle\;1+\text{dtIdx}(x_{i})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(3)}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle\;i+1

(1) According to
Lemma

B.81
.

(2) From the definition of
\max
.

(3) According to the IH
\text{dtIdx}(x_{i})\geq i
.

One more ingredient that is needed for the proof of

Theorem

32

is a generalization of
Theorem

34
to multiple theories, which we prove here
for the sake of completeness.
Our proof uses Theorem 2.5 of
11
(which is an extended version of
10
) and Theorem 9 of
20
(the order-sorted Löwenheim-Skolem Theorem):

Definition C.122
.

Let
V
be a set of variables and
\delta_{V}
a conjunction
of equality literals and negations of such literals (i.e., equalities and disequalities) between variables in
V
.
We say that

\delta_{V}
is an
arrangement
of
V
if there exists an equivalence relation
E

on
V
such that
\delta_{V}=\wedge\{x=y\mid(x,y)\in E\}\bigwedge\wedge\{x\neq y\mid(x,y)\notin E\}
.

Theorem C.123

(Many-sorted Löwenheim-Skolem Theorem)
.

Given a many-sorted signature
\tilde{\Sigma}
, a
\tilde{\Sigma}
-theory
\tilde{T}
, a satisfiable
\tilde{T}
-formula
\Phi
, and a
\tilde{T}
-interpretation
{\cal A}
satisfying
\Phi
, there exists a
\tilde{\Sigma}
-interpretation
{\cal B}
satisfying
\Phi
such that
|\sigma^{\cal A}|\geq\aleph_{0}
implies
|\sigma^{\cal B}|=\aleph_{0}
, for each sort
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\tilde{\Sigma}}
.

Lemma C.124

(Thm 2.5 of
11
)
.

Let
T_{i}
be a
\Sigma_{i}
-theory, for
i=1,2
, such that

{\cal F}_{\Sigma_{1}}\cap{\cal F}_{\Sigma_{2}}=\emptyset
and

{\cal P}_{\Sigma_{1}}\cap{\cal P}_{\Sigma_{2}}=\emptyset
.
Let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2}
,

T=T_{1}\oplus T_{2}
, and

S={\cal S}_{\Sigma_{1}}\cap{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{2}}
.
For
i=1,2
, let
{{\phi}}_{i}
be a flat
\Sigma_{i}
-cube, and let

V={\it vars}({{{\phi}}_{1}})\cap{\it vars}({{{\phi}}_{2}})
.
Suppose there exist a
T_{1}
-interpretation
\mathcal{A}
, a
T_{2}
-interpretation

\mathcal{B}
, and an arrangement

\delta_{V}
of
V
such that

•

\mathcal{A}\models{{\phi}}_{1}\wedge\delta_{V}
,

•

\mathcal{B}\models{{\phi}}_{2}\wedge\delta_{V}
, and

•

\lvert A_{\sigma}\rvert=\lvert B_{\sigma}\rvert
for every
\sigma\in S
.

Then there exists a
T
-interpretation
{\cal C}
such that

•

{\cal C}\models{{\phi}}_{1}\wedge{{\phi}}_{2}\wedge\delta_{V}
,

•

C_{\sigma}=A_{\sigma}
for every
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{1}}
, and

•

C_{\sigma}=B_{\sigma}
for every
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{2}}\setminus S
.

Theorem C.125

(Nelson–Oppen combination for
n
theories)
.

Let
n\geq 2
and, for each
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, let

\Sigma_{i}
be a signature and

T_{i}

a

\Sigma_{i}
-theory. Assume:

(i)

For all
i\neq j
,

{\cal F}_{\Sigma_{i}}\cap{\cal F}_{\Sigma_{j}}=\emptyset
and

{\cal P}_{\Sigma_{i}}\cap{\cal P}_{\Sigma_{j}}=\emptyset
(only sorts may be shared).

(ii)

For every
i\in[1,n]
we have that
T_{i}
is stably infinite w.r.t.
{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{i}}
.

(iii)

The quantifier-free satisfiability problem for each
T_{i}
is decidable.

Let
\Sigma=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\Sigma_{i}
and let

T=T_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus T_{n}
.
Then the quantifier-free
\Sigma
-satisfiability problem for
T
is decidable and
T
is stably infinite w.r.t.
{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
.

Proof C.126
.

We prove the claim by induction on
n
:

Base case
(
n=2
):
According to
Theorem

34
,
T
is decidable.
We want to also show that
T
is stably infinite w.r.t.
{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{1}}\cup{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{2}}
.
Let
{{\phi}}
be a
\Sigma
-formula that is
T
-satisfiable.

We need to show that there exists a
T
-interpretation
{\cal C}
such that
{\cal C}\models{{\phi}}
and
\lvert\sigma^{{\cal C}}\rvert
is infinite for every
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{1}}\cup{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{2}}
.

Since
{{\phi}}
is
T
-satisfiable, there exists a
T
-interpretation
\hat{{\cal C}}
such that
\hat{{\cal C}}\models{{\phi}}
.

W.l.o.g,
{{\phi}}={{\phi}}_{1}\wedge{{\phi}}_{2}
, where

{{\phi}}_{1}
and
{{\phi}}_{2}
are conjunctions of
\Sigma_{1}
-literals and
\Sigma_{2}
-literals in
{{\phi}}
, respectively.
Let
V={\it vars}({{{\phi}}_{1}})\cap{\it vars}({{{\phi}}_{2}})
be the set of shared variables between
{{\phi}}_{1}
and
{{\phi}}_{2}
.

We can define the following arrangement
\delta_{V}
of
V
:

\delta_{V}=\wedge\{x=y\mid\hat{{\cal C}}\models x=y\}\bigwedge\wedge\{x\neq y\mid\hat{{\cal C}}\models x\neq y\}

Note that
\hat{{\cal C}}^{\Sigma_{1}}\models{{\phi}}_{1}\wedge\delta_{V}
and
\hat{{\cal C}}^{\Sigma_{2}}\models{{\phi}}_{2}\wedge\delta_{V}
.
Since both
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are stably infinite w.r.t. all their sorts, there exist:

•

a
T_{1}
-interpretation
{\cal B}
such that
{\cal B}\models{{\phi}}_{1}\wedge\delta_{V}
and for every
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{1}}
,
\lvert\sigma^{{\cal B}}\rvert
is infinite, and

•

a
T_{2}
-interpretation
{\cal A}
such that
{\cal A}\models{{\phi}}_{2}\wedge\delta_{V}
and for every
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{2}}
,
\lvert\sigma^{{\cal A}}\rvert
is infinite.

By
Theorem

C.123
, we can further assume that
|\sigma^{{\cal B}}|=\aleph_{0}
for every
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{1}}
, and
|\sigma^{{\cal A}}|=\aleph_{0}
for every
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{2}}
.
In particular, for every
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{1}}\cap{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{2}}
, we have
|\sigma^{{\cal B}}|=\aleph_{0}=|\sigma^{{\cal A}}|
.

Thus, the conditions of
Lemma

C.124
are satisfied.

By
Lemma

C.124
, there exists a
T
-interpretation
{\cal C}
such that
{\cal C}\models{{\phi}}_{1}\wedge{{\phi}}_{2}\wedge\delta_{V}
, and for every
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{1}}\cup{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{2}}
,
|\sigma^{{\cal C}}|
is infinite. In particular, we also have
{\cal C}\models{{\phi}}
.

Inductive step
(
n>2
):
Assume the claim holds for
k<n
theories, and let
T_{1},\dots,T_{n}
be
\Sigma_{1},\dots,\Sigma_{n}
-theories, respectively,
such that conditions (i), (ii), and (iii) of the theorem statement hold.
Let
\Sigma^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n-1}\Sigma_{i}
and

T^{\prime}=T_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus T_{n-1}
.
By the induction hypothesis,
T^{\prime}
is decidable and
stably infinite w.r.t.
\bigcup_{i=1}^{n-1}{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{i}}
.
Applying the IH once more to
T^{\prime}
and
T_{n}
,
we conclude that
T^{\prime}\oplus T_{n}=T
is decidable and
stably infinite w.r.t.
\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{i}}
.

We now prove two lemmas that will be useful in the proof of

Theorem

32
.
The first restricts the sorts with finite interpretations in
T_{\text{new}}^{{{\phi}}}
, while the second does so for sorts with infinite interpretations.

Lemma C.127
.

Let
{\cal C}
be a
T_{\text{new}}^{{{\phi}}}
-interpretation that satisfies

\forall\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}}\setminus({\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}}).|\sigma^{{\cal C}}|=\infty
.
For every
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
if
{\sigma^{*}}^{{\cal C}}
is finite then
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
.

Proof C.128
.

Let
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
be such that
{\sigma^{*}}^{{\cal C}}
is finite. We show that
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
by ruling out all other possibilities.

By
Definition

12
,
{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}\cap{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}=\emptyset
, so we can partition
{\cal S}_{\Sigma}
into three disjoint sets:

{\cal S}_{\Sigma}=({\cal S}_{\Sigma}\setminus({\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}))\uplus{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}\uplus{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}

We show that
\sigma
cannot belong to the first two sets.

Case 1:
Suppose
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma}\setminus({\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma})
.
By
Table

6
, we have
{\sigma^{*}}\notin{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}
.
Additionally, by
1
and
Table

4
,
{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}}=\{A_{i}^{\prime}\mid i\in[1,n]\}
, which implies
{\sigma^{*}}\notin{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}}
.
Thus,
{\sigma^{*}}\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}}\setminus({\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}})
.
Since
{\cal C}
satisfies the condition of the lemma, we have
|{\sigma^{*}}^{{\cal C}}|=\infty
, contradicting our assumption that
{\sigma^{*}}^{{\cal C}}
is finite.

Case 2:
If
\sigma=A_{i}
for some
i\in[1,n]
, then since
{{A_{i}}^{\circ}}\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}}\setminus({\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}})

(see
Table

6
),
we have
|{{A_{i}}^{\circ}}^{{\cal C}}|=\infty
.
Fix an element
t\in{E_{i}^{*}}^{{\cal C}}
. Then for every
j\in{{A_{i}}^{\circ}}^{{\cal C}}
, we have
c_{i}(j,t,\ldots,t)\in{A_{i}^{*}}^{{\cal C}}
(if
c_{i}
only has one argument, we omit the
t
s).
Therefore,
|{A_{i}^{*}}^{{\cal C}}|=\infty
, which is a contradiction.

Lemma C.129
.

Let
{\cal C}
be a
T_{\text{new}}^{{{\phi}}}
-interpretation in which
for each

\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}}\setminus({\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}})
,
|\sigma^{{\cal C}}|=\infty
.
Define
\widehat{S}={\cal S}_{\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}}\cap\big({\cal S}_{{\Sigma_{1}^{*}}}\cup\ldots\cup{\cal S}_{{\Sigma_{n}^{*}}}\cup{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+2}}\big)
, then:

|\tau^{{\cal C}}|=\infty

for every
\tau\in\widehat{S}
.

Proof C.130
.

First note that
\widehat{S}\subseteq{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}
.
Thus, it suffices to consider
\tau\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}
with
|\tau^{{\cal C}}|<\infty
and show that
\tau\notin\widehat{S}
.

Step 1.
\tau={\sigma^{*}}
for some
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
.

By
Table

6
,
{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}=\{{\sigma^{*}}\mid\sigma\in{\bf Elem}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\setminus{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}\}\cup\{{{A_{i}}^{\circ}}\mid i\in[1,n]\}\cup\{{E_{i}^{*}}\mid i\in[1,n],E_{i}\notin({\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}\cup{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}})\}\cup\{{\sigma^{*}}\mid\sigma\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}\}
.

If
\tau={{A_{i}}^{\circ}}
for some
i\in[1,n]
, then since
{{A_{i}}^{\circ}}\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}}\setminus({\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}})
, by the assumption over
{\cal C}
we have
|{{A_{i}}^{\circ}}^{{\cal C}}|=\infty
, which is a contradiction.

Hence,
\tau={\sigma^{*}}
for some
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
. By
Lemma

C.127
, we have
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
.

Step 2. Constructing a matching subgraph for
\sigma
.

Consider the graph
G_{\Sigma}
(see
Definition

B.76
).
Since
|{\sigma^{*}}^{{\cal C}}|<\infty
,
Lemma

C.120
shows that
\sigma
is not eventually cyclic.
Consider the subgraph of
G_{\Sigma}
reachable from
\sigma
, denoted
G_{\Sigma}^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
.
Since
\sigma
is not eventually cyclic,
G_{\Sigma}^{\prime}
is a DAG.

Let
\rho\in V^{\prime}
. Since
\rho
is reachable from
\sigma
in
G_{\Sigma}
,
Lemma

C.118
implies that
|{\rho^{*}}^{{\cal C}}|\leq|{\sigma^{*}}^{{\cal C}}|<\infty
. By
Lemma

C.127
,
\rho\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
.

Hence,
V^{\prime}\subseteq{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
.

Step 3. Showing that
|\sigma^{{\cal A}^{\prime}}|<\infty
for every
T_{n+1}
-interpretation
{\cal A}^{\prime}
.

Let
{\cal A}^{\prime}
be any
T_{n+1}
-interpretation. We use induction on the length of the longest path starting from a node in the graph
G_{\Sigma}^{\prime}
to show that
\forall\rho\in V^{\prime}.|\rho^{{\cal A}^{\prime}}|<\infty
.

•

Base Case (longest path length = 0):
Let
\rho\in V^{\prime}
be a node such that the longest path starting from
\rho
has length 0. This means
\rho
has no outgoing edges in
G_{\Sigma}^{\prime}
. Since
\rho\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
, any constructor in
{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
with target sort
\rho
must take no arguments (otherwise there would be an outgoing edge from
\rho
).
Therefore,
\rho^{{\cal A}^{\prime}}
is the finite set of all constant constructors in
{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
of sort
\rho
, which is finite since
\Sigma_{n+1}
is a datatype signature (see
Definition

3
).

•

Inductive Step:

Assume the claim holds for every node
\rho^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}
where the longest path starting from
\rho^{\prime}
has length at most
k
.
Let
\rho\in V^{\prime}
be a node where the longest path starting from
\rho
has length
k+1
.
Since
\rho\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
, for every constructor
c:\tau_{1}\times\ldots\times\tau_{m}\to\rho
, and for each argument sort
\tau_{i}
(where
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
), there is an edge from
\rho
to
\tau_{i}
in
G_{\Sigma}
.
Since the longest path from
\rho
has length
k+1
, the longest path from any of its successors
\tau_{i}
has length at most
k
.
By the induction hypothesis,
|\tau_{i}^{{\cal A}^{\prime}}|<\infty
for each
i
.
Therefore, due to
Definition

6
we get that
\rho^{{\cal A}^{\prime}}
is finite, since it is the finite union of finite sets.

Thus, for all
\rho\in V^{\prime}
,
including
\sigma
,
we have
|\rho^{{\cal A}^{\prime}}|<\infty
.

Step 4.
\sigma\notin{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cap\big({\cal S}_{\{}\Sigma_{1}\}\cup\ldots\cup{\cal S}_{\{}\Sigma_{n}\}\big)
.

By the assumption of
Theorem

32
, the theory
T_{n+1}
is stably infinite with respect to
{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cap\big({\cal S}_{\{}\Sigma_{1}\}\cup\ldots\cup{\cal S}_{\{}\Sigma_{n}\}\big)
.

This means that there is a
T_{n+1}
interpretation in which every sort in
{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cap\big({\cal S}_{\{}\Sigma_{1}\}\cup\ldots\cup{\cal S}_{\{}\Sigma_{n}\}\big)
has an infinite domain.

However, by Step 3, the interpretation of
\sigma
is finite for every
T_{n+1}
-interpretation
{\cal A}^{\prime}
.
Therefore,

\sigma\notin{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cap\big({\cal S}_{\{}\Sigma_{1}\}\cup\ldots\cup{\cal S}_{\{}\Sigma_{n}\}\big).

(3)

Step 5. Concluding that
\tau\notin\widehat{S}

According to Step 1, we have
\tau={\sigma^{*}}
for some
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
and by our assumption over
\tau
we know that
\tau\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}
.

We now show that
\tau
does not appear in any of the other signatures (i.e.
{\Sigma_{1}^{*}}\ldots{\Sigma_{n}^{*}}
or
\Sigma_{n+2}
) and thus
\tau\notin\widehat{S}
.
First, because of Step 3, we know that
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}
and by
Equation

3
it holds that
\sigma\notin{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{i}}
for every
i\in[1,n]
.
According to
Table

4
we have
{\cal S}_{{\Sigma_{i}^{*}}}=\{{I_{i}^{*}},{E_{i}^{*}},A_{i}^{\prime}\}
,
it follows that
\tau={\sigma^{*}}\notin{\cal S}_{{\Sigma_{i}^{*}}}
for every
i\in[1,n]
.

Second, we have established that
\sigma\notin{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{i}}
for every
i\in[1,n]
and thus
\sigma\notin{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}
.
By
Table

6
, we have
{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+2}}=\{{A_{i}^{*}}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}\cup\{A_{i}^{\prime}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}
.
Therefore,
\tau={\sigma^{*}}\notin{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+2}}
.

Thus,
\tau={\sigma^{*}}\notin{\cal S}_{{\Sigma_{1}^{*}}}\cup\ldots\cup{\cal S}_{{\Sigma_{n}^{*}}}\cup{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+2}}
, which implies
\tau\notin\widehat{S}
.

Now we can restate and prove
Theorem

32
.

See
32

Domains

\forall\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}}.\sigma^{{\cal B}_{1}}=\begin{cases}\sigma^{{\cal B}_{0}}&\text{if }\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}},\\[4.0pt]
{I_{i}^{*}}^{{\cal B}_{1}}\rightarrow{E_{i}^{*}}^{{\cal B}_{1}}&\text{if }\sigma=A_{i}^{\prime}\text{ for some }i\in[1,n],\\[4.0pt]
\{c_{\sigma}\}&\text{else }\end{cases}

Functions

select
and
store
are defined as

read and update
operations on functions.

All functions in
{\cal F}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}
are interpreted as in
{\cal B}_{0}
.

For every
i\in[1,n]
we fix
f_{A_{i}}^{{\cal B}_{1}}
and

g_{A_{i}}^{{\cal B}_{1}}
to some arbitrary functions.

Predicates

All predicates are interpreted as in
{\cal B}_{0}
.

Variables

All variables are interpreted as in
{\cal B}_{0}
.

Table 24:
A
T_{\text{new}}^{{{\phi}}}
-interpretation
{\cal B}_{1}
.
c_{\sigma}
is an arbitrary but fixed element for each sort
\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}}\setminus\big({\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}\cup\{A_{i}^{\prime}\mid i\in[1,n]\}\big)
.

Proof C.131
.

Step 1. Reducing to stable infiniteness of
T_{n+1}^{{\phi}}
.

Using

Section

2.3

observe that the theories
{T_{1}^{*}},\ldots,{T_{n}^{*}}
are stably infinite with respect to all their sorts, since they are array theories.
Similarly,
T_{n+2}
is stably infinite with respect to all its sorts, since it is the theory of uninterpreted functions.

Therefore, by
Theorem

C.125
, the combination
{T_{1}^{*}}\oplus\ldots\oplus{T_{n}^{*}}\oplus T_{n+2}
is decidable and stably infinite with respect to all its sorts.

To apply
Theorem

34
to
{T_{1}^{*}}\oplus\ldots\oplus{T_{n}^{*}}\oplus T_{n+2}
and
T_{n+1}^{{\phi}}
, it remains to prove that
T_{n+1}^{{\phi}}
is stably infinite with respect to
\widehat{S}={\cal S}_{\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}}\cap\big({\cal S}_{{\Sigma_{1}^{*}}}\cup\ldots\cup{\cal S}_{{\Sigma_{n}^{*}}}\cup{\cal S}_{\Sigma_{n+2}}\big)
.

Let
\theta
be a
T_{n+1}^{{{\phi}}}
-satisfiable
\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}
-formula. Then there exists a
T_{n+1}^{{\phi}}
-interpretation
{\cal B}_{0}
such that
{\cal B}_{0}\models\theta
.

Step 2. Constructing a
T_{\text{new}}^{{{\phi}}}
-interpretation of
\theta
.

Since
\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}={\Sigma_{1}^{*}}\cup\ldots\cup{\Sigma_{n}^{*}}\cup\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}\cup\Sigma_{n+2}
,
we can view
\theta
as a formula in the signature
\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}
.
We define an interpretation
{\cal B}_{1}
over the signature
\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}
using
{\cal B}_{0}
(see
Table

24
).

We now show that
{\cal B}_{1}
is a
T_{\text{new}}^{{{\phi}}}
-interpretation:

•

For every
i\in[1,n]
,
{\cal B}_{1}^{{\Sigma_{i}^{*}}}
is an array interpretation: the array sort is interpreted as the set of functions
A_{i}^{\prime{\cal B}_{1}}={I_{i}^{*}}^{{\cal B}_{1}}\rightarrow{E_{i}^{*}}^{{\cal B}_{1}}
, and the symbols
\textbf{select}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}
and
\textbf{store}_{A_{i}^{\prime}}
are interpreted as the usual read and update operations on arrays.

•

{\cal B}_{1}^{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}
is a
T_{n+1}^{{\phi}}
-interpretation, since all domains, functions, and predicates in
\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}
are interpreted as in
{\cal B}_{0}
.

•

{\cal B}_{1}^{\Sigma_{n+2}}
is a
T_{n+2}
-interpretation, since
T_{n+2}
is the theory of uninterpreted functions and thus every interpretation over the signature
\Sigma_{n+2}
is a
T_{n+2}
-interpretation.

Note that
{\cal B}_{1}
satisfies
\theta
, since
{\cal B}_{1}^{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}
is interpreted as
{\cal B}_{0}
, which satisfies
\theta
.

By
Lemma

B.64
, there exists a
T_{\text{new}}^{{{\phi}}}
-interpretation
{\cal C}
such that
{\cal C}\models\theta
and:

\forall\sigma\in{\cal S}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}}\setminus({\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}}).|\sigma^{{\cal C}}|=\infty

(4)

Step 3.
T_{n+1}^{{\phi}}
is stably infinite w.r.t.
\widehat{S}
.

Define the
T_{n+1}^{{\phi}}
-interpretation
{\cal B}={\cal C}^{\Sigma_{n+1}^{{\phi}}}
.
By construction,
{\cal B}\models\theta
, and due to
Equation

4
and
Lemma

C.129
,
|\tau^{{\cal B}}|=|\tau^{{\cal C}}|=\infty
for every
\tau\in\widehat{S}
.

Since
\theta
was an arbitrary satisfiable

T_{n+1}^{{\phi}}
-formula, we
conclude that
T_{n+1}^{{\phi}}
is stably infinite with respect to

\widehat{S}
.

Appendix D
Additional Material

D.1
A concrete interpretation for
Example

15

In
Example

15
, satisfiability of
{{\varphi}}
from
Table

1
was proven indirectly,
using an argument that relates to the Nelson-Oppen method.
In this section, we provide a concrete

{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}}}^{{\mathit{P}NDT}}
-interpretation
that satisfies the formula
{{\varphi}}
.
Define an interpretation
{\cal A}
by setting

I^{\cal A}=\{-1\}
,

E^{\cal A}=\{{\mathbf{emp}}\}\cup\{{\mathbf{c}}(-1,n)\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}\}
,
and

A^{\cal A}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.
The interpretation of the variables is given by

a^{\cal A}=\infty
,

i^{\cal A}=-1
, and

x^{\cal A}={\mathbf{c}}(-1,\infty)
.
To interpret the symbols, we define
a bijection
m:\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}\to E^{\cal A}

by setting

m(0)={\mathbf{emp}}
,

m(n)={\mathbf{c}}(-1,n-1)
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
,
and

m(\infty)={\mathbf{c}}(-1,\infty)
.
Finally,

{\textbf{select}}^{{\cal A}}(\alpha,\beta)=m(\alpha)

{\textbf{store}}^{{\cal A}}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=m^{-1}(\gamma)
, and
the remaining function symbols
of
\Sigma_{2}
are defined according to
Definition

6
.

It can be shown that
{\cal A}
is indeed a
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma}}^{{\mathit{P}NDT}}
-interpretation
that satisfies
{{\varphi}}
.

First, let’s show that
{\cal A}^{\Sigma_{1}}
is an arrays
\Sigma_{1}
-structure
by showing it satisfies the needed axioms from
Figure

5
.

{\cal R}{{\cal O}}{\cal W}_{1}
:
\forall a,i,e.\textbf{select}_{A}(\textbf{store}_{A}(a,i,e),i)=e

{\cal R}{{\cal O}}{\cal W}_{2}
:
\forall a,i,e,j.\neg(j=i)\implies\textbf{select}_{A}(\textbf{store}_{A}(a,i,e),j)=\textbf{select}_{A}(a,j)

{\cal E}{\cal X}{\cal T}
:
\forall a_{1},a_{2}.\neg(a_{1}=a_{2})\implies\exists j:\neg(\textbf{select}_{A}(a_{1},j)=\textbf{select}_{A}(a_{2},j))

Figure 5:
Axioms for arrays.

1.

{\cal R}{{\cal O}}{\cal W}_{1}
:

\displaystyle\forall\alpha\in A^{\cal A}.\forall\beta\in I^{\cal A}.\forall\gamma\in E^{\cal A}.

\displaystyle\textbf{select}^{\cal A}\big(\textbf{store}^{\cal A}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma),\beta\big)

\displaystyle=\textbf{select}^{\cal A}\big(m^{-1}(\gamma),\beta\big)

\displaystyle=m\big(m^{-1}(\gamma)\big)=\gamma.

2.

{\cal R}{{\cal O}}{\cal W}_{2}
: We need to show that:

\displaystyle\forall\alpha\in A^{\cal A}.\beta\in I^{\cal A}.\gamma\in E^{\cal A}.\delta\in I^{\cal A}.

\displaystyle\neg(\delta=\beta)\implies\textbf{select}^{\cal A}\big(\textbf{store}^{\cal A}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma),\delta\big)

\displaystyle=\textbf{select}^{\cal A}(\alpha,\delta).

And since
|I^{\cal A}|=1
, the condition
\neg(\delta=\beta)
is never satisfied and the axiom is trivially true.

3.

{\cal E}{\cal X}{\cal T}
: Let there be
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in A
such that
\alpha_{1}\neq\alpha_{2}
. Then, we need to show that there exists
\beta\in I
such that
\textbf{select}^{\cal A}(\alpha_{1},\beta)\neq\textbf{select}^{\cal A}(\alpha_{2},\beta)
.
Then, since
m
is a bijection, we have:

\displaystyle\textbf{select}^{\cal A}(\alpha_{1},-1)

\displaystyle=m(\alpha_{1})\neq m(\alpha_{2})=\textbf{select}^{\cal A}(\alpha_{2},-1).

Now, we show that
{\cal A}^{\Sigma_{2}}
is a datatype
\Sigma_{2}
-structure generated by
{\bf Elem}
.

1.

E^{\cal A}
is indeed the
T_{E}\bigl({\Sigma}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{,}}\{I^{\cal A},A^{\cal A}\}\bigr)
,

2.

c^{{\cal A}}(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})=c(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
for every
c\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{2}}
of
arity
(\sigma_{1}\times\ldots\times\sigma_{n})\rightarrow\sigma
and
t_{1}\in\sigma_{1}^{{\cal A}},\dots,t_{n}\in\sigma_{n}^{{\cal A}}
,

3.

s_{(c,i)}^{{\cal A}}(c(t_{1},\dots,t_{n}))=t_{i}

for every
c\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{2}}
of
arity
(\sigma_{1}\times\ldots\times\sigma_{n})\rightarrow\sigma
,
t_{1}\in\sigma_{1}^{{\cal A}},\dots,t_{n}\in\sigma_{n}^{{\cal A}}
and
1\leq i\leq n
,

4.

\text{is}_{c}^{{\cal A}}=\left\{c(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\mid t_{1}\in\sigma_{1}^{{\cal A}},\dots,t_{n}\in\sigma_{n}^{{\cal A}}\right\}
for every
c\in{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma_{2}}
of arity

(\sigma_{1}\times\ldots\times\sigma_{n})\rightarrow\sigma
.

Now let’s show that
{\cal A}\models{{\varphi}}
:

1.

\text{is}_{{\mathbf{c}}}^{\cal A}(x^{\cal A})
holds since
x^{\cal A}={\mathbf{c}}(-1,\infty)
is an application of the constructor
{\mathbf{c}}
.

2.

{\mathbf{chld}}^{\cal A}(x^{\cal A})={\mathbf{chld}}^{\cal A}({\mathbf{c}}(-1,\infty))=\infty=a^{\cal A}
.

3.

x^{\cal A}={\mathbf{c}}(-1,\infty)=m(\infty)=\textbf{select}^{\cal A}(\infty,-1)=\textbf{select}^{\cal A}(a^{\cal A},i^{\cal A})

Finally, we verify that indeed
Definition

16
excludes
the interpretation
{\cal A}
from being an NDT-
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}
-structure.

\displaystyle{\cal R}_{{\cal A}}=

\displaystyle\{({\mathbf{c}}(-1,n-1),n)\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}\}

\displaystyle\cup\{({\mathbf{emp}},0),({\mathbf{c}}(-1,\infty),\infty)\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(-1,{\mathbf{c}}(-1,n))\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(n,{\mathbf{c}}(-1,n))\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}\}

The first two sets come from the select function,
and the last two from constructor applications.
Since
{\cal R}_{{\cal A}}
contains the cycle
(a^{\cal A},x^{\cal A},a^{\cal A})
,
we have that
{\cal A}
is not an NDT
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}
-interpretation.

D.2
More Details on
Example

17

The formula
{{\varphi}}
from

Table

1

is
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-unsatisfiable.
Assume otherwise, and
let there be a
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-interpretation
{\cal A}
that satisfies
{{\varphi}}
.
{\cal A}\models\psi_{1}^{\prime}
and therefore
x^{\cal A}=\textbf{select}(a,i)
and so:

(x^{\cal A},a^{\cal A})\in{\cal R}_{{\cal A}}.

(5)

Similarly,
{\cal A}\models\psi_{2}
and thus

\text{is}_{{\mathbf{c}}}^{\cal A}(x^{\cal A})
and
a^{\cal A}={\mathbf{chld}}(x^{\cal A})
.

Definition

6
then suggests that
x^{\cal A}={\mathbf{c}}(a^{\cal A},c)

for some
b
and
c
,
and thus:

(a^{\cal A},x^{\cal A})\in{\cal R}_{{\cal A}}

(6)

From
Equations

5
and
6
, we get
a cycle in
{\cal R}_{{\cal A}}
which entails an infinite descending sequence,
thus violating well-foundedness.

D.3
Proof of
Lemma

21

See
21

Proof D.132
.

\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}_{n+1}
is already presented
using element and struct sorts, as well as constructors,
selectors and testers.
In particular,

{\bf Elem}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}_{n+1}}
and

{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}_{n+1}}

are specified in
Table

6
.
It is left to show that each sort in

{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}_{n+1}}

is well-founded w.r.t.
{\bf Elem}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}_{n+1}}

in
{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}_{n+1}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}_{n+1}}}
,
according to
Definition

2
.
Let
\tau\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}_{n+1}}
.
Then there exists
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}

such that
\tau={\sigma^{*}}
.
Since
\Sigma
is well-founded as an NDT signature, we have that there exists some
m\in\mathbb{N}
with

\sigma\in F_{m}(\Sigma,{\cal S}_{\Sigma}\,\setminus\,({\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}\,\cup\,{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}))
.
We prove that for every
m\geq 0
, if

\sigma\in F_{m}(\Sigma,{\cal S}_{\Sigma}\,\setminus\,({\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}\,\cup\,{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}))

then

{\sigma^{*}}\in G_{m}({\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}}},{\bf Elem}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}_{n+1}})
.
We do so
by induction on
m
.
As a result, we then get that

\tau={\sigma^{*}}

is well-founded w.r.t.
{\bf Elem}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}_{n+1}}

in
{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}_{n+1}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}_{n+1}}}
.

For the sake of this proof, we abbreviate
F_{k}(\Sigma,{\cal S}_{\Sigma}\,\setminus\,({\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}\,\cup\,{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}))
as
F_{k}
and
G_{k}({\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\Sigma^{{\phi}}_{n+1}}},{\bf Elem}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}_{n+1}})
as
G_{k}
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

Base case (
m=0
):
No
\sigma\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}
belongs to

F_{0}
, so the claim holds vacuously.

•

Inductive step:

Assume the claim holds for
m
, and let

\sigma\;\in\;F_{m+1}
.
If already

\sigma\in F_{m}
, then by definition
G_{m}\subseteq G_{m+1}
and
the inductive hypothesis we are done. Otherwise there are two cases:

1.

Array sort.

Suppose
\sigma=A_{i}
for some
i\in[1,n]
.
Since
\sigma\in F_{m+1}
, we have that

I_{i}

and
E_{i}
are in

F_{m}
.

–

If
E_{i}\notin{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}
, then

{E_{i}^{*}}
is in
{\bf Elem}_{\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}_{n+1}}=G_{0}
and clearly so is
{{A_{i}}^{\circ}}
, so by the constructor
c_{i}
we get

{A_{i}^{*}}\in G_{1}\subseteq G_{m+1}
.

–

Otherwise
E_{i}\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}
and since
E_{i}\in F_{m}
, we have by IH that
{E_{i}^{*}}\in G_{m}
, and clearly
{{A_{i}}^{\circ}}\in G_{0}\subseteq G_{m}
.
Again
c_{i}
yields

{A_{i}^{*}}\in G_{m+1}
.

2.

Struct sort.

There is a constructor

c:\sigma_{1}\times\cdots\times\sigma_{k}\to\sigma

with each
\sigma_{j}\in F_{m}
. For every
j\in[1,k]
, if
\sigma_{j}\in{\bf Struct}_{\Sigma_{n+1}}\cup{\bf{Array}}_{\Sigma}
, by IH
{\sigma_{j}^{*}}\in G_{m}
, otherwise,

{\sigma_{j}^{*}}\in G_{0}\subseteq G_{m}
. Hence constructor

{c^{*}}
places
{\sigma^{*}}
into
G_{m+1}
.

D.4
More Details on
Example

27

We hereby define a
T_{\text{new}}^{{{\varphi}}}
-interpretation,
{\cal B}
that
satisfies
\bf{tr}({{\varphi}})
.

For the domains, we set

{I^{*}}^{\cal B}=\mathbb{N}
,
{{{A}^{\circ}}}^{\cal B}=\{-1\}
,
and define an
{\bf Elem}_{\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}^{{{\varphi}}}}
-sorted set
B
such that
B_{\sigma}=\sigma^{\cal B}
for each
\sigma\in{\bf Elem}_{\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}^{{{\varphi}}}}
.
Then we can set

{E^{*}}^{\cal B}=T_{{E^{*}}}({{\Sigma_{2}^{*}}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{{\Sigma_{2}^{*}}}},B)
,

{A^{*}}^{\cal B}=T_{{A^{*}}}({{\Sigma_{2}^{*}}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{{\Sigma_{2}^{*}}}},B)
, and

A^{\prime{\cal B}}={I^{*}}^{{\cal B}}\rightarrow{E^{*}}^{\cal B}
.

The function symbols
select
and
store
,
as well as the constructors, selectors and testers are interpreted as expected
(for this example, it does not matter how wrongly applied selectors are interpreted).

As for the variables, we set

{i^{*}}^{\cal B}=1
,

{a^{*}}^{\cal B}=c_{1}(-1,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}})
,

{x^{*}}^{\cal B}={{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}(3,c_{1}(-1,{{\mathbf{emp}}^{*}}))
,
and

a^{\prime{\cal B}}=\{(n,{x^{*}}^{\cal B})\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
,
that is,

a^{\prime{\cal B}}
is the constant function that always returns
{x^{*}}^{\cal B}
.

{\cal B}
is therefore a
T_{\text{new}}^{{{\varphi}}}
-interpretation that clearly
satisfies every literal of
\bf{tr}({{\varphi}})
.

The stable infiniteness condition is necessary
in the right-to-left direction of
Theorem

31
.
Consider the NDT signature

\tilde{\Sigma}=\tilde{\Sigma_{1}}\cup\tilde{\Sigma_{2}}

described in
Table

25
,
and let

\tilde{T_{1}}
be the
\tilde{\Sigma_{1}}
-theory of arrays and

\tilde{T_{2}}
the
\tilde{\Sigma_{2}}
-theory of datatypes.

Consider the
\tilde{\Sigma}
‐formula

\theta_{2}:=x\neq y
, such that

x,y
both have sort
S
.
Let
{\cal C}
be a
\tilde{T_{2}}
-interpretation.
Let
B
be the
{\bf Elem}_{\tilde{\Sigma_{2}}}
-sorted set with

B_{\rho}=\rho^{\cal C}
for each

\rho\in{\bf Elem}_{\tilde{\Sigma_{2}}}
.
Thus:

E^{{\cal C}}\;=\;T_{E}({\tilde{\Sigma_{2}}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\tilde{\Sigma_{2}}}},B)\;=\;\{{\mathbf{emp}}\}
.

Note that
E
is a shared sort between
\tilde{\Sigma_{1}}
and

\tilde{\Sigma_{2}}
and since
\tilde{T_{2}}
enforces that the
interpretation of
E
is
\{{\mathbf{emp}}\}
, we know that
\tilde{T_{2}}
is
not stably infinite over
{\cal S}_{\tilde{\Sigma_{1}}}\cap{\cal S}_{\tilde{\Sigma_{2}}}
.

Assume towards a contradiction that
\theta_{2}
is satisfiable by a

{{\cal T}}_{{\tilde{\Sigma}}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-interpretation
{\cal A}_{1}
.
Since
{{\cal A}_{1}}^{\tilde{\Sigma_{2}}}
is a

\tilde{T_{2}}
-interpretation, we can denote as
\tilde{B}
the

{\bf Elem}_{\tilde{\Sigma_{2}}}
-sorted set with

\tilde{B}_{\rho}=\rho^{{\cal A}_{1}}
for each

\rho\in{\bf Elem}_{\tilde{\Sigma_{2}}}
.
Thus:

S^{{\cal A}_{1}}\;=\;T_{S}({\tilde{\Sigma_{2}}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\tilde{\Sigma_{2}}}},\tilde{B})\;=\;\{{\mathbf{c}}(z)\mid z\in A^{{\cal A}_{1}}\}\quad E^{{\cal A}_{1}}\;=\;T_{E}({\tilde{\Sigma_{2}}}_{\mid{{\cal C}}{{\cal O}}_{\tilde{\Sigma_{2}}}},\tilde{B})\;=\;\{{\mathbf{emp}}\}

Hence,
x^{{\cal A}_{1}}={\mathbf{c}}(a)
and

y^{{\cal A}_{1}}={\mathbf{c}}(b)
for some

a,b\in A^{{\cal A}_{1}}
.
Since
x^{{\cal A}_{1}}\neq y^{{\cal A}_{1}}
, we have
a\neq b
. According to the Extensionality
axiom, we have that there exists
k\in I^{{\cal A}_{1}}
such that

\textbf{select}_{A}^{{\cal A}_{1}}(a,k)\neq\textbf{select}_{A}^{{\cal A}_{1}}(b,k)
. However, since

\textbf{select}_{A}^{{\cal A}_{1}}(a,k),\textbf{select}_{A}^{{\cal A}_{1}}(b,k)\in E^{{\cal A}_{1}}
, we have that

\textbf{select}_{A}^{{\cal A}_{1}}(a,k)={\mathbf{emp}}=\textbf{select}_{A}^{{\cal A}_{1}}(b,k)
, which is a contradiction.

Yet,
its translation
\bf{trFormula}(\theta_{2})
and its matching signature are given in
Table

26
,
and
\bf{trFormula}(\theta_{2})
is satisfied
by the
{{\cal T}}_{{\tilde{\Sigma}^{\theta_{2}}}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-interpretation

{\cal B}
defined in
Table

19
.

D.6
Discussion on the translation

We describe various decisions made in our translation-based
approach.

D.6.1
The sorts
{{A}^{\circ}}

Although
\bf{trFormula}({{\phi}})
does not introduce
terms of the sorts

{{A_{i}}^{\circ}}
for
i\in[1,n]
,
those sorts are still required in
\Sigma^{{{\phi}}}
.
If we drop them, the translation may turn a satisfiable
formula into an unsatisfiable one,
thus violating
Theorem

31
.
The following example makes this precise.

Let
\theta
be defined as follows:

\theta\;:=\;a\neq b\;\land\;x=\textbf{select}_{A}(a,i)\;\land\;x=\textbf{select}_{A}(b,i),

Where
a
and
b
are array variables of sort
A
,
x
is a variables of sort
E
and
i
is a variable of sort
I
.

\theta
is
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-satisfiable.

Assume we construct
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}^{\theta}
exactly as in
Table

6
but
omit
the index sort
{{A}^{\circ}}
.
The three component signatures that form

\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}^{\theta}
are summarised in

Table

27
.
Because
{{A}^{\circ}}
is absent,
the constructor
c_{1}
now has arity

{E^{*}}\!\to\!{A^{*}}
and hence only one selector.

Signature

Sorts

Functions

Predicates

{\Sigma_{1}^{*}}

\{{I^{*}},{E^{*}},A^{\prime}\}

\textbf{select}_{A^{\prime}}:A^{\prime}\!\times\!{I^{*}}\!\to\!{E^{*}}

\textbf{store}_{A^{\prime}}:A^{\prime}\!\times\!{I^{*}}\!\times\!{E^{*}}\!\to\!A^{\prime}

—

\Sigma_{2}^{\theta}

\{{I^{*}},{E^{*}},{A^{*}}\}

c_{1}:{E^{*}}\to{A^{*}}

{{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}:{I^{*}}\!\times\!{A^{*}}\!\to\!{E^{*}}

s_{(c_{1},1)}:{A^{*}}\!\to\!{E^{*}}

s_{({{\mathbf{c}}^{*}},1)}:{E^{*}}\!\to\!{I^{*}}

s_{({{\mathbf{c}}^{*}},2)}:{E^{*}}\!\to\!{A^{*}}

\text{is}_{c_{1}}:{A^{*}}

\text{is}_{{{\mathbf{c}}^{*}}}:{E^{*}}

\Sigma_{3}

\{{A^{*}},A^{\prime}\}

f_{A}:{A^{*}}\!\to\!A^{\prime}

g_{A}:A^{\prime}\!\to\!{A^{*}}

—

Table 27:
Component signatures of
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}^{\theta}
when the
index sort
{{A}^{\circ}}
is
omitted
.

Then, the translation of
\theta
would be

\displaystyle\bf{trFormula}(\theta)=\;

\displaystyle\bigl({a^{*}}\neq{b^{*}}\land{x^{*}}=\textbf{select}_{A^{\prime}}(a^{\prime},{i^{*}})\land{x^{*}}=\textbf{select}_{A^{\prime}}(b^{\prime},{i^{*}})\bigr)

\displaystyle\land\bigl(u_{(a,1)}=\textbf{select}_{A^{\prime}}(a^{\prime},{i^{*}})\land u_{(b,1)}=\textbf{select}_{A^{\prime}}(b^{\prime},{i^{*}})

\displaystyle\land u_{(a,1)}=s_{(c_{1},1)}({a^{*}})\land u_{(b,1)}=s_{(c_{1},1)}({b^{*}})

\displaystyle\qquad\;\land a^{\prime}=f_{A}({a^{*}})\land b^{\prime}=f_{A}({b^{*}})\land{a^{*}}=g_{A}(a^{\prime})\land{b^{*}}=g_{A}(b^{\prime})\bigr).

Since
c_{1}
is the only constructor whose target sort is
{A^{*}}
,
and it has only one argument (which is of sort
{E^{*}}
),
the four middle conjuncts imply

s_{(c_{1},1)}({a^{*}})=s_{(c_{1},1)}({b^{*}}),

hence
{a^{*}}={b^{*}}
.
This contradicts
{a^{*}}\neq{b^{*}}
,
the first conjunct,
so
\bf{trFormula}(\theta)
is
T_{\text{new}}^{\theta}
-
unsatisfiable
.

On the other hand,
when the index sort
{{A}^{\circ}}
is retained,

c_{1}
has sort

{{A}^{\circ}}\times{E^{*}}\to{A^{*}}
.
Hence

s_{(c_{1},2)}({a^{*}})
and

s_{(c_{1},2)}({b^{*}})
may be equal without forcing

{a^{*}}={b^{*}}
.

D.6.2
On The Third Lemma

The motivation for
\mathbf{L_{3}}({{{\phi}}})
is to ensure that
for every array sort
A
,
there is a one to one mapping between the interpretations of free variables of sort
A^{\prime}
and those of sort
{A^{*}}
.

f_{A}
and
g_{A}
are asserted to be inverses of one another
when restricted to the relevant domain, thus ensuring the 1-1 correspondence.

Example D.133
.

Consider the
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}
-formula
{{\varphi}}
from
Table

1
. Then,

\mathbf{L_{3}}({{{\varphi}}})=\Bigl\{a^{\prime}=f_{A}({a^{*}}),\quad{a^{*}}=g_{A}(a^{\prime})\Bigr\}.

Here,
{a^{*}}
and
a^{\prime}
are new variables of sort

A^{\prime}
,

f_{A}
is a function symbol of arity

{A^{*}}\to A^{\prime}
,
and
g_{A}
is a function symbol of arity

A^{\prime}\to{A^{*}}
.

\widehat{{{\varphi}}}

a\neq b\;\land\;x=\textbf{select}(a,i)\;\land\;b=\textbf{store}(a,i,x)

\bf{tr}(\widehat{{{\varphi}}})\wedge\mathbf{L_{1}}({\widehat{{{\varphi}}}})\wedge\mathbf{L_{2}}({\widehat{{{\varphi}}}})

{a^{*}}\neq{b^{*}}\land{x^{*}}=\textbf{select}(a^{\prime},{i^{*}})\land b^{\prime}=\textbf{store}(a^{\prime},{i^{*}},{x^{*}})\land

u_{(a,1)}=\textbf{select}(a^{\prime},{i^{*}})\land u_{(a,1)}=s_{(c_{1},2)}({a^{*}})\land

u_{(b,1)}=\textbf{select}(b^{\prime},{i^{*}})\land u_{(b,1)}=s_{(c_{1},2)}({b^{*}})

Table 28:
A
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-formula and its translation.
Note that
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}^{{{\psi}}}=\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}^{{{\varphi}}}
,
given in
Table

7
.

Example D.134
.

In order to demonstrate the necessity of
\mathbf{L_{3}}({{{\phi}}})
, we can consider the following
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}
-formula
\widehat{{{\varphi}}}
in
Table

28
.
Notice that
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}^{\widehat{{{\varphi}}}}
is the same
as
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}^{{{\varphi}}}
(given in
Table

7
), and

\bf{tr}(\widehat{{{\varphi}}})\wedge\mathbf{L_{1}}({\widehat{{{\varphi}}}})\wedge\mathbf{L_{2}}({\widehat{{{\varphi}}}})
is also given in
Table

28
.

Now,
\widehat{{{\varphi}}}
is not
{{\cal T}}_{{\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}}}^{{\mathit{N}DT}}
-satisfiable,
which can be seen by pure array reasoning.
However, its translation with the first two sets of lemmas is

T_{\text{new}}^{\widehat{{{\varphi}}}}
-satisfiable, since there is nothing
preventing
{a^{*}}\neq{b^{*}}
while
a^{\prime}=b^{\prime}
, despite
the fact that both
{a^{*}}
and
a^{\prime}
represent
a
and
{b^{*}}

and
b^{\prime}
represent
b
.
The issue is that
a
and
b
may differ on indices that do not occur in
\widehat{{{\varphi}}}
,
and this is lost in translation, unless we use
\mathbf{L_{3}}({{{\phi}}})
.

D.6.3
Optimized Lemmas

The lemmas from
Section

4.3
are defined in a way that

\mathbf{L_{1}}({{{\phi}}})
and

\mathbf{L_{2}}({{{\phi}}})
collect
the lemmas of

\mathbf{L_{0}^{{{\phi}}}}({a,i})
for specific terms
a
and
i
.
A simpler alternative is to discard the delicate
construction of
\mathbf{L_{1}}({{{\phi}}})
and
\mathbf{L_{2}}({{{\phi}}})
and instead add
every
lemma

\mathbf{L_{0}^{{{\phi}}}}({a,i})
for each array variable
a\in{\it vars}_{A_{j}}({{{\phi}}})

and every index
i\in{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{j},{{\phi}})}
.
Formally, we could have replaced
{\bf L}({{{\phi}}})
(see
Examples

30
and
D.133
) by:

{\bf L}({{{\phi}}})^{\prime}\;=\;\bigl(\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq n}\,\bigcup_{a\in{\it vars}_{A_{j}}({{{\phi}}})}\,\bigcup_{i\in{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{j},{{\phi}})}}\mathbf{L_{0}^{{{\phi}}}}({a,i})\bigr)\;\cup\;\mathbf{L_{3}}({{{\phi}}}),

and clearly
{\bf L}({{{\phi}}})^{\prime}\supseteq\mathbf{L_{1}}({{{\phi}}})\cup\mathbf{L_{2}}({{{\phi}}})
.

The reason to bother with
{\bf L}({{{\phi}}})
rather than using the simpler

{\bf L}({{{\phi}}})^{\prime}
is not correctness, but efficiency.
Let
k
be the number of distinct variables of sort
A_{j}
in
{{\phi}}
,

l
the number of
select
literals
x=\textbf{select}_{A_{j}}(a,i)
in
{{\phi}}
,

m
the number of
store
literals

b=\textbf{store}_{A_{j}}(a,i,v)
in
{{\phi}}
,
and
t=n_{(A_{j},{{\phi}})}=|{\mathbb{I}}_{(A_{j},{{\phi}})}|
.
Because
\mathbf{L_{0}^{{{\phi}}}}({a,i})
contains a constant (two) literals,

{\bf L}({{{\phi}}})
contributes
O(l)

literals from the
select
s
and
O(m\,t)
from the
store
s, while never exceeding

O(k\,t)
.
Hence

|{\bf L}({{{\phi}}})|=O\bigl(\min\{\,l+m\,t,\;k\,t\,\}\bigr).

By contrast the naive set
{\bf L}({{{\phi}}})^{\prime}
always contains

k\,t
such blocks, so

|{\bf L}({{{\phi}}})^{\prime}|=O(k\,t)
.

Consequently, whenever
l\ll k\,t\land m\ll k
, the original
definition saves a factor of

O\!\bigl(k\,t/(l+m\,t)\bigr)
literals, whereas in the worst
case the two approaches are equivalent.

For instance, consider the case where
m
is constant and
l,t,k\in O\!\bigl(n\bigr)
for some
n
, then
{\bf L}({{{\phi}}})\in O\!\bigl(n\bigr)
while
{\bf L}({{{\phi}}})^{\prime}\in O\!\bigl(n^{2}\bigr)
.

Example D.135
.

Let there be
t\in\mathbb{N}
, consider the following formula in the signature
\Sigma_{\mathbf{ndt}}
:

\theta=\bigwedge_{j=1}^{t}\bigl(x_{j}=\textbf{select}_{A}(a_{j},i_{j})\bigr).

Where
x_{j}
are variables of sort
E
,
a_{j}
are variables of sort
A
, and
i_{j}
are variables of sort
I
.

Then,
{\bf L}({\theta})=\bigcup_{j=1}^{t}\mathbf{L_{0}^{\theta}}({a_{j},i_{j}})\cup\mathbf{L_{3}}({\theta})
, which contains
4t
literals, while
{\bf L}({\theta})^{\prime}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{t}\bigcup_{k=1}^{t}\mathbf{L_{0}^{\theta}}({a_{j},i_{k}})\cup\mathbf{L_{3}}({\theta})
, which contains
2t^{2}+2t
literals.
